The (3, 4, ∞) modular family of 2-tori, completed at its three special points, is a
                           complex structure on S6 .
The base and the family. Let ∆ = ∆(3, 4, ∞) be the triangle group, acting on the upper half plane h; the
quotient h/∆ is P1 minus a point, and adding the cusp gives P1 with orbifold points of orders 3 and 4 and the
cusp. Let V ∼ = Z4 carry automorphisms T1 , T2 of orders 3 and 4 with T0 := ( T1 T2 )−1 unipotent, ( T0 − I )2 = 0:
this is a representation ∆ → SL(V ), and we let it act on the dual lattice Λ = V ∗ by A j = ( Tj−1 )t (§2). The period
functions τ, µ, β on h are an equivariant period map for it (§3), and the quotient is a family of complex 2-tori
over the punctured orbifold curve: the analogue of a universal family of abelian surfaces over a modular curve,
with this rank-four lattice representation in place of the symplectic one. The very general fibre is not algebraic.
The very general fibre F has NS( F ) = Zη with η a single indefinite class, and the algebraic dimension of the
completed threefold is a( X ) = 1 (§9).
The three special fibres. The three conjugacy classes of maximal finite and parabolic cyclic subgroups of ∆
single out the three special points, and at these three points we take the three standard completions; each is a
choice, and every ℓ0 and every admissible v j below occurs. At an elliptic point of order m the local monodromy
is a torus automorphism of order m, and the filling is Kodaira’s logarithmic transform: (disc × torus)/(Z/m),
the generator acting by a rotation of the disc and by the automorphism followed by a translation; the reduced
fibre is of bielliptic type (§5). At the cusp the monodromy is unipotent of rank two, and the filling N0 is
Mumford’s toric degeneration; its central fibre W is read off the prescribed A2 -triangulation of R2 , the deck
action being the one in which the unimodular map B0 : Λ̄ → Λtor induced by M0 − I = ( T0−1 )t − I on Λ enters.
Modulo the lattice the triangulation has one vertex, three edges and two triangles, i.e. W is one toric surface
with hexagonal moment polygon — the degree-six del Pezzo dP6 — glued to itself along opposite sides, with
two triple points (§4). Thus there is a single family, completed in the three standard ways at the three kinds of
special point of an orbifold curve with a cusp.
The gluing and the fundamental group. Each filling is glued to the global family along a collar by a fibre-
preserving map, and we reglue by translations by local sections (§6, Prop. 6.3); among these, π1 and H∗ depend
only on three integers ℓ0 (at the cusp), ℓ1 , ℓ2 (at the elliptic points) (§7). Let γ ∈ V be the linear coordinate on Λ
whose kernel ker γ = ⟨û, ŵ, δ̂⟩ is the span of all ( A j − 1)Λ; then the coinvariants Λ/ ker γ ∼    = Z are detected by
                                                                                      ∼
γ alone, and ℓ j = γ(v j ) for the translation vectors v j . The result is π1 ( X ) = Z/|12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 | (§7).1 This
formula admits a reading, which the body does not use. It is the formula | a1 a2 e0 + a2 b1 + a1 b2 | of [Orl72] for
the order of H1 of the Seifert fibred space over S2 ( a1 , a2 ), evaluated at e0 = ℓ0 and b j = −ℓ j — the sign coming
from the convention s j ◦ g j = e−2πi/m j s j for the local sections — so here ( a1 , a2 ) = (3, 4) with Seifert invariants
(3, −ℓ1 ), (4, −ℓ2 ) and obstruction ℓ0 ; for (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (0, 1, −1) the value is 1 and the space in question is S3
with the circle action (z, w) 7→ (λ3 z, λ4 w), whose orbit space is S2 (3, 4) [Orl72]. Heuristically the twist condition
says: glue so that the coinvariant circle of the fibres traces out the (3, 4) Seifert fibration of the three-sphere
over the base.
The homology and the Euler number. The remaining torus directions contribute nothing: ŵ and δ̂ are vanishing
cycles at the cusp, collapsed in the hexagonal fibre, and the rest is killed by monodromy, the coinvariants of
Λ having rank one. With the twists above, X has the integral homology of S6 and is diffeomorphic to S6 (§7,
§8). The Euler number localises at the cusp: e( X ) = e(W ) = 2 (§7), every other fibre being a torus or a free
torus quotient and so of Euler number 0 — one singular fibre carries the whole Euler characteristic of S6 , as the
twelve nodal fibres carry the 12 of a rational elliptic surface.
Why the argument of [CDP20] does not apply. That argument requires a line bundle non-torsion on every
fibre and propagates a vanishing statement through each singular fibre by way of its normalisation. Here
H 2 ( X; Z) = 0 and Pic( X ) ∼= C, so every line bundle is topologically trivial; at the non-normal toric fibre the
differential of the fibration itself supplies the section which the passage to the normalisation assumes away,
and R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0 for all L. The count of H1 in [CDP20, Lemma 4.2] (after [CDP98, Lemma 3.2]) assumes
trivial monodromy, whereas the monodromy representation here is non-trivial; §10 shows that this last point is
repairable for X, while the failure of the reduction at the non-normal fibre is decisive.

Theorem. X is a compact connected complex 3-manifold and f : X → B is a surjective holomorphic map with
connected fibres.
(1) Over B◦ the map f is a proper submersion whose fibres are complex 2-tori. The fibre f −1 ( p0 ) is the divisor
    W ⊂ N0 , which is reduced, irreducible and has normal crossings. The normalisation η identifies opposite
    sides of the hexagon of (−1)-curves of dP6 , the double locus is three rational curves through two triple
    points, and e(W ) = 2. For j = 1, 2 one has f ∗ ( p j ) = m j S j , where S j := ( f −1 ( p j ))red is a smooth bielliptic
    surface in Nj , and O X (S j )|Sj has order m j .
(2) The threefold X is simply connected, since π1 ( X ) ∼    = Z/|12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 | = 1, and H∗ ( X; Z) ∼      = H∗ (S6 ; Z);
                                                6
    consequently X is diffeomorphic to S (§7ff.).
(3) The algebraic dimension of X is a( X ) = 1 and M( X ) = f ∗ C(t): every meromorphic function on X is
    constant along the fibres of f .
(4) The canonical bundle is KX ∼     = f ∗ O B (−1) ⊗ O X (2S2 ) and is not torsion; c3 ( X ) = 2; and Aut0 ( X ) ∼= C∗ acts
    along the fibres of f with fixed locus a double curve of W (§9).

    A compact complex threefold fibred by tori over the projective line, and the
                                   six-sphere.

                                                        Abstract
        We construct a compact connected complex manifold X of dimension three together with a surjective
        holomorphic map f : X → P1 whose fibres over the complement of three points p0 , p1 , p2 are complex
        tori of dimension two, whose fibre over p0 is a reduced irreducible rational surface with normal crossings
        (a del Pezzo surface of degree six with the opposite sides of its anticanonical hexagon identified), and
        whose fibres over p1 and p2 are multiple, of multiplicities 3 and 4, with bielliptic reductions S1 , S2 . The
        family over the thrice punctured sphere is given by explicit period functions on the upper half plane
        attached to the (3, 4, ∞) orbifold; the fibre over p0 is filled in by a quotient of an infinite smooth toric
        threefold, and the fibres over p1 , p2 by logarithmic transforms. We prove that X is simply connected
        with the integral homology of S6 , hence diffeomorphic to S6 , and we compute a( X ) = 1 with f the
        algebraic reduction — the Néron–Severi group of the very general fibre being infinite cyclic, generated
        by a class η whose associated hermitian form Hη has signature (1, 1) and which is therefore represented
        by no positive line bundle, so that the very general fibre has algebraic dimension 0 — together with
         f ∗ O X = O , R1 f ∗ O X ∼
                                  = O ⊕ O(−1), R2 f ∗ O X ∼ = O(−1), h0,1 = 1, h0,2 = h0,3 = 0, h1,0 = h2,0 = 0,
        KX = f O(−1) ⊗ O X (2S2 ), c1 c2 = 0, c3 = 2, h ( TX ) = 1 and Aut0 ( X ) ∼
              ∼   ∗                                        0
                                                                                   = C∗ . The existence of such an
        X is incompatible with [CDP20, Cor. 2.3]; the last section locates the point at which the two accounts
        diverge, by showing that R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0 for every holomorphic line bundle L on X, a consequence of
        the non-normality of the normal crossings fibre W.

1      Introduction.
1.1     The result
Throughout, B := P1 carries the affine coordinate t, and p1 , p2 , p0 are the points t = 0, t = 1, t = ∞, with
tc := 1/t the coordinate at p0 and B◦ := B \ Σ, Σ := { p0 , p1 , p2 }; further m1 := 3, m2 := 4. We write dP6 for
                                                      3
the del Pezzo surface of degree 6, the blow up of P2 in three non-collinear points, whose anticanonical
cycle is a hexagon of six (−1)-curves. A bielliptic surface is a smooth compact complex surface ( E × F )/G
with E, F elliptic curves and G a finite group of translations of E acting on F with F/G ∼
                                                                                         = P1 [BHPV, V.5],
[BdF], [Ser]. We write M(Y ) for the field of meromorphic functions of a compact complex space Y, and
a(Y ) = tr. degC M(Y ) for its algebraic dimension.

Theorem. There exist a compact connected complex manifold X of dimension 3 and a surjective holomor-
phic map f : X → B = P1 with connected fibres, with the following properties.

  (1) f is a proper holomorphic submersion over B◦ , and every fibre f −1 ( p) with p ∈ B◦ is a complex torus
      of dimension 2.

  (2) The fibre W := f −1 ( p0 ) is reduced, irreducible, and a normal crossings divisor in X: at every point of
      W the function tc ◦ f is, in suitable local coordinates, z1 , z1 z2 or z1 z2 z3 . The normalisation of W is
      dP6 , and W is obtained from dP6 by identifying the three pairs of opposite sides of its hexagon of
      (−1)-curves. The double locus D ⊂ W consists of three smooth rational curves, each passing through
      both triple points of W and otherwise pairwise disjoint; and e(W ) = 2.

  (3) The fibres over p1 and p2 are multiple: as divisors, f ∗ ( p1 ) = 3 S1 and f ∗ ( p2 ) = 4 S2 , where S j =
      ( f −1 ( p j ))red is a smooth bielliptic surface, and the normal bundle O X (S j )|Sj has order exactly m j in
      Pic(S j ).

  (4) X is simply connected and H∗ ( X; Z) ∼  = H∗ (S6 ; Z); in particular b2 ( X ) = b3 ( X ) = 0 and e( X ) =
      c3 ( X ) = 2 = e(W ). Consequently X is diffeomorphic to the 6-sphere.

  (5) a( X ) = 1, and f is the algebraic reduction of X: M( X ) = f ∗ M( B) = f ∗ C(t), that is, every
      meromorphic function on X is the pull back under f of a rational function on B; moreover the very
      general fibre of f has algebraic dimension 0.

  (6) f ∗ O X = O B , R1 f ∗ O X ∼
                                 = O B ⊕ O B (−1) and R2 f ∗ O X ∼
                                                                 = O B (−1); moreover
                   h0,1 ( X ) = 1,  h0,2 ( X ) = h0,3 ( X ) = 0,      h1,0 ( X ) = h2,0 ( X ) = h3,0 ( X ) = 0.
      In particular χ(O X ) = 0; Pic( X ) = Pic0 ( X ) ∼= H 1 ( X, O X ) ∼
                                                                         = C, since H 1 ( X; Z) = H 2 ( X; Z) = 0; and
      the Frölicher spectral sequence of X does not degenerate at E1 , since b1 ( X ) = 0 < h0,1 ( X ).

  (7) KX ∼ = f ∗ O B (−1) ⊗ O X (2S2 ); in particular KX is not a torsion element of Pic( X ), because O X (4S2 ) =
        ∗                 ⊗4k ∼ ∗
      f O B (1) gives KX        = f O B (−2k), whose space of sections is H 0 ( B, O B (−2k)) = 0 for k ≥ 1 by
      f ∗ O X = O B , while h0 (O X ) = 1 shows that the trivial bundle does have sections. The Chern classes
      satisfy c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = 0 in H ∗ ( X; Z), and the Chern numbers are c1 c2 = 0 and c3 = 2, whence
      χ( X, TX ) = 1 by Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch.

  (8) h0 ( X, TX ) = 1 and Aut0 ( X ) ∼
                                      = C∗ , acting along the fibres of f ; the generating vector field is the
      vertical field ξ determined by the monodromy invariant vector δ̂ of the period lattice, and the fixed
      locus of the action is a single double curve of W, a smooth rational curve (cf. [HKP, Prop. 2.5]).

Corollary 1.1. The six-sphere S6 carries an integrable complex structure, namely the one obtained by
transporting the complex structure of X along a diffeomorphism X → S6 .

Remark 1.2. Since b2 ( X ) = 0, X is not Kähler, is not Moishezon [CDP98, Lemma 1.4], and does not belong
to Fujiki’s class C . The invariants in items (6)–(8) agree with the known constraints on a compact complex
threefold homeomorphic to S6 : h0 ( TX ) = 1 ≤ 2 [HKP, Cor. 1.3]; h0,1 = h0,2 + 1, H 3 ( X, O X ) = 0 and
Pic( X ) = Pic0 ( X ) ∼
                      = H 1 ( X, O X ) [HKP, Cor. 10.2]; KX not torsion, as forced in [HKP, §10]; and the fixed
locus of the C -action of item (8), computed in §9, is of one of the two shapes permitted by [HKP, Prop. 2.5].
               ∗




                                                          4
Remark 1.3 (Hodge numbers not computed here). All the Hodge numbers of X are determined by item
(6) except h1,1 and h1,2 = h2,1 . Since ∑ p,q (−1) p+q h p,q = e( X ) on any compact complex manifold (the Euler
characteristic is unchanged through the Frölicher spectral sequence), equivalently by the Hirzebruch–
                                         p
Riemann–Roch identity ∑ p (−1) p χ(Ω X ) = c3 ( X ) (§9.4), the values χ(O X ) = 0, e( X ) = 2 and the vanishings
of item (6) force h1,1 = h2,2 = h1,2 + 1. Only h1,2 is left undetermined; we expect h1,2 = 1 (whence
h1,1 = h2,2 = 2). A computation would require the direct images Rq f ∗ Ω1X and Rq f ∗ Ω2X , hence a description
                  p
of the sheaves Ω X/B along the three singular fibres, where they have torsion, together with an analysis of
the corresponding Leray spectral sequences in the light of the non-degeneration recorded in item (6).
    The construction is explicit. Over B◦ one has a family J → B◦ of 2-tori whose period matrix is written
down in closed form in terms of three functions τ, µ, β on the upper half plane hz uniformising the
orbifold ( B; 3, 4, ∞): τ is the elliptic modular parameter, j(τ ) = 1728 t, while µ and β solve prescribed
inhomogeneous transformation laws. The local monodromies at p1 and p2 have orders 3 and 4, and the
local monodromy at p0 is unipotent with square-zero logarithm. The three fibres over Σ are filled in by three
local models: at p0 by a quotient of an infinite smooth toric threefold built on the A2 -triangulation of the
plane, in the spirit of Mumford’s construction of degenerating abelian varieties [Mum72, AMRT, KKMS];
at p1 and p2 by logarithmic transforms in the sense of Kodaira [Kod64], with twist vectors v1 = ε and
v2 = −ε′ , explicit vectors of the period lattice fixed by the respective local monodromies. The three twists
are what makes X simply connected: §7 gives π1 ( X ) ∼  = Z/| p| Z with p = 12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 , where (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 )
records the twist data, and the above choice yields (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (0, 1, −1), so p = −1.

1.2   Relation to [CDP20]
The Main Theorem is incompatible with a published result. Campana, Demailly and Peternell proved
in [CDP98] that a compact complex threefold X with b2 ( X ) = 0 and a( X ) > 0 has c3 ( X ) = e( X ) = 0,
and deduced that a compact complex threefold homeomorphic to S6 has algebraic dimension 0. That
proof rests on [CDP98, Lemma 1.5], which is incorrect as stated; the corrigendum [CDP20] says so and
re-proves the results in two cases. [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] treats the case in which there exists a non-constant
meromorphic map g : X 99K P1 which is not holomorphic (this case is written out in detail in [LRS,
Thm. 3.1, Cor. 3.2], following [CDP98]), while [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] treats the case H 1 ( X, Z) = H 2 ( X, Z) = 0,
a( X ) = 1 with holomorphic algebraic reduction f : X → C, concluding (a) H i ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = 0 for i ̸= 1 and
generic M ∈ Pic0 ( X ), (b) χ( X, TX ⊗ M ) ≤ 0 and (c) c3 ( X ) ≤ 0; together these give [CDP20, Cor. 2.3]: if X
is homeomorphic to S6 then a( X ) = 0. The manifold of the Main Theorem has H 1 ( X, Z) = H 2 ( X, Z) = 0,
a( X ) = 1, holomorphic algebraic reduction f : X → P1 , and c3 ( X ) = 2 > 0, so that conclusion (c)
fails; so does conclusion (b), since c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = 0 and Pic( X ) = Pic0 ( X ) give, by Riemann–Roch,
χ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = 21 c3 ( X ) = 1 > 0 for every M ∈ Pic( X ). It therefore contradicts [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] and
[CDP20, Cor. 2.3], as well as [CDP20, Lemma 4.2] (the special case g = 0, b1 = 0 of [CDP98, Lemma
3.2]), [CDP20, Cor. 5.2] and [CDP20, Prop. 5.5] (= [CDP98, Thm. 3.1, Rem. 3.8(1)]), whose conclusions
fail for X (their printed proofs presuppose trivial monodromy); this failure is repairable for the purpose
of the proof of Cor. 2.3 at X (§10), unlike the reduction at the non-normal fibre, which is decisive. It
does not contradict [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] or [LRS], whose hypothesis is the existence of some non-constant
non-holomorphic meromorphic map X 99K P1 , and no such map exists on our X: by item (5) of the Main
Theorem M( X ) = f ∗ C(t), so every non-constant meromorphic map X 99K P1 is of the form h ◦ f with h a
non-constant rational function on B, hence is holomorphic.
    Section 10 is devoted to this conflict and locates the step at which the argument of [CDP20] and the
example part company. The key point is the fibre W = f −1 ( p0 ): it is a normal crossings divisor which
is not normal, the six sides of its hexagon being glued to each other in three opposite pairs (item (2) of
the Main Theorem). We prove (Theorem 10.5) that for every holomorphic line bundle L on X one has
R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0, the obstruction being supported at p0 and arising from the section d f ⊗ e of a sheaf
supported on W which exists precisely because W is non-normal. The vanishing of this second direct
image is the hypothesis under which [CDP20, Prop. 2.4] is invoked in the proof of [CDP20, Thm. 2.2]; for
X it fails, for every L, and so the proof of [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] does not cover X. Section 10 also shows, in the

                                                         5
light of the monodromy group of f computed in §3, that the conclusions of [CDP20, Lemma 4.2], [CDP20,
Cor. 5.2] and [CDP20, Prop. 5.5] fail for X as well. Section 10 discusses the two steps of the printed proofs
that do not apply to X; the two statements cannot both be correct. For this reason the construction is given
with every matrix, chart and identification explicit.

1.3   Plan of the paper
Section 2 fixes the linear algebra: the lattice V ∼        = Z4 with basis (γ, u, w, δ), the matrices T1 , T2 of orders
3 and 4, the unipotent T0 = ( T1 T2 )        − 1  = I + N with N 2 = 0, and the dual picture on Λ = V ∗ with
A j = A( Tj ) and M0 . All facts used later are proved there: Λtor = ker( M0 − I ) = im( M0 − I ), the
unimodularity of B0 : Λ → Λtor , the descriptions Λ A1 = ⟨ε, δ̂⟩, Λ A2 = ⟨ε′ , δ̂⟩, and the presentation
∆ = ⟨ g1 , g2 | g13 = g24 = 1⟩ ∼
                               = Z/3 ∗ Z/4 of the orbifold fundamental group of ( B; 3, 4, ∞), the (3, 4, ∞)
triangle group, together with the representation ρV : ∆ → GL(V ), ρV ( g j ) = Tj . The group ∆ is not
isomorphic to PSL2 (Z) ∼   = Z/2 ∗ Z/3; there is a surjection ∆ → PSL2 (Z), and the modular parameter τ is
equivariant along it.
     Section 3 constructs the period family. Theorem 3.4 establishes existence and uniqueness, up to one
additive constant c0 which is then chosen, of τ, µ, β on hz subject to the equivariance and boundedness
conditions listed there, their behaviour at the two elliptic points and at the cusp, the equivariance
Π( gz) = R g (z)Π(z) Mg of the period matrix Π = [ Z | I ], and the nondegeneracy Im τ · D ̸= 0 which makes
L(z) = Π(z)Λ a lattice in C2 for every z.2 This yields the family T → hz of complex 2-tori, the quotient
J → B◦ , its marking by Λ, and its monodromy.
     Section 4 constructs the filling at p0 . The fan F of cones over the A2 -triangulation of R2 × {1} gives a
smooth toric threefold YF , not of finite type, with a global holomorphic function tc ; the group Λ ∼            = Z2 acts
by the maps Ψλ̄ = (cλ̄ (tc ), 1) · Φλ̄ . Theorem 4.5 shows that for small radius this action is free and properly
discontinuous, that N0 is a complex threefold with f 0 proper, that the fibres over tc ̸= 0 are the tori of J
under the tautological identification E0 , and that W = f 0−1 (0) is as described in item (2). For the quotient to
be a complex manifold proper over the disc, only det B0 ̸= 0 and the holomorphy of C (tc ) at tc = 0 are used;
it is the unimodularity | det B0 | = 1 that makes the central fibre irreducible with normalisation a single dP6 .
No positivity or symmetry of B0 enters. Toric generalities are taken from [Ful93, Oda88, KKMS].
                                                                                                                 log
     Section 5 constructs the fillings at p1 , p2 by logarithmic transforms. Theorem 5.4 shows that g̃ j , which
in flat coordinates reads (z, x ) 7→ ( g j z, A j x + v j /m j ), generates a free Z/m j -action on T |∆ j — freeness holds
because 3 ∤ γ(v1 ) and γ(v2 ) is odd — that the quotient Nj is a complex threefold with f j∗ ( p j ) = m j S j and
                                                                                     log
S j bielliptic, and that translation by the logarithmic section σj conjugates g̃ j to g̃0j , identifying Nj \ f j−1 ( p j )
with J | D∗j .
     Section 6 glues J, N0 , N1 , N2 and proves Theorem 6.2: X is a compact connected complex threefold, f is
holomorphic and surjective, and X is independent, up to biholomorphism over B, of the auxiliary choices
(radii, discs, branches of logarithm).
     Section 7 computes the topology. Using the 0-section of J to split π1 ( J | B◦ ) ∼
                                                                                      = Λ ⋊ π1 ( B◦ ), van Kampen
gives π1 ( X ) ∼= Z/| p|Z with p = 12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 , hence π1 ( X ) = 1 here (Theorem 7.17); Mayer–Vietoris,
with the homology of W and of the S j computed from the cell structures of the appendix, gives H∗ ( X; Z) ∼            =
H∗ (S ; Z) and e( X ) = 2 (Theorem 7.22). The group H∗ ( X; Z) is in fact computed twice in that section:
       6

once along the Mayer–Vietoris route just described, which uses the retraction and collapse of §7.2, and once
along the Leray route of §7.7 together with Appendix B, which, in combination with Theorem 7.17 and
Corollary 4.8, makes no use of §7.2; the recognition of X as S6 therefore does not depend on Proposition
7.2. For comparison, the threefold X ′ obtained with v2 = +ε′ has π1 ( X ′ ) ∼  = Z/7Z.
     Section 8 concludes that X is diffeomorphic to S6 (Theorem 8.1): X is a closed smooth simply connected
6-manifold with the integral homology of S6 , hence a homotopy 6-sphere by Hurewicz and Whitehead
[Hat02], hence homeomorphic to S6 by [Sma61], and diffeomorphic to S6 since Θ6 = 0 [KM63], with
   2 The manifold X depends on the choice of c ; see Remark 6.4.
                                              0




                                                              6
[Mil65].
    Section 9 computes the invariants of Theorem 9.1. The algebraic dimension is determined by a direct
computation on the fibres: from the explicit period matrix one finds that a class E ∈ Λ2 V lies in NS( Fz ) if
and only if an explicit linear form PE (z) in the five functions 1, τ, µ, β, 6µ2 − τβ vanishes, and these five
functions are linearly independent over C; hence for z outside a countable set one gets NS( Fz ) = Zη with
η = u ∧ w + 6 γ ∧ δ, whose hermitian form Hη has signature (1, 1), so that neither η nor −η is the class of
a positive line bundle, while η 2 = 12 · vol ̸= 0 excludes a( Fz ) = 1 as well; the very general fibre therefore
has algebraic dimension 0. Every meromorphic function on X is therefore constant on the very general
fibre, whence M( X ) = f ∗ M( B) = f ∗ C(t) and a( X ) = 1, with f the algebraic reduction. Section 9 also
gives a second proof, by exclusion: a( X ) ≥ 1 because f ∗ embeds M( B) into M( X ); a( X ) ̸= 3 because a
Moishezon threefold has b2 > 0 [CDP98, Lemma 1.4] while b2 ( X ) = 0 by §7; and a( X ) ̸= 2, since otherwise
X carries a meromorphic map to a curve which is not holomorphic (as in the proof of [CDP20, Cor. 2.3]),
whence c3 ( X ) = 0 by [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] (see also [LRS, Thm. 3.1]), contradicting c3 ( X ) = e( X ) = 2. This
route also yields the identification M( X ) = f ∗ M( B), as explained in Remark 9.9: once a( X ) = 1, the
field M( X ) is algebraic over the subfield f ∗ M( B) of transcendence degree 1, and f ∗ M( B) is algebraically
closed in M( X ) because f has connected fibres [Ue75, §3]; hence M( X ) = f ∗ M( B) and f is the algebraic
reduction. The section then computes the direct images Rq f ∗ O X and the numbers h0,q of item (6), the
vanishing of the holomorphic forms h1,0 = h2,0 = h3,0 = 0, the canonical bundle of item (7), the Chern
numbers, and the vertical C∗ -action with h0 ( TX ) = 1 and its fixed locus. Section 10 is the discussion of
§1.2; the appendix collects the matrices, the fan F, and the cell structures used in §7.

1.4   Context
The question whether S6 admits a complex structure goes back to Hopf [Hopf48]; for its history and the
state of the art we refer to the surveys [AGr, Ang18]. Among the even-dimensional spheres only S2 and S6
carry almost complex structures; the structure on S6 induced by octonionic multiplication is not integrable,
its Nijenhuis tensor being nowhere zero, and the space of almost complex structures inducing a given
orientation is connected, so that no homotopy-theoretic invariant can separate a hypothetical integrable
structure from the octonionic one. The one general structural theorem in this direction is that of LeBrun
[LeB87], anticipated by Blanchard: no complex structure on S6 is orthogonal with respect to the round
metric; the proof shows that such a structure would make S6 Kähler, contradicting b2 = 0, and the later
refinements also use only H 2 (S6 ) = 0. The structure constructed here is not produced from a metric and
carries no a priori relation to the round one, so [LeB87] does not bear on it.
    Compact complex threefolds with b2 = 0 are classical: Calabi and Eckmann [CE53] put complex
structures on S3 × S3 and on products of odd-dimensional spheres; these, the Hopf threefolds S1 × S5 ,
the quotients SL(2, C)/Γ and the principal elliptic bundles of [LRS, §4] furnish many examples, with
a = 0, 1, 2 (see [CDP98, §4]). What distinguishes S6 among these is e = 2 ̸= 0. The complex-analytic
constraints on a hypothetical complex structure on S6 have been studied in detail: Huckleberry, Kebekus
and Peternell [HKP] proved that such an X is not almost homogeneous, that h0 ( TX ) ≤ 2, H 3 ( X, O X ) = 0,
h0,1 = h0,2 + 1 and Pic( X ) = Pic0 ( X ) ∼
                                          = H 1 ( X, O X ), and analysed the possible fixed-point sets of a C∗ -
action; Lehn, Rollenske and Schinko [LRS] obtained bounds on spaces of sections, and wrote out in detail
the meromorphic-map case of [CDP98, CDP20], following [CDP98]. Constructions of complex structures
on S6 have been claimed by Etesi [Ete15] by entirely different means, from Samelson structures on G2 and
conjugate orbits; nothing here depends on them or bears on their validity.
    The two mechanisms combined here are classical. Logarithmic transformations were introduced by
Kodaira for elliptic surfaces [Kod64] (see also [BHPV, V.13]), where they are the standard device for
changing the fundamental group of an elliptic fibration without changing the base; the Dolgachev surface
obtained from a rational elliptic surface by logarithmic transformations of coprime multiplicities is simply
connected [Dol81], and the present construction is an analogue one dimension up, with 2-tori in place
of elliptic curves. Filling in a degenerating family of abelian varieties by a toroidal model over the
nilpotent monodromy cone goes back to Mumford [Mum72], and in the arithmetic setting to [AMRT]; the


                                                       7
combinatorial input used here is the A2 -triangulation of the plane. What is specific to the present situation
is that the two mechanisms are used simultaneously, over a base P1 with exactly three critical points, for a
representation ρV of the (3, 4, ∞) triangle group whose local monodromies have orders 3, 4 and ∞, and
that the resulting central fibre W at the unipotent point is non-normal, with e(W ) = 2 accounting for the
whole Euler characteristic of X.


2     Lattice and monodromy data.
2.1   The lattices V and Λ
As on page 1 (Setup), V is free of rank 4 with ordered basis (γ, u, w, δ) and Λ := V ∗ carries the dual basis
(γ̂, û, ŵ, δ̂); we write ⟨λ, x ⟩ := λ( x ), elements are columns, matrices act on columns from the left, and the
columns of a matrix are the images of the basis vectors. Evaluation at γ is the functional
                       γ : Λ → Z,       γ( aγ̂ + bû + cŵ + dδ̂) = a,      ker γ = ⟨û, ŵ, δ̂⟩.
For T ∈ GL(V ) put
                                       A( T ) := ( T −1 )t ∈ GL(Λ)                                      (2.1)
(matrix in the dual basis): the unique element of GL(Λ) with ⟨A( T )λ, Tx ⟩ = ⟨λ, x ⟩ for all λ, x, i.e. the
contragredient ( T t )−1 of T.

Proof. By (2.3), for λ = aγ̂ + bû + cŵ + dδ̂,
                                          ( M0 − I )λ = − N t λ = − aδ̂ + bŵ.
Hence ( M0 − I )λ = 0 if and only if a = b = 0, i.e. λ ∈ Λtor ; and the image is exactly ⟨ŵ, δ̂⟩ = Λtor , the pair
( a, b) ranging over all of Z2 . Thus M0 − I kills Λtor and the induced map B0 sends γ̂¯ 7→ −δ̂, û¯ 7→ ŵ, i.e.

                            ( A1 − I )λ = a(6û − 6ŵ − 2δ̂) + b(−û − ŵ + δ̂) + c(û − 2ŵ),

so ( A1 − I )λ = 0 reads 6a − b + c = −6a − b − 2c = −2a + b = 0, i.e. b = 2a, c = −4a, d free, and

saturated, being kernels of endomorphisms of the torsion-free group Λ, and γ(ε) = γ(ε′ ) = 1 by definition
of γ.

Lemma 2.7. Let G act on V tautologically and on Λ through A. Then:

  (i) V T1 = ⟨γ, 2u + w + 3δ⟩, V T2 = ⟨γ, u + w + 2δ⟩, V G = Zγ;

 (ii) ΛG = Λ A1 ∩ Λ A2 = Zδ̂;




                                                          9
 (iii) every g ∈ G preserves ⟨γ, u, w⟩ and acts trivially on V/⟨γ, u, w⟩, and every A( g) satisfies γ ◦ A( g) = γ;
       with ΣV := ∑ g∈G im( g − I ) and ΣΛ := ∑ g∈G im(A( g) − I ),
                                           ΣV = ⟨γ, u, w⟩,       ΣΛ = ker γ,
      so the coinvariants VG ∼   = Z (generated by the class of δ) and ΛG ∼         = Z (via γ) are free of rank 1;
      equivalently [( T1 − I ) | ( T2 − I )] and [( A1 − I ) | ( A2 − I )] have Smith normal form diag(1, 1, 1, 0);

 (iv) the smallest ⟨ A1 , A2 ⟩-invariant subgroup of Λ containing Λtor is ker γ.

Proof. (i) For x = aγ + bu + cw + dδ, (2.2) gives
                          T1 x = ( a − 6c + 2d)γ + (−b + c + d)u + (−b + d)w + dδ,
                          T2 x = ( a + 6b − 3d)γ + (−c + d)u + bw + dδ,
so T1 x = x iff d = 3c, b = 2c, and T2 x = x iff d = 2b, c = b. Comparing aγ + c(2u + w + 3δ) =
a′ γ + b(u + w + 2δ) forces b = c = 0, so V G = Zγ.
     (ii) By Lemma 2.6 an element of the intersection is aε + dδ̂ = a′ ε′ + d′ δ̂; the γ̂- and û-coefficients give
a = a′ and 2a = 3a′ , so a = 0.
     (iii) By (2.2) each generator Tj preserves U := ⟨γ, u, w⟩ and acts trivially on V/U; these two properties
are stable under composition and inversion, hence hold for every g ∈ G, so im( g − I ) ⊆ U and ΣV ⊆ U.
Likewise γ ◦ A j = γ (Lemma 2.4) gives γ ◦ A( g) = γ for every g ∈ G, whence im(A( g) − I ) ⊆ ker γ and
ΣΛ ⊆ ker γ. For the reverse inclusions it suffices to use the generators: ΣV contains the images of the basis
vectors under Tj − I, namely
                                 − 2u − w,         −6γ + u − w,     2γ + u + w,
                                6γ − u + w,          −u − w,     −3γ + u,
and (−2u − w) − (−u − w) = −u, then w, then 2γ and 3γ, hence γ, lie in ΣV ; while ( A1 − I )ŵ + ( A2 − I )û =
−ŵ, then û, then δ̂ = ( A1 − I )û + û + ŵ lie in ΣΛ . Both ΣV and ΣΛ are spanned by parts of the bases,
hence are direct summands with free rank-1 quotients.
    (iv) Such a subgroup contains ŵ and A1 ŵ = û − ŵ, hence û and δ̂, i.e. ker γ; and ker γ is invariant since
γ ◦ A j = γ.

2.4   The invariant alternating form
Lemma 2.8. The group of G-invariant alternating forms on V is infinite cyclic, generated by Q0 with
              Q0 (γ, δ) = 1,   Q0 (u, w) = 6,   Q0 (γ, u) = Q0 (γ, w) = Q0 (u, δ) = Q0 (w, δ) = 0;
thus Q0 is, up to sign, the unique primitive invariant alternating form. Moreover b( x, y) := Q0 ( x, Ny) is
symmetric and descends to V/ ker N ∼  = Z2 ; in the basis given by the classes of w, δ its Gram matrix is
                                                  diag(6, −1),
so b is nondegenerate of signature (1, 1) and discriminant −6.

Proof. Write q xy := Q( x, y) for an invariant alternating Q. By (2.2), T1 -invariance on the pairs (γ, u) and
(γ, w) reads
                                       −qγu − qγw = qγu ,     qγu = qγw ,
i.e. 2qγu + qγw = 0 and qγu = qγw , whence 3qγu = 0 and
                                                 qγu = qγw = 0.
Using these first two equations to drop all terms in qγu , qγw from the remaining expansions, T1 -invariance
on the pairs (u, δ) and (w, δ) reads
                            −quδ − qwδ = quδ ,     −6qγδ + quw + quδ = qwδ ,
and, in the same way (again using the first two equations), T2 -invariance on the same four pairs reads
                   qγw = qγu , −qγu = qγw , −quδ = qwδ , 6qγδ − quw + qwδ = quδ ;



                                                        10
the remaining pairs give no condition. Hence qγu = qγw = quδ = qwδ = 0 and quw = 6qγδ , i.e. Q ∈ ZQ0 ,
and Q0 is invariant. For b: ker N = im N = ⟨γ, u⟩ and Q0 vanishes on ⟨γ, u⟩ × ⟨γ, u⟩, so b( x, y) = 0
whenever x or y lies in ker N, and b descends; finally
                   b(w, w) = Q0 (w, −u) = 6,                                    b(δ, δ) = Q0 (δ, γ) = −1,
                    b(w, δ) = Q0 (w, γ) = 0 = Q0 (δ, −u) = b(δ, w).


Remark 2.9. In §4 it is the unimodularity of B0 that is used: the lattice B0 Λ of cocharacter translations is all
of Λtor , and this is what makes the toric filling have an irreducible central fibre W containing a single copy
of dP6 ; for | det B0 | = k > 1 the same construction would produce k such pieces. The form Q0 is used again
in Remark 3.23, in §7 through the invariant bivector η = u ∧ w + 6 γ ∧ δ dual to Q0 , and in Appendix A. It
is recorded here already in order to place the present data in context: for a one-parameter degeneration of
polarized abelian varieties with unipotent monodromy exp N and polarization Q the form x 7→ Q( x, Nx )
on V/ ker N is definite ([Cle77], [Mum72]), whereas by Lemma 2.8 the pair ( Q0 , N ) has signature (1, 1).

2.5   The base orbifold, the group ∆, and the representation ρV
With the conventions of page 1 (Setup): B = P1 with coordinate t, p1 : t = 0, p2 : t = 1, p0 : t = ∞ with
tc = 1/t, B◦ = B ∖ { p0 , p1 , p2 }, m1 = 3, m2 = 4, ζ j = e−2πi/m j . Let O be the orbifold with underlying space
B, cone points of orders 3 at p1 and 4 at p2 , and a puncture at p0 , i.e. of signature (0; 3, 4, ∞).

Lemma 2.10. Let g ∈ PSL2 (R) be elliptic of order m < ∞ with fixed point z0 ∈ h, and c(z) := (z − z0 )/(z −
z̄0 ). Then c maps h biholomorphically onto the unit disc with c(z0 ) = 0, and c ◦ g ◦ c−1 is the rotation
s 7→ ζs with ζ = g′ (z0 ) a primitive m-th root of unity; in particular c ◦ g = ζ · c.

Proof. c is a Möbius map carrying R ∪ {∞} to the unit circle and z0 to 0; the disc automorphism c ◦ g ◦ c−1
fixes 0, hence is s 7→ ζs with ζ its derivative at 0, equal to g′ (z0 ), of order m.

Proposition 2.11. There exist a copy hz of the upper half-plane, a Fuchsian group ∆ ⊂ PSL2 (R) and a
holomorphic surjection π : hz → B ∖ { p0 } such that:

  (i) ∆ acts properly discontinuously, π induces hz /∆ ∼      = B ∖ { p0 }, π is a local biholomorphism off
      π { p1 , p2 } and has local degree m j at each point of π −1 ( p j ); the stabiliser of such a point is cyclic of
        − 1

      order m j and all other stabilisers are trivial;

 (ii) there are z j ∈ π −1 ( p j ) and g1 , g2 ∈ ∆ with g j (z j ) = z j , g j of order m j and g′j (z j ) = ζ j , such that
                                           ∆ = ⟨ g1 , g2 | g13 = g24 = 1⟩ ∼
                                                                          = Z/3 ∗ Z/4,
      and such that g0 := ( g1 g2 )−1 is parabolic; by construction g1 g2 g0 = 1;

 (iii) if ∗ ∈ ∂hz is the fixed point of g0 , its stabiliser in ∆ is exactly ⟨ g0 ⟩, and for all small r > 0 the
       component Ur of π −1 ({0 < |tc | < r }) whose closure contains ∗ is a ⟨ g0 ⟩-invariant, connected and
       simply connected cusp neighbourhood of ∗ squeezed between two horodiscs at ∗, i.e. H ′′ ⊆ Ur ⊆ H ′
       for suitable horodiscs H ′′ , H ′ at ∗; shrinking r so that Ur lies in a strictly smaller horodisc at ∗, the set
       Ur is closed in π −1 ({0 < |tc | < r }) and hence is a connected component of it, every other component
       lying in a translate gH ′ with g ∈ ∆ ∖ ⟨ g0 ⟩; it satisfies gUr ∩ Ur = ∅ for g ∈ ∆ ∖ ⟨ g0 ⟩, and π induces a
       biholomorphism Ur /⟨ g0 ⟩ ∼   = {0 < | t c | < r }.
Proof. Since 13 + 14 < 1, O is hyperbolic, hence uniformised by a Fuchsian group of signature (0; 3, 4, ∞)
([Thu97], Ch. 13; [Bea83], Ch. 10); explicitly, ∆ is the orientation-preserving subgroup of the group generated
by the reflections σa , σb , σc in the sides a = BC, b = CA, c = AB of the hyperbolic triangle T with vertices
A, B, C counterclockwise and angles π/3, π/4, 0 (Poincaré’s theorem; [Bea83], Ch. 7, 9–10). The quotient
surface is a once-punctured sphere, i.e. C; composing with the affine automorphism carrying the order-3


                                                               11
cone point to 0 = p1 and the order-4 one to 1 = p2 yields π, and (i) is the standard description of a
Fuchsian group by its signature ([Bea83], Ch. 10).
     (ii) Put x := σb σc , y := σc σa , z := σa σb , so xyz = 1 and ∆ = ⟨ x, y, z | x3 = y4 = 1, xyz = 1⟩ ([Bea83],
Ch. 10). The relevant rule is: if two geodesics α, β meet at P and θ denotes the angle from α to β measured
counterclockwise, then the product σβ σα (reflect in α first, then in β) is the counterclockwise rotation about
P through 2θ ([Bea83], Ch. 7). Since the vertices A, B, C are counterclockwise, the counterclockwise angle
at A from c = AB to b = CA is the interior angle π/3, and that at B from a = BC to c = AB is π/4; hence
x = σb σc is the counterclockwise rotation about A through 2π/3, y = σc σa the counterclockwise rotation
about B through 2π/4, and z is parabolic, fixing the ideal vertex C; by Lemma 2.10, x ′ ( A) = e2πi/3 and
y′ ( B) = e2πi/4 . With
                                        z1 := A, z2 := B, g1 := x −1 , g2 := y−1
the two rotations are reversed simultaneously, so that g1′ (z1 ) = ζ 1 and g2′ (z2 ) = ζ 2 , of orders 3, 4. Inverting
xyz = 1 gives g2 g1 = z, so g1 g2 = g1 zg1−1 is parabolic, hence so is g0 ; eliminating z gives the stated
presentation.
     (iii) The stabiliser of ∗ is ⟨ g0 ⟩. By the standard description of a cusp ([Bea83], Ch. 10) the stabiliser ∆∗
of the fixed point of a parabolic element of a Fuchsian group is infinite cyclic, generated by a primitive
parabolic h, and there is a horodisc H ′ at ∗ with gH ′ ∩ H ′ = ∅ for g ∈         / ∆∗ . Write g0 = hn with n ≥ 1;
                                                      −1
we claim n = 1, i.e. that g0 , equivalently g0 = g1 g2 , is not a proper power in ∆. Indeed, in the free
product ∆ = ⟨ g1 ⟩ ∗ ⟨ g2 ⟩ the element g1 g2 is cyclically reduced of length 2, and cyclically reduced length is
a conjugacy invariant. An element h ∈ ∆ of finite order is conjugate into a free factor, so all its powers have
finite order and cannot be parabolic; hence h has infinite order and is conjugate to a cyclically reduced
word h0 of even length ℓ ≥ 2, so that hn is conjugate to the cyclically reduced word h0n of length nℓ. If
n ≥ 2 this length is ≥ 4 > 2, a contradiction. Thus n = 1 and ∆∗ = ⟨ g0 ⟩, and H ′ /⟨ g0 ⟩ embeds in hz /∆ as a
punctured-disc neighbourhood of the puncture.
     The cusp coordinate. Conjugate in PSL2 (R) so that ∗ = ∞ and H ′ = {Im ζ > C }; then g0 (ζ ) = ζ + ε 0
with ε 0 ∈ R× , and after the further conjugation ζ 7→ ζ/|ε 0 | we may assume ε 0 ∈ {+1, −1}. The sign ε 0
is intrinsic, the elements of PSL2 (R) fixing ∞ being the maps ζ 7→ aζ + c with a > 0, whose conjugation
action preserves it. Its value plays no role in the present proof, since q := e2πiζ is ⟨ g0 ⟩-invariant for either
sign and identifies H ′ /⟨ g0 ⟩ with a punctured disc D ∗ ; the value is determined once the period map τ
is taken into account, and Remark 2.12 gives ε 0 = −1. Thus π induces an injective holomorphic map
ψ : D → B with ψ(0) = p0 , where we read B near p0 in the coordinate tc and set ψ(0) = 0. Choose
r0 > 0 with {|tc | < r0 } ⊆ ψ( D ), and let 0 < r < r0 . Then Vr := ψ−1 ({|tc | < r }) is a simply connected
neighbourhood of 0 in D, biholomorphic to a disc, and
                                        Ur = {ζ ∈ H ′ : e2πiζ ∈ Vr ∖ {0}}
is the component of π −1 ({0 < |tc | < r }) accumulating at ∗: it is the preimage under ζ 7→ e2πiζ of a
punctured simply connected domain around 0, hence connected, simply connected and ⟨ g0 ⟩-invariant, and
π induces Ur /⟨ g0 ⟩ ∼= {0 < |tc | < r }; disjointness of its ∆-translates off ⟨ g0 ⟩ follows from that of H ′ ⊇ Ur .
It is moreover the only component accumulating at ∗: since π is the quotient by ∆ and Ur is a component
of π −1 ({0 < |tc | < r }) with trivial setwise stabiliser outside ⟨ g0 ⟩, every component of π −1 ({0 < |tc | < r })
is a translate gUr with g ∈ ∆, and every component other than Ur is such a translate with g ∈        / ⟨ g0 ⟩, hence
lies in gH ′ ; since Ur ⊆ H ′ is contained in a horodisc at ∗, the closure of gUr meets ∂hz only at g∗, and
g∗ ̸= ∗ because the stabiliser of ∗ is exactly ⟨ g0 ⟩. Hence no other component accumulates at ∗. Finally,
choosing 0 < ϱ < ϱ′ with {|q| < ϱ} ⊆ Vr ⊆ {|q| < ϱ′ } gives horodiscs H ′′ = {Im ζ > log(1/ϱ)/2π } ⊆ Ur
and Ur ⊆ {Im ζ > log(1/ϱ′ )/2π } ∩ H ′ , as claimed.

Remark 2.12 (The translation sign at the cusp). The normalisation g0 (ζ ) = ζ + ε 0 , ε 0 ∈ {±1}, of the proof
of Proposition 2.11(iii) concerns the uniformising coordinate ζ on hz , whereas the relation τ ◦ g0 = τ − 1 of
§3 concerns the period map τ : hz → h and expresses its equivariance along ρΛ , i.e. ρΛ ( g0 ) = M0 . The two
maps are distinct, but the second relation determines the first sign: we show that ε 0 = −1.
     Indeed, τ is equivariant along the surjection ∆ → PSL2 (Z) of Remark 2.13, so j ◦ τ = 1728 t ◦ π on
hz ; equivalently, on Ur the function e2πiτ is, through the identifications of Proposition 2.11(iii), the local

                                                         12
parameter of the cusp of X (1) pulled back along the map ψ, which has a simple zero at 0. Writing q := e2πiζ ,
this means that there are a holomorphic unit u1 near 0 (the ratio of the two local cusp parameters, coming
from the local biholomorphism j-chart versus tc -chart at the cusp) and the expansion ψ(υ) = υ w(υ) with
w(0) ̸= 0, such that
               e2πiτ = u1 ψ(q) ψ(q),              e2πi(τ −ζ ) = V (q), V (υ) := u1 ψ(υ) w(υ),
                                                                                      
                                           so
where V is holomorphic and nowhere zero on a neighbourhood of 0 in Vr ; in particular e2πi(τ −ζ ) extends
across q = 0 as a non-vanishing holomorphic function, of winding number 0 around small loops |q| = const.
    Now shrink r so that V is holomorphic and non-vanishing on the simply connected domain Vr , and fix
there a branch of
                                        F0 := 2πi1
                                                   log V,      e2πiF0 = V;
this is possible because Vr is simply connected and V is non-vanishing, equivalently because the winding
number is 0. Put F := F0 (q), a holomorphic function on Ur . Since q = e2πiζ is ⟨ g0 ⟩-invariant, F is g0 -
invariant. Moreover e2πi(τ −ζ ) = V (q) = e2πiF , so (τ − ζ ) − F is a continuous integer-valued function on the
connected set Ur , hence a constant k ∈ Z. Therefore τ − ζ = F + k is single-valued and g0 -invariant:
                                             ( τ − ζ ) ◦ g0 = ( τ − ζ ) .
It is the single-valuedness of the chosen logarithm on the simply connected Vr that yields this; boundedness
of Im(τ − ζ ) alone would still allow a non-zero integer period. Consequently
                                ζ ◦ g0 − ζ = τ ◦ g0 − τ = −1,         i.e. ε 0 = −1.
The value of ε 0 is thus determined by the data rather than chosen. It is in any case not needed elsewhere:
§3 uses only the ⟨ g0 ⟩-invariance of q = e2πiζ together with the quoted equivariance, and an asymptotic
comparison of τ with ζ at the cusp, should one be needed, may be made with ε 0 = −1 and τ − ζ bounded.

3    The period family over the thrice-punctured sphere.
Throughout this section we use the data and conventions of §2: ∆ = ⟨ g1 , g2 | g13 = g24 = 1⟩ is the

on this one component Ur only; see Remark 3.2. The goal of the section is the following statement, proved
in §§3.1–3.6.

Lemma 3.5. For R > 1728 put Ω R := {τ ∈ h : | j(τ )| > R} and

components of Ω R are the pairwise disjoint translates γVR , γ ∈ PSL2 (Z), each squeezed between two
horoballs at the cusp γ∞, and every γ′ ∈ PSL2 (Z) with γ′ γVR = γVR fixes the cusp γ∞.

Proof. First, c1 ( R) → ∞: given A > 0, the set F ∩ {Im τ ≤ A} is compact, so | j| is bounded on it by some
R( A); for R ≥ R( A) no point of F with Im τ ≤ A satisfies | j| > R, i.e. c1 ( R) ≥ A. In particular R0 exists.
    Now let R ≥ R0 . Ω R = j−1 ({|w| > R}) is invariant, and j induces a biholomorphism Ω R /PSL2 (Z) ∼        =
{|w| > R}, a connected set; hence the components of Ω R form one orbit. Since j = q−1 (1 + O(q)) as
Im τ → ∞, we have | j| > R on {Im τ > c2 ( R)} for suitable c2 ( R) ≥ c1 ( R), so VR is well defined.
    For the other inclusion: by the definition of c1 ( R), {| j| > R} ∩ F ⊂ {Im τ ≥ c1 ( R)}; as every point of h

Proposition 3.6. Part (i) of Theorem 3.4 holds.

Proof. Existence of a lift. Put F := 1728 (t ◦ π ) : hz → C. Since π is unramified outside π −1 { p1 , p2 } and
ramified there of order exactly 3, resp. 4, the map F (resp. F − 1728) vanishes to order exactly 3 (resp. 4) on
π −1 ( p1 ) (resp. π −1 ( p2 )), and F has no other critical points. Let Z ◦ := hz ∖ F −1 {0, 1728}; its fundamental
group is normally generated by small meridians (of either orientation, since only the orders below are
used), whose F-images are cubes (resp. fourth powers) of meridians of 0 (resp. 1728), whose classes in
the deck group PSL2 (Z) have order 3 (resp. 2) and hence are trivial. Therefore            the image of F∗ π1 ( Z ◦ ) in
the deck group is trivial, i.e. F∗ π1 ( Z ) ⊂ j∗ π1 h ∖ ( PSL2 (Z)ρ ∪ PSL2 (Z)i ) , which is exactly the lifting
                                            ◦
                                                                                      

criterion for the covering j. So F | Z◦ lifts to a holomorphic τ : Z ◦ → h with j ◦ τ = F.
     We extend τ across each point z0 ∈ F −1 {0, 1728} by the maximum principle in the bounded model.
Let δ : h → D be a biholomorphism onto the unit disc and let D ∗ be a small punctured disc around z0
contained in Z ◦ (possible, as F −1 {0, 1728} is discrete). Then δ ◦ τ is a bounded holomorphic function on
D ∗ , so it extends holomorphically across z0 , with |(δ ◦ τ )(z0 )| ≤ 1. It is non-constant, because j ◦ τ = F
is non-constant near z0 ; hence the maximum principle excludes |(δ ◦ τ )(z0 )| = 1, and the extension takes
values in D. Thus τ extends holomorphically across z0 with value in h, and j ◦ τ = F persists by continuity:
this is (τ1).
     Equivariance. F ◦ g = F, so τ ◦ g and τ are lifts of the same map through the Galois covering j:
τ ◦ g = φ( g) ◦ τ with φ : ∆ → PSL2 (Z) a homomorphism.
     Local orders. Near z1 , t ◦ π = s31 · (unit) while j has a zero of order 3 at ρ, so τ − τ (z1 ) has a simple zero;
near z2 , (t − 1) ◦ π = s42 · (unit) and j − 1728 has a zero of order 2, so τ − τ (z2 ) has a zero of order exactly
2.
     Normalisation. Replacing τ by γ ◦ τ replaces φ by γφγ−1 ; since j(τ (z1 )) = 0 we may arrange τ (z1 ) = ρ.
Then φ( g1 ) fixes ρ and, τ being a local biholomorphism at z1 , φ( g1 )′ (ρ) = g1′ (z1 ) = e−2πi/3 ; as the stabiliser
of ρ is ⟨S1 ⟩ and S1′ (ρ) = ρ−2 = e−2πi/3 , φ( g1 ) = S1 .

     Rigidity: P is parabolic. First P ̸= 1. Otherwise τ ◦ g0 = τ, so τ is g0 -invariant on Ur and descends to
a holomorphic function of tc on {0 < |tc | < r }; so does q = e2πiτ , which moreover satisfies |q| < 1 (as
Im τ > 0) and hence extends holomorphically across tc = 0 with |q(0)| ≤ 1. If |q(0)| = 1, then |q| would
attain its maximum at the interior point tc = 0, so q would be a constant of modulus 1, contradicting
|q| < 1; hence |q(0)| < 1. Next, j(τ ) = J (q) with J holomorphic on {0 < |q| < 1} and with a simple pole at

Hence q(0) = 0; put n := ord0 q ≥ 1, so that the winding number of q along a small circle |tc | = ε is n ≥ 1.
On the other hand 2πiτ is, by g0 -invariance, a single-valued continuous (indeed holomorphic) logarithm of
q on {0 < |tc | < r }, so that winding number is 0 — a contradiction. Thus P ̸= 1.
     Next, shrink r so that R := 1728/r ≥ R0 , with R0 as in Lemma 3.5. Then | j(τ )| = 1728/|tc | > R on Ur ,

P(γVR ) = γVR by disjointness of the components. By Lemma 3.5, P fixes the cusp γ∞; the stabiliser of a
cusp in PSL2 (Z) is infinite cyclic generated by a parabolic element, so P is parabolic.

Corollary 3.7. Let h := 2πi log u1 (a branch on {|tc | < r }) and s := τ − h(tc ◦ π ) on Ur . Then e2πis = tc ◦ π,
s ◦ g0 = s − 1, and s maps Ur biholomorphically onto a half plane {Im s > const}. In particular Im τ → ∞
as tc → 0 on Ur .

Proof. e2πis = q/u1 = tc ◦ π, and h(tc ◦ π ) is g0 -invariant, so s ◦ g0 = s − 1. Both tc ◦ π : Ur → {0 < |tc | < r }
and e2πi(·) are universal coverings of the punctured disc with deck group Z, so s is an isomorphism of
coverings; τ = s + h with h bounded gives the last assertion.

definition makes sense. Finally L acts on M by addition: the difference ν = µ − µ′ of two sections of M(U )
satisfies ν ◦ g1 = −ν/τ, ν ◦ g2 = ν/τ.

Remark 3.12. We record a geometric interpretation, which will not be needed later. Condition (τ1)
says that τ is the period map of the rational elliptic surface with j = 1728t and Weierstrass model
y2 = 4x3 − 3t3 (t − 1) x − t4 (t − 1)2 , with fibres of types IV ∗ , I I I, I1 over p1 , p2 , p0 [Kod64], [BHPV, V.10]; its
Mordell–Weil group is generated by P = (0, i t2 (t − 1)), and −µ is the Abel–Jacobi coordinate of P, the
values µ(z1 ), µ(z2 ) being the 3- resp. 2-torsion values dictated by the non-identity components of the IV ∗
and I I I fibres.

and Π( gz) = R g (z)Π(z) Mg , R gh (z) = R g (hz) Rh (z) for all g, h ∈ ∆.

Proof. Direct computation from the matrices of §2 (T1 γ = γ, T1 u = −u − w, T1 w = −6γ + u, T1 δ =
2γ + u + w + δ; T2 u = 6γ + w, T2 w = −u, T2 δ = −3γ + u + δ; T0 = I + N) gives

                                     T1 σ2 = ( β + 2)γ + (1 − µ)u + (1 − µ)w + δ,

                                     T0 σ1 = 6µγ + (τ − 1)u + w,
                                     T0 σ2 = ( β + 1)γ + µu + δ.
Dividing T1 σ1 by −τ normalises the w-coefficient to 1 and leaves u-coefficient (τ − 1)/τ = τ ◦ g1 and
γ-coefficient 6(1 − µ)/τ = 6(µ ◦ g1 ) by (τ2), (µ1); so the result is σ1 ( g1 z). Subtracting (1 − µ) times it

from T1 σ2 kills the w-coefficient and leaves u-coefficient (1 − µ)(1 − τ −        1
                                                                                 τ ) = τ = µ ◦ g1 and γ-coefficient
β + 2 − 6(1 − µ)2 /τ = β ◦ g1 by ( β1), i.e. σ2 ( g1 z). The same normalisation applied to the T2 -row gives
τ ◦ g2 = −1/τ, µ ◦ g2 = 1 + µ/τ, β ◦ g2 = β − 3 − 6µ2 /τ, and the T0 -row is already (σ1 ( g0 z), σ2 ( g0 z)), so
R g0 = I.
     If σi ( gz) = ∑k ( R g )ik ρV ( g)σk (z) then, for λ ∈ Λ, ⟨σi ( gz), λ⟩ = ∑k ( R g )ik ⟨σk (z), ρV ( g)t λ⟩, i.e. Π( gz) =
R g (z)Π(z) Mg ; equivalently R g (z)Π(z) = Π( gz) Mg−1 . Since the right (ŵ, δ̂)-block of Π is I, the right
block of Π( gz) Mg−1 = R g Π(z) is R g , the matrix so called in Definition 3.3; it is invertible because
R g Π(z) = Π( gz) Mg−1 has rank 2. Finally Mgh = ρV ( gh)t = Mh Mg , so
                           Π( ghz) = R g (hz) Rh (z)Π(z) Mh Mg = R g (hz) Rh (z)Π(z) Mgh ,
and the cocycle relation follows from the uniqueness of R gh .




                                                              21
3.6   The family J → B◦ and its marking
Convention 3.17. Paths are composed left to right: if c(1) = c′ (0) then cc′ means “first c, then c′ ”. With
these conventions the map of §2 sending the homotopy class of π ◦ c, for a path c from the base point z to
gz in h◦z , to g ∈ ∆ is a group homomorphism π1 ( B◦ , π (z)) → ∆.
                                                                                  m
     Orientations of meridians. Since s j ◦ g j = e−2πi/m j s j and t j ◦ π = s j j , the deck transformation g j rotates
the linearising coordinate clockwise, so it is the clockwise meridian at p j (and likewise the clockwise loop at
the cusp p0 ) that lifts to g j (resp. g0 ); the counterclockwise meridians lift to g−          1         −1
                                                                                               j , resp. g0 . We therefore
declare: all meridians used for the marking are oriented clockwise, i.e. negatively for the complex orientation
of B, and then the product of the meridians around p1 , p2 , p0 , taken in this order and suitably based, is
trivial, matching g1 g2 g0 = 1 in ∆. Equivalently, if one insists on counterclockwise meridians σ̌1 , σ̌2 , σ̌0 , these
lift to g1−1 , g2−1 , g0−1 and the relation g1 g2 g0 = 1 reads σ̌0 σ̌2 σ̌1 = 1 in π1 ( B◦ ), i.e. the product of the three
counterclockwise meridians taken in the opposite order.
Remark 3.18. Reversing the orientations of all three meridians simultaneously replaces the marking by its
inverse and hence replaces p by − p in §7. All statements there involve only | p|, so they hold equally for
either orientation, and in particular for the choice made in Convention 3.17.
Proposition 3.19. Part (v) of Theorem 3.4 holds.
                                                                             gh, so g 7→ g̃ is an action of ∆ on hz × C2
Proof. By Proposition 3.16, g̃(z, ζ ) = ( gz, R g (z)ζ ) satisfies g̃ ◦ h̃ = f
by biholomorphisms over the ∆-action on hz . It normalises the Λ-action, since R g (z)Π(z) = Π( gz) Mg−1
gives
                                 g̃ z, ζ + Π(z)λ = gz, R g (z)ζ + Π( gz) Mg−1 λ ,
                                                                                       

so it descends to T and maps L(z)-cosets to L( gz)-cosets. By §2, ∆ acts on hz properly discontinuously
with stabilisers trivial outside ∆z1 ∪ ∆z2 and cyclic of orders 3, 4 at z1 , z2 ; hence it acts freely and properly
discontinuously on h◦z , and therefore on T |h◦z . So J is a complex 3-manifold; the induced map to B◦ is
holomorphic, proper with fibres the compact tori C2 /L(z), and a submersion, and the zero section descends
because R g (z)0 = 0.
    Marking and monodromy. We use Convention 3.17. For z ∈ h◦z identify H1 of the fibre C2 /L(z) with Λ by
λ 7→ [ 0 → Π(z)λ ]. Let c be a path from z to gz in h◦z , so that π ◦ c is a loop in B◦ ; the fibre of J over its
base point is identified with the fibre of T at z, and with the fibre at gz through g̃. Parallel transport in T
along c is tautological, i.e. the identity in the Λ-coordinates, and by
                                             R g (z)Π(z)λ = Π( gz) Mg−1 λ
the map g̃ carries the cycle with coordinate λ at z to the cycle with coordinate Mg−1 λ at gz. Hence the
monodromy on H1 , read in the marking at the base point, is λ 7→ Mg−1 λ.
    We now fix the meaning of “monodromy” used here and in §7. The two identifications of the fibre of J
over the base point used above — with Tz at the start of the loop and with T gz at its end — differ by g̃,
and g̃∗ = Mg−1 in the Λ-coordinates; consequently forward parallel transport of H1 around the loop lifting
to g, read in the marking at the base point, is g̃∗−1 , i.e. λ 7→ Mg λ, while the monodromy representation is by
definition the inverse of forward transport,
                                                      g 7−→ Mg−1 .
This is the convention (the inverse-transport, or “representation”, convention of §7) in which the assignment
                             −1
is a homomorphism: Mgh           = Mg−1 Mh−1 since Mgh = Mh Mg , whereas forward transport g 7→ Mg is anti-
multiplicative for the left-to-right composition of Convention 3.17. The two conventions differ by inverting
all the matrices, which changes neither the group ⟨ A1 , A2 ⟩ nor the invariant | p| of §7 (cf. Remark 3.18), so
the statements of §7 hold in either one.
     For the clockwise meridians of p1 , p2 , p0 — i.e. the meridians of Convention 3.17, which lift to g1 , g2 , g0
— the matrices of the monodromy representation are A1 , A2 , M0 , and A1 A2 M0 = I matches g1 g2 g0 = 1.
                                                       ( j)
     Finally, for j = 1, 2 the fibre of T over z j is A0 = C2 /L(z j ), a torus by Proposition 3.15, and g̃ j restricts
to the automorphism ζ 7→ R gj (z j )ζ of order m j .

Remark 3.20. In the flat real trivialisation T ∼               = hz × (Λ ⊗ R)/Λ, (z, x ) 7→ (z, Π(z) x ), the map g̃ reads
(z, x ) 7→ ( gz, Mg−1 x ), i.e. (z, x ) 7→ ( g j z, A j x ) for g = g j . This is the form used in §5.

Remark 3.22. The regularity of C at tc = 0 comes from the two torsor problems of §§3.2,3.3: their sheaves
L and O B are the sheaves of local solutions with the prescribed regularity at the distinguished cusp over
p0 , and they still have local sections there (µ = 0, resp. β = −τ). It is thus obtained independently of the
nilpotent orbit theorem, which does not apply in the present setting (Remark 3.23).

3.8   The Hodge form: signature (1, 1)
Remark 3.23. Let Q0 be the ⟨ T1 , T2 ⟩-invariant alternating form of §2. Then Q0 (σ1 , σ2 ) = 6µ Q0 (γ, δ) +
µ Q0 (w, u) = 6µ − 6µ = 0, so F1 (z) is Q0 -Lagrangian, and the Hermitian form h(v, v′ ) := iQ0 (v, v′ ) on
F1 (z) has Gram matrix
                                                      
                                    12 Im τ 12 Im µ
                                −                        ,    det = 24 Im τ · D < 0.
                                    12 Im µ 2 Im β
Thus h| F1 is nondegenerate of signature (1, 1) for either sign of Q0 : the fibres of J carry no monodromy-
compatible polarisation, and the arguments of this paper use none. The conclusion holds for every choice
of c0 , and indeed for every T0 -equivariant family on a punctured neighbourhood of p0 : positivity with
respect to ± Q0 would force Im τ and Im β to have the same strict sign, while both e±2πiτ , e±2πiβ (signs
making them bounded) are g0 -invariant, hence holomorphic in tc and vanishing at 0 to orders ν, ν′ ≥ 1 (a
non-zero value would make τ, resp. β, single-valued), so that continuation along the same loop would shift
τ and β by −ν and −ν′ , of the same sign; but (τ2), ( β1) give the shifts −1 and +1. Invariantly, the form
b N ( x, y) := Q0 ( x, Ny) on V/ ker N = ⟨w̄, δ̄⟩ is diag(6, −1), indefinite, so (V, Q0 , N ) supports no polarised
limit mixed Hodge structure.

and the description of J over {0 < |tc | < ϵ} as (Uϵ × C2 )/G from Proposition 2.11(iii).

4.2     The toric model Y = YF
Notation 4.1. N ′ := Z3 with standard basis e1 , e2 , e3 , points written (y, y3 ), y ∈ R2 ; M′ := Hom( N ′ , Z),
with characters χm of TN ′ = N ′ ⊗ C∗ = (C∗ )3 and coordinates x1 = χ(1,0,0) , x2 = χ(0,1,0) , t = χ(0,0,1) . The
cocharacter lattice Z2 of {( x1 , x2 , 1)} is identified with Λtor by e1 ↔ ŵ, e2 ↔ δ̂; then B0 : Λ → Λtor = Z2 ,
B0 γ̂¯ = −δ̂, B0 û¯ = ŵ.
      T A2 is the triangulation of R2 with vertex set Z2 and triangles {v, v + e1 , v + e2 }, {v + e1 , v + e2 , v + e1 +
e2 } (v ∈ Z2 ); equivalently it is cut out by the three pencils y1 ∈ Z, y2 ∈ Z, y1 + y2 ∈ Z. Its edge directions
are ±e1 , ±e2 , ±(e1 − e2 ), and it is invariant under all integral translations. For a cell (vertex, edge, triangle)
P put P̂ := cone( P × {1}) ⊂ NR     ′ and

Lemma 4.2.  (i) F is a fan of strongly convex rational polyhedral cones with |F| = (R2 × R>0 ) ∪ {0},
    and every cone of F is unimodular. Consequently Y is a connected, Hausdorff, second countable
    complex 3-manifold containing TN ′ as a dense open subset, with holomorphic TN ′ -action.

        divisor, by (iii)).

 (iii) If σ has vertices v0 , v1 , v2 and m0 , m1 , m2 ∈ M′ is the basis dual to (v0 , 1), (v1 , 1), (v2 , 1), then

Proof. (i) A direct computation gives det (v0 , 1), (v1 , 1), (v2 , 1) = det(e1 , e2 ) = 1 resp. det(e2 − e1 , e2 ) = −1
for the two types of triangle, so every σ̂ and hence every face is unimodular. For y3 > 0, (y, y3 ) ∈ P̂ iff
y/y3 ∈ P, and P̂ ∩ (R2 × {0}) = {0} as P is bounded; hence P̂ ∩ P̂′ = P ∩          \     P′ , a common face, and F is a fan
with the stated support. Smoothness, separatedness and the orbit–cone correspondence for Y = σ Uσ are
                                                                                                                  S

the standard toric statements [Ful93, §1.4, §3.1], [Oda88, §1.2–1.3]; they concern single cones and pairs of
cones, hence hold verbatim for the infinite fan F (only quasi-compactness is lost), as in [Mum72], [KKMS],
[AMRT]. In particular, for a triangle σ the orbits contained in Uσ are exactly the OP̂ with P̂ a face of σ̂, so
the only divisors Ev meeting Uσ are those with v a vertex of σ. F is countable, whence second countability;
all charts meet the dense irreducible TN ′ , whence connectedness.
     (ii) (0, 0, 1) takes the value y3 ≥ 0 on |F|, so t is regular on Y, and invertible on OP̂ iff (0, 0, 1) ∈ P̂⊥ ,
i.e. iff P̂ = 0; so t−1 (0) is the union of the orbit closures of the rays. The order of vanishing of χ(0,0,1)
along Ev is ⟨(0, 0, 1), (v, 1)⟩ = 1, since every primitive generator of a ray of F has height 1; this gives
div(t) = ∑v Ev with all multiplicities 1. Reducedness of the scheme t−1 (0) is then local and immediate
from (iii): in the chart Uσ ∼   = C3 one has t = z0 z1 z2 , and C[z0 , z1 , z2 ]/(z0 z1 z2 ) is reduced. (Note that this is a
statement upstairs, on Y, proved before and independently of any quotient construction; it will be quoted
in Theorem 4.5(d) and in Proposition 4.6(i).)
     (iii) Unimodularity gives σ̂∨ ∩ M′ = ∑i Z≥0 mi with (mi ) a basis of M′ , so Uσ ∼                          = C3 ; and
⟨(0, 0, 1), (v j , 1)⟩ = 1 gives (0, 0, 1) = m0 + m1 + m2 , i.e. t = z0 z1 z2 . The last assertion is the orbit–
cone correspondence: Evi ∩ Uσ is the closure of Ov̂i in Uσ , cut out by the characters χm with m ∈ σ̂∨ ,
⟨m, (vi , 1)⟩ > 0, i.e. by zi .

4.6   The central fibre W
Proposition 4.6. Let ν := pr| E0 : E0 → W and Cv := E0 ∩ Ev the six lines of the hexagon on E0 ∼
                                                                                               = dP6 .
  (i) ν is surjective, finite, and injective on E0 \ v Cv ; hence W is irreducible and connected, and ν is its
                                                     S

      normalisation. (Here W is reduced by Lemma 4.2(ii): all rays of F lie at height 1, so t−1 (0) is reduced
      upstairs, and pr is a local biholomorphism.)

 (ii) For each pair {v, −v}, v ∈ {e1 , e2 , e1 − e2 }, ν identifies Cv with C−v by Ψλ̄ |Cv , B0 λ̄ = −v, and makes
      no other identification.

 (iii) The double locus D ⊂ W consists of three smooth rational curves D1 , D2 , D3 ; any two meet exactly in
       the two triple points P, Q of W, the images of the two Λ-orbits of triangles of T A2 , through each of
       which all three pass. The six hexagon vertices of E0 map three-to-one onto { P, Q}, alternately around
       the hexagon.

 (iv) e(W ) = 2.

Proof. (i),(ii) By Theorem 4.5, W = t−1 (0)/Λ, with Ψλ̄ (OP̂ ) = O \ and B0 Λ = Z2 . Every cell has a vertex
                                                                    P+ B0 λ̄
and the vertices form one Z2 -orbit, so every OP̂ ⊂ t−1 (0) is equivalent to one with 0 ∈ P, i.e. contained
           S
in E0 = P∋0 OP̂ : ν is surjective, and its fibres have at most three points (three cells containing 0 lie in
one Z2 -orbit only if they are triangles), so ν is finite, E0 being compact. Two points of E0 are identified
only if their cells P, P′ ∋ 0 satisfy P′ = P + w, w ∈ Z2 \ {0}, which forces either P = {0, v}, P′ = {0, −v},
w = −v, or P, P′ triangles containing 0; in particular ν is injective on the open orbit. This is (ii) and the
identification of the hexagon vertices: the two Z2 -orbits of triangles are the “lower” ones {v, v + e1 , v + e2 }
and the “upper” ones {v + e1 , v + e2 , v + e1 + e2 }, and the six triangles containing 0 alternate between
them around the hexagon, by the cyclic list in the proof of Lemma 4.2(iv). Since E0 is smooth, compact
and irreducible with ν finite and generically injective, ν is the normalisation of W (W being reduced by
Theorem 4.5(d), whose proof of reducedness does not use the present Proposition), which is therefore
irreducible and connected.
    (iii) Each Cv ∼
                  = P1 maps isomorphically onto its image (no Ψλ̄ , λ̄ ̸= 0, preserves the edge {0, v}, and the
two triangles on {0, v} lie in different Z2 -classes), and Cv , C−v have the same image; this gives three smooth
rational curves D1 , D2 , D3 (images of Ce1 , Ce2 , Ce1 −e2 ) whose union is the locus where ν is not injective, i.e.
D. Each Cv contains exactly two hexagon vertices, one of each class, so each Di passes through P and Q;
conversely two distinct Di meet only where ν identifies points of two non-opposite lines of E0 , i.e. only at
P, Q.
    (iv) W is stratified by the images of the TN ′ -orbits of t−1 (0), one for each Λ-orbit of cells: one stratum
∼
= (C∗ )2 , three ∼
                 = C∗ (edge directions e1 , e2 , e1 − e2 ), and two points. By additivity of the topological Euler
characteristic over finite stratifications by locally closed subspaces, e(W ) = 0 + 3 · 0 + 2 = 2; equivalently
e(W ) = (6 − 6) + (3 · 2 − 2 · 2) = 2 from e(dP6 ) = 6. In sum, W is a single copy of dP6 with opposite sides
of its hexagon identified, with double locus the union of the three rational curves D1 , D2 , D3 through the
two triple points P, Q.

Corollary 4.8. π1 (Yϵ ) = 1; hence pr is the universal covering and π1 ( N0 ) ∼
                                                                              =Λ∼
                                                                                = Z2 . Moreover:
  (i) for 0 < |t0 | < ϵ the map π1 ( f 0−1 (t0 )) → π1 ( N0 ) is the projection Λ → Λ (in the marking of
      Proposition 4.7); in particular the vanishing cycles Λtor die in π1 ( N0 );

  (ii) the loop ς({|tc | = ρ}), 0 < ρ < ϵ, is null-homotopic in N0 .

Proof. First, π1 (Y ) = 1. As the statement of [Ful93, §3.2] is made for finite fans, we reduce to that case: for
n ∈ N let Fn ⊂ F be the (finite) subfan of the cones P̂ over the cells P of T A2 contained in the disc B̄(0, n),
together with {0}. Then YFn is an open subset of Y, Y = n≥1 YFn is an increasing union of open sets, and
                                                                 S

each YFn is a smooth toric variety of finite type whose rays R≥0 (v, 1), |v| ≤ n, span N ′ over Z (already
v = 0, e1 , e2 suffice, for n ≥ 1); hence π1 (YFn ) = N ′ /⟨(v, 1)⟩ = 1 by [Ful93, §3.2]. Since π1 commutes with
increasing unions of open sets (every loop and every null-homotopy has compact image, hence lies in some
YFn ), π1 (Y ) = limn π1 (YFn ) = 1.
                 −→
    For 0 < u ≤ 1 let ϑu = (1, 1, u) ∈ TN ′ ; then t(ϑu · p) = u t( p), so ϑu (Yϵ ) ⊂ Yϵ . A loop γ in Yϵ bounds a
disc h : D2 → Y; for u < ϵ/ supD2 |t ◦ h| the disc ϑu ◦ h lies in Yϵ and bounds ϑu ◦ γ, which is homotopic to


                                                                 30
γ in Yϵ through ϑu′ ◦ γ, u′ ∈ [u, 1]. Hence π1 (Yϵ ) = 1 and π1 ( N0 ) is the deck group Λ. (i) Restrict pr over
t0 : it is the covering (C∗ )2 → f 0−1 (t0 ) with deck group Λ, and π1 ((C∗ )2 ) = Λtor maps to 1 in π1 (Yϵ ) = 1.
(ii) The loop lifts to {(1, 1)} × {|t| = ρ} ⊂ Yϵ .

Remark 4.9. The only properties of the data entering §§4.2–4.7 are: (a) B0 ∈ M2 (Z) with det B0 ̸= 0, used
through B0 Λ ⊂ Z2 = Vert(T A2 ) and through | B0 λ̄| ≥ c B0 |λ̄|, c B0 > 0 (Steps 1,2); | det B0 | = 1, i.e. the
equality B0 Λ = Z2 , is used in addition in Lemma 4.3(i), in the boundary case of Step 4 of Theorem 4.5
(solving B0 λ̄ = −v0 for an arbitrary vertex v0 ), and in Proposition 4.6; the torus case of Step 4 uses only
∥ Bt − B0 ∥ ≤ 12 and the invertibility of Bt (if one only assumes B0 Λ ⊂ Z2 of finite index, the boundary case
of Step 4 still goes through with 12 replaced by a constant depending on B0 , namely the covering radius of
                                           √
B0 Λ in the sup-norm, and with B̄(0, 2) enlarged accordingly); in Proposition 4.6 the action on vertices
must be simply transitive, so that W is irreducible with normalisation a single dP6 (for | det B0 | = d one
would get d components and e(W ) = 2d); (b) holomorphy of C on the full disc {|tc | < r }, used twice:
through the bound ∥R∥∞ < ∞ (Steps 1,2,4), and — through holomorphy of C at tc = 0 — to make τλ̄ ,
hence Ψλ̄ , holomorphic across t = 0 in Lemma 4.3(iii). No symmetry or positivity of B0 is used or available
(B0 maps Λ to the different lattice Λtor , and the family of §3 carries no polarisation). Re-indexing λ̄ 7→ −λ̄
                  ( B ,C )      (− B ,−C )
shows that {Ψλ̄ 0 } and {Ψλ̄ 0             } are the same subgroup of Aut(Yϵ ); substituting µ := B0 λ̄ rewrites
                ′             ′
(4.2) as x 7→ cµ (t)t x with cµ := c B−1 µ , so N0 is a two-dimensional Tate-type degeneration twisted by the

holomorphic matrix CB0−1 . Here T A2 is prescribed, Z2 -periodic, locally finite with bounded unimodular

the only features of it that the above arguments require; correspondingly f 0 is not asserted to be projective
and the fibres f 0−1 (t0 ) need not be algebraic — as they must not be, for the global manifold X of §6.


5     The fillings at p1 and p2 : logarithmic transforms.
Conventions for this section
Throughout, j ∈ {1, 2} is fixed, m1 = 3, m2 = 4, and all notation is that of §2 and §3; for S ∈ GL(V )
we write V S := ker(S − I ), and Λ A j = ker( A j − I ). The subscripted ζ j = e−2πi/m j is a root of unity, the
unsubscripted ζ the fibre coordinate on C2 . The construction modifies the family J only over a disc D j ∋ p j ,
leaving J | B◦ untouched.

5.1   The local family and its tautological deck transformation
Since ∆ acts properly discontinuously on hz with stabiliser ⟨ g j ⟩ at z j , we may choose the disc ∆ j ∋ z j of
§3 so small that g j ∆ j = ∆ j , that the linearising coordinate s j maps ∆ j biholomorphically onto a round disc
{|s| < r j } with s j (z j ) = 0 and s j ◦ g j = ζ j s j , and that g∆ j ∩ ∆ j = ∅ for g ∈ ∆ ∖ ⟨ g j ⟩. We fix once and for all
the following terminology, used here and in §6–§7.

Definition 5.1. A linearising pair (∆ j , s j ) at z j consists of a g j -invariant simply connected open neighbourhood
∆ j ∋ z j in hz with g∆ j ∩ ∆ j = ∅ for g ∈ ∆ ∖ ⟨ g j ⟩, together with a coordinate s j on ∆ j (a holomorphic
injection) such that
                                 s j (z j ) = 0,        sj ◦ gj = ζ j sj ,    ζ j = e−2πi/m j .
The pair is round if moreover s j (∆ j ) = {|s| < r j } for some r j > 0.

     By the previous paragraph we may and do choose (∆ j , s j ) to be a round linearising pair; in particular
∆ j is simply connected. Simple connectedness is used in §6 (Proposition 6.3(c),(d)), where branches of log
of nowhere-vanishing functions on ∆ j are taken, and it is retained as part of the convention. Beyond
simple connectedness, nothing below uses the shape of ∆ j : every statement of this section and of §6 holds
verbatim for an arbitrary linearising pair (∆ j , s j ) in the sense of Definition 5.1, round or not. Then π |∆ j

5.2     Order, fixed points, freeness
Lemma 5.5. (i) Let A ∈ GL(Λ) have finite order, b ∈ Λ ⊗ Q, and let α( x ) = Ax + b be the induced
self-map of (Λ ⊗ R)/Λ. Then α has a fixed point if and only if φ(b) ∈ Z for every φ ∈ V = Hom(Λ, Z)
                               2                                                  2      3
with φ ◦ A = φ. (ii) V T1 = V T1 = ⟨γ, ψ1 ⟩ with ψ1 := 2u + w + 3δ, and V T2 = V T2 = V T2 = ⟨γ, ψ2 ⟩ with
ψ2 := u + w + 2δ.

Proof. (i) Put ΛR := Λ ⊗ R, W := { φ ∈ V : φ ◦ A = φ}. A fixed point exists iff b ∈ ( A − I )ΛR + Λ, which
forces φ(b) ∈ Z for φ ∈ W. Conversely let N be the order of A and p := N1 ∑i< N Ai , a rational projector
with image U = ker( A − I )R and kernel U ′ = im( A − I )R , so ΛR = U ⊕ U ′ . Every φ ∈ WR := W ⊗ R
kills U ′ and dim WR = dim U, so the pairing U × WR → R is perfect. The group p(Λ) ⊂ U is a lattice (it
spans U and lies in N1 Λ ∩ U) and φ( pλ) = φ(λ) for φ ∈ WR ; hence the dual lattice of p(Λ) is W and, by
biduality, p(Λ) = {u ∈ U : φ(u) ∈ Z ∀ φ ∈ W }. If φ(b) ∈ Z for all φ ∈ W, then φ( pb) = φ(b) ∈ Z, so
pb = pλ with λ ∈ Λ and b − λ ∈ U ′ .
    (ii) A direct computation with the matrices of §2 gives, for x = aγ + bu + cw + dδ,
                              T1 x = ( a − 6c + 2d)γ + (−b + c + d)u + (−b + d)w + dδ,
                              T2 x = ( a + 6b − 3d)γ + (−c + d)u + bw + dδ,
so that T1 x = x iff b = 2c and d = 3c, while T2 x = x iff c = b and d = 2b. Hence V T1 = ⟨γ, ψ1 ⟩ and
          3                     2      −1
V T2 = V T2 = ⟨γ, ψ2 ⟩. Also V T1 = V T1 = V T1 , and T22 acts by
                             γ 7→ γ,      u 7→ 6γ − u,      w 7→ −6γ − w,           δ 7→ u + w + δ,
with fixed space {b = c, d = 2b} = ⟨γ, ψ2 ⟩.

Remark 5.8 (The twist parameter). Theorem 5.4 holds for every v ∈ Λ A j satisfying Proposition 5.6, with
the same global family J: the surgery takes place over D j only and Gj is always a biholomorphism onto
J | D∗j . As Λ A1 = ⟨ε, δ̂⟩, Λ A2 = ⟨ε′ , δ̂⟩ with γ(ε) = γ(ε′ ) = 1 and γ(δ̂) = 0, the integer γ(v) realises every
                                                                     v/m j        (v+m µ)/m
admissible residue class. By Lemma 5.2(iii), g̃ j     = g̃ j   j   j
                                                                     for µ ∈ Λ A j , so Nj depends on v only
modulo m j Λ A j , whereas Gj depends on v itself: replacing v by v + m j µ composes Gj with translation by

                                                                             35
