the single-valued section 2πi j Πµ of J | D∗j ; this dependence is taken up in §6 and used in §7. For X we take
v1 = ε, v2 = −ε′ , so (ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (1, −1); for comparison, the threefold X ′ with v2 = +ε′ , i.e. (ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (1, 1),
is equally admissible.


6     The compact complex manifold X.
Throughout we use the family f J : J → B◦ of §3, the toric filling f 0 : N0 → D0 of §4, the logarithmic fillings
f j : Nj → D j ( j = 1, 2) of §5, and the gluing maps G0 , G1 , G2 constructed there.

6.1    The glued space
Construction 6.1. Fix once and for all: (i) the orbifold cover π : hz → B ∖ { p0 } with z1 , z2 , g1 , g2 and the
period functions τ, µ, β of §3, in particular a constant c0 for which condition ( β3) of Definition 3.1 holds;
(ii) a radius r > 0 as in §3 (so µ and β + τ are bounded on Ur ), a branch of log u1 on {|tc | < r }, hence
h, s = τ − h(tc ◦ π ) and C (tc ); (iii) a number ϵ with 0 < ϵ < min{r, 1} small enough that Theorem 4.5
applies, and linearising pairs (∆1 , s1 ), (∆2 , s2 ) in the sense of §5, small enough that Theorem 5.4 applies,
with branches of log s j on simply connected subsets of ∆∗j .
       Put D0 := {|tc | < ϵ}, D j := π (∆ j ), Di∗ := Di ∖ { pi }; shrinking ϵ and ∆ j we assume D0 , D1 , D2 pairwise
disjoint, so that
                                  B = B◦ ∪ D0 ∪ D1 ∪ D2 ,           Di∗ ⊂ B◦ (i = 0, 1, 2).                       (6.1)
                                                                                            ∼
By Proposition 4.7(iii) the map G0 induced by E0 is a biholomorphism G0 : J | D0 −−→ N0 ∖ W with f 0 ◦ G0 =
                                                                                          ∗

 f J , and by Theorem 5.4 the map Gj induced by h j is a biholomorphism
                                                           ∼
                               Gj : Nj ∖ f j−1 ( p j ) −−→ J | D∗j ,          f J ◦ Gj = f j      ( j = 1, 2).
Let Z := J ⊔ N0 ⊔ N1 ⊔ N2 and let ∼ be generated by p ∼ G0 ( p) ( p ∈ J | D0∗ ) and q ∼ Gj (q) (q ∈ Nj ∖ f j−1 ( p j )).
Set X := Z / ∼ with the quotient topology, let ι J , ι N0 , ι N1 , ι N2 be the induced maps, and let f : X → B equal
f J on ι J ( J ), f 0 on ι N0 ( N0 ) and f j on ι Nj ( Nj ).

Theorem 6.2. X is a connected compact Hausdorff complex manifold of dimension 3, and f : X → B ∼
                                                                                               = P1
is a proper surjective holomorphic map with connected fibres. More precisely:

    (1) ι J , ι N0 , ι N1 , ι N2 are open holomorphic embeddings whose images cover X, with
                           f −1 ( B ◦ ) = ι J ( J ) ,   f −1 ( D0 ) = ι N0 ( N0 ),   f −1 ( D j ) = ι Nj ( Nj ) ( j = 1, 2),
        and f corresponds under ι J , resp. ι Ni , to f J , resp. f i .

    (2) For b ∈ B◦ the fibre f −1 (b) is the complex 2-torus C2 /L(z), π (z) = b; f −1 ( p0 ) = W as a reduced
        divisor; and f ∗ ( p j ) = m j S j , j = 1, 2, with S j the bielliptic surface of Theorem 5.4.

Proof. Since D0 , D1 , D2 are pairwise disjoint, the sets J | Di∗ are pairwise disjoint in J, while the Ni ∖ f i−1 ( pi )
lie in different summands; hence ∼ is already an equivalence relation whose nonsingleton classes are
the pairs { p, G0 ( p)}, {q, Gj (q)}, and each class meets each summand at most once. The saturation of an
open U ⊂ Z is the union of U with the images of U ∩ J | Di∗ and U ∩ ( Ni ∖ f i−1 ( pi )) under G0±1 , Gj±1 , hence
open; so the quotient map is open and the four ι are open embeddings covering X. Transporting the
complex structures gives an atlas whose only transition maps are the biholomorphisms G0±1 , Gj±1 , so X is
a complex 3-manifold. The three definitions of f agree on overlaps because f 0 ◦ G0 = f J and f J ◦ Gj = f j ;
since ι N0 ( N0 ∖ W ) and ι Nj ( Nj ∖ f j−1 ( p j )) lie in ι J ( J ), (1) follows, and (2) restates §3, Theorem 4.5 and
Theorem 5.4.
    Two points of X with distinct images in B are separated by f -preimages of disjoint discs, and two points
with the same image lie, by (6.1) and (1), in a common Hausdorff chart f −1 (V ), V ∈ { B◦ , D0 , D1 , D2 }; so X
is Hausdorff. Over each such V the map f is f J , f 0 or f j , hence proper; properness being local on the base


                                                                       36
for maps of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, f is proper and X = f −1 ( B) compact. Finally J, N0 , Nj are
connected, being quotients of h◦z × C2 , of Yϵ and of T |∆ j , and each Ni meets ι J ( J ); so X is connected, f ( X )
is a compact analytic subset of B containing B◦ , i.e. f is surjective, and the fibres are connected by (2) with
Theorems 4.5 and 5.4.

Remark 6.4. The construction does depend on c0 : already the fibres C2 /L(z) vary with it. We write Xc0
when the choice matters. The constructions of §§4–6 and the proofs of §§7–10 are written for an arbitrary
admissible c0 — admissible in the sense of condition ( β3) of Definition 3.1 — and use the period functions
τ, µ, β only through the laws and normalisations of §3, which hold for every admissible c0 . In particular
no continuity or local constancy of Xc0 in the parameter c0 enters the arguments. Whether Xc0 ∼   = Xc0′ for
                  ′
admissible c0 ̸= c0 is left open.

6.3    The zero section
By Theorem 3.4 the maps g̃ are fibrewise linear, so g̃(z, 0) = ( gz, 0) and z 7→ [(z, 0)] descends to a

Remark 6.8. Thus ℓ0 is a discrete parameter of the p0 -gluing with values in γ(Λ) = Z, and it is not a
function of the transcendental constants µ( p0 ), b(0), h(0): the filling N0 is manufactured from C (tc ) itself,
and twists with c(σ ) ∈ Λtor leave ℓ0 unchanged. The triple (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (0, γ(v1 ), γ(v2 )) = (0, 1, −1) is the
complete list of discrete gluing data of X, and enters π1 ( X ) in §7.


7     Topology of X: fundamental group and integral homology.
7.1   Decomposition, collapse, and the pieces
The integral homology of X is computed twice on the following pages, by two independent routes: first
by the collapse/Mayer–Vietoris computation — the collapse of a general fibre onto the central fibre W
(Proposition 7.2, proved in §7.2), the homology of W and the specialisation maps (§7.3), and Mayer–Vietoris
for X = N0′ ∪ X ◦ (§7.6), culminating in Theorem 7.22; and secondly by the Leray computation of §7.7 (“A
second computation”) (for admissible pairs with 4 | ε 2 (v2 ), in particular for X), which recovers H 1 , H 2 , H 3
of X from E2a,b = H a ( B, Rb f ∗ Z). Neither computation uses the other: the Leray route takes as input
only the invariants statement at p0 (Theorem B.1, proved in Appendix B by nearby cycles, without §7.2),
the local data at p1 , p2 of §7.4 (Lemma 7.13, Proposition 7.14), the meridian identifications of §7.5 (with
Lemma 7.16) and Corollary 4.8; it uses neither Proposition 7.2 nor Proposition 7.11 nor §7.6 (Theorem 7.22);
see Corollary 7.29.
    Throughout, (co)homology has coefficients Z and F denotes a general fibre of f , a real 4-torus; by §2

Lemma 7.5 (Straightening to a constant twist). There is η0 with 0 < η0 < min(ε, 1) — so that Y≤η0 ⊂ Yε
and log |t| < 0 for 0 < |t| ≤ η0 — such that ∥ Bt − B0 ∥ ≤ 12 , hence Bt ∈ GL2 (R), and likewise Bt0 ∈ GL2 (R),
for all 0 < |t| ≤ η0 . Fix 0 < η ≤ η0 and define
                                                                         
                G ( x, t) := exp − 2πi (C (t) − C0 ) Bt−1 y( x, t) · x, t
                                                                  
                                                                            ( t ̸ = 0), G |Y0 := id.


                                                                  42
Then G is a homeomorphism of Y≤η over {|t| ≤ η } with the properties:

  (a) G |Y0 = id; G preserves t and commutes with T f ;

  (b) G ◦ Ψλ̄ = Ψ0λ̄ ◦ G for every λ̄ ∈ Λ;

  (c) on each fibre Yt (0 < |t| ≤ η ), G induces a real-linear isomorphism Ḡt : Yt /Γ → Yt /Γ0 of the quotient
      4-tori which is the identity on H1 = Λ with respect to the markings Πt = [ Zt | I ] and Π0t = [ Zt0 | I ] (it
      fixes e1 , e2 and carries Zt λ̄ to Zt0 λ̄); here Γ, Γ0 preserve Yt since Ψλ̄ preserves t, and (b) makes Ḡt well
      defined.

Consequently, if R is a Γ0 - and Tc -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 , then G −1 Rs G
is a Γ- and T f -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 , and it descends to a strong
deformation retraction of N0′ = Y≤η /Γ onto W = Y0 /Γ.

Proof. By the boundedness of C on {|tc | ≤ r/2} (§4, the bound ∥R∥∞ of Theorem 4.5) we may fix
η0 < min(ε, 1) so small that ∥ Bt − B0 ∥ ≤ 12 for 0 < |t| ≤ η0 ; then Bt ∈ GL2 (R), and the same estimate and
conclusion hold for Bt0 = B0 + R(0)/ log |t|. Fix 0 < η ≤ η0 .
   In ξ-coordinates G |Yt is induced by the R-linear map
                                         Lt (ξ ) = ξ − (C (t) − C0 )(Im Zt )−1 Im ξ,
which is the unique R-linear map with Lt |R2 = id and Lt ( Zt λ̄) = Zt0 λ̄ for all λ̄ ∈ R2 : writing ξ = Zt a + b
with a = (Im Zt )−1 Im ξ and b ∈ R2 , one has Lt ξ = Zt0 a + b. Consequently:
     (i) Lt carries the R-basis (e1 , e2 , Zt e1 , Zt e2 ) of C2 to the R-basis (e1 , e2 , Zt0 e1 , Zt0 e2 ), hence is invertible;
being the identity on R2 ⊃ Z2 it descends to a diffeomorphism of C2 /Z2 = Yt .
     (ii) G ◦ Ψλ̄ = Ψ0λ̄ ◦ G on Y≤η \ Y0 , because Lt is linear and carries the translation vector Zt λ̄ to Zt0 λ̄; on
Y0 the two actions agree and G = id. This is (b).
     (iii) G preserves t and commutes with T f , since it multiplies x by a torus element depending only on
(y, t) and T f does not change y; together with G |Y0 = id this is (a).
     (iv) G and G −1 (given by the same formula with (C (t) − C0 )( Bt0 )−1 in place of −(C (t) − C0 ) Bt−1 ) are
continuous on Y≤η . Away from Y0 this is clear. At p∗ ∈ Y0 continuity is a local statement, so we may first
shrink: choose a triangle σ with p∗ ∈ Uσ and, by Lemma 4.4(i), a shrunken subchart Uσ (S) ∋ p∗ on which
the positions y( p) of torus points p ∈ Uσ (S) ∩ Y≤η remain in a fixed bounded set (on all of Uσ ∼                      = C3 the
                                                                   −1
positions are unbounded), while C (t) − C0 → 0 and Bt stays bounded as t → 0; hence the acting torus
element tends to 1 and G ( p) → p∗ = G ( p∗ ) for p → p∗ inside Uσ (S). Points of Y0 near p∗ are fixed.
     So G is a homeomorphism of Y≤η over {|t| ≤ η } with (a), (b); and on each fibre it is induced by the
real-linear automorphism Lt , whence a real-linear isomorphism Ḡt : Yt /Γ → Yt /Γ0 of the quotient 4-tori
which is the identity on H1 = Λ with respect to the markings Πt = [ Zt | I ] and Π0t = [ Zt0 | I ] (it fixes e1 , e2
and carries Zt λ̄ to Zt0 λ̄); here Γ, Γ0 preserve Yt since Ψλ̄ preserves t, and (b) makes Ḡt well defined. This is
(c).
     Finally, let R be a Γ0 - and Tc -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 . Then G −1 Rs G
is a strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 (as G is a homeomorphism fixing Y0 pointwise), it is
Γ-equivariant by (b) and T f -equivariant by (a); being Γ-equivariant it descends to the quotients N0′ = Y≤η /Γ
and W = Y0 /Γ.

attached to saturated sublattices with L1 + L2 saturated one has TL1 ∩ TL2 = TL1 ∩ L2 , which is connected.

a − b = n1 + n2 with ni ∈ Li ; then a − n1 = b + n2 ∈ ( L1 ∩ L2 ) ⊗ R, so a represents a point of TL1 ∩ L2 .)

closed subset of a finite union of compact hexagons, hence compact. A continuous proper bijection between
locally compact Hausdorff spaces is a homeomorphism; thus ϑ is a Γ+ -equivariant homeomorphism. In
particular (Y0 )≥0 ∼
                   = R2 is contractible.



                                                              45
Lemma 7.8 (Retracting the positive quotient). Let 0 < η ≤ η0 and put
                                   Q+ := (Y≤η )≥0 /Γ+ ,     H := (Y0 )≥0 /Γ+ ⊂ Q+ .
Then Q+ is a compact topological 3-manifold with boundary H ⊔ Tη2 , where Tη2 := {|t| = η }≥0 /Γ+ ∼    =
Ry /Bη Λ is a 2-torus (Γ acts on the slice by y 7→ y + Bη λ̄, Lemma 7.7(ii), and Bη ∈ GL2 (R), Lemma 7.5),
  2   +                 +                               +                         +

the boundary component H is a 2-torus, and there exists
                        P̄ : Q+ × [0, 1] → Q+ ,       P̄0 = id,   P̄s |H = id,   P̄1 ( Q+ ) = H.

Proof. The action of Γ+ on (Y≤η )≥0 is free and properly discontinuous, being the restriction to the closed
invariant subset (Y≤η )≥0 ⊂ Yε of the free properly discontinuous action of Theorem 4.5 for the data ( B0 , C + )
(Lemma 7.7(ii)); and it preserves the boundary (Y0 )≥0 ⊔ {|t| = η }>0 of the manifold-with-boundary
(Y≤η )≥0 (Lemma 7.7(iii)), since it preserves Y0 and the function |t|. Hence Q+ is a topological 3-manifold
with boundary H ⊔ Tη2 . It is compact: A := (Y≤η )≥0 is closed and Γ+ -invariant, i.e. saturated for the
open quotient map q : Y≤η → Y≤η /Γ+ ; hence q( A) is closed (its complement is the image of the invariant
open set Y≤η \ A), so compact, Y≤η /Γ+ being compact (Lemma 7.7(ii)); and the restriction of a quotient
map to a saturated closed subset is a quotient map onto its image, so Q+ = A/Γ+ ∼       = q( A). Its universal
                                                         ∼
cover (Y≤η )≥0 is contractible (Lemma 7.7(iii)); and H = R /B0 Λ (via ϑ, Lemma 7.7(iv)) is a 2-torus with
                                                             2

contractible universal cover (Y0 )≥0 . Thus Q+ and H are both K (Z2 , 1)’s, and the inclusion H ,→ Q+ , being
covered by the Γ+ -equivariant inclusion of the two contractible covers, induces the identity of Γ+ on π1
and isomorphisms on all πk . Compact manifolds are ANRs, hence of CW homotopy type, so H ,→ Q+ is
a homotopy equivalence (Whitehead, [Hat02, Thm. 4.5]). As H is a boundary component of the compact
manifold-with-boundary Q+ it has a collar [Bro62], so the inclusion is a cofibration, and a homotopy
equivalence which is a cofibration is the inclusion of a strong deformation retract [Hat02, Prop. 0.19,
Cor. 0.20]: such a P̄ exists.

Lemma 7.9 (Spreading by phases). Let 0 < η ≤ η0 and let P : (Y≤η )≥0 × [0, 1] → (Y≤η )≥0 be continuous
with

(P1) P0 = id;

(P2) Ps |(Y0 )≥0 = id for all s;
                    
(P3) P1 (Y≤η )≥0 ⊂ (Y0 )≥0 ;

(P4) every Ps is Γ+ -equivariant.

Then
                              Rs (ϕ · q) := ϕ · Ps (q)  (ϕ ∈ Tc , q ∈ (Y≤η )≥0 )
is a well-defined, continuous, Γ0 - and Tc -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 ; con-
sequently (Lemma 7.5) G −1 Rs G is a Γ- and T f -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 ,
which descends to a strong deformation retraction rs of N0′ onto W. Moreover such a P exists: the lift of the
retraction P̄ of Lemma 7.8 through the universal covering (Y≤η )≥0 → Q+ with P0 = id satisfies (P1)–(P4).

Proof. Existence. Lift P̄ through the universal covering (Y≤η )≥0 → Q+ to the unique homotopy Ps with
P0 = id. By uniqueness of lifts, Ps is Γ+ -equivariant, Ps |(Y0 )≥0 = id, and P1 is a retraction onto (Y0 )≥0 ; i.e.
(P1)–(P4) hold.
     The construction. Let now P satisfy (P1)–(P4). Then Rs is well defined: if ϕ · q = ϕ′ · q then ϕ−1 ϕ′ ∈
StabTc (q) (Lemma 7.6(ii)), and either q lies in the interior, where the stabiliser is trivial, or q ∈ ∂; on
{|t| = η }>0 the stabiliser is again trivial, and on (Y0 )≥0 we have Ps (q) = q by (P2), so ϕ · Ps (q) = ϕ′ · Ps (q).
It is continuous because Rs ◦ m = m ◦ (id × Ps ) and m × id[0,1] is a quotient map (m is closed by Lemma 7.6(i)
and [0, 1] is compact; the product of a closed quotient map with the identity of a locally compact space is
again a quotient map). By construction R commutes with Tc , and by (7.2)
                 Rs Ψ0λ̄ (ϕ · q) = Rs uλ̄ φλ̄ (ϕ) · Ψ+  q = uλ̄ φλ̄ (ϕ) · Ψ+ P (q) = Ψ0λ̄ Rs (ϕ · q) ,
                                                                                                  

using (P4). Finally R0 = id by (P1), Rs |Y0 = id by (P2) (as Y0 = Tc · (Y0 )≥0 ) and R1 (Y≤η ) ⊂ Tc · (Y0 )≥0 = Y0
by (P3). So R is a Γ0 - and Tc -equivariant strong deformation retraction of Y≤η onto Y0 , and the last assertion
is the final clause of Lemma 7.5.

Proof of Proposition 7.2. Let η0 be the constant of Lemma 7.5, let 0 < η ≤ η0 and fix t0 with 0 < |t0 | ≤ η; put
F = f −1 ( t 0 ).
      By Lemmas 7.7 and 7.8 the quotient Q+ admits a strong deformation retraction P̄ onto H; by Lemma 7.9
its lift P satisfies (P1)–(P4) and produces a Γ0 - and Tc -equivariant strong deformation retraction R of Y≤η
onto Y0 , whence, by Lemma 7.5, a {Ψλ̄ }- and T f -equivariant strong deformation retraction G −1 Rs G of

the honeycomb homeomorphism ϑ of Lemma 7.7) the map cϑ , read in the splitting Ft00 ∼        = TB × T f , collapses
T f over each vertex of Θ, collapses Snk over the interior of ek onto C̄k and is injective over TB \ Θ; since Ḡ is
                                       1

an isomorphism of tori which is the identity on the Λ-markings, the composite c = r | F = cϑ ◦ Ḡ has exactly
the description stated in the Proposition. Finally, the last assertion of the Proposition is Lemma 7.10(iii).

7.3   The homology of W and the specialisation maps
Proposition 7.11. H∗ (W ) = (Z, Z2 , Z4 , Z2 , Z), torsion free, with H2 (W ) = ⟨[C̄1 ], [C̄2 ], [C̄3 ], [TB ]⟩, TB :=
c( TB × {pt}). Moreover π1 (W ) ∼
                                = Z2 and e(W ) = 2.
Proof. Let ν : dP6 → W be the normalisation (§4); D = C̄1 ∪ C̄2 ∪ C̄3 , three copies of P1 meeting pairwise
exactly at P, Q, so H∗ (D) = (Z, Z2 , Z3 , 0, 0) and e(D) = 2.
    Let ∂ ⊂ dP6 be the hexagonal cycle of six (−1)-curves C1 , . . . , C6 . It is nodal (six nodes), hence not a
submanifold, and we use instead a regular (plumbing) neighbourhood N of ∂ in dP6 : the plumbing of six disc
bundles according to the hexagonal cycle, a compact 4-manifold with boundary which deformation retracts
onto ∂ and whose interior is an open neighbourhood of ∂. Any such N will do: since ν is injective off ∂,
and the identifications Ci → Ci+3 by which ν glues the six curves in opposite pairs take place inside ∂ ⊂ N ,
the set N is ν-saturated, and a strong deformation retraction of N onto ∂ fixing ∂ pointwise is compatible


                                                          50

coefficients H q (S j ) is torsion free for all q.

Remark 7.15. For a map φ of free finitely generated abelian groups, coker( φ∨ ) ∼= Ext(coker φ, Z) ⊕
Hom(ker φ, Z); so π2∗ : H2 ( F ) → H2 (S2 ) has image of index 2, whereas π1∗ is onto. This produces

7.5    The fundamental group

tλ (λ ∈ Λ) changes ℓ j by m j γ(λ) (with m0 := 1), hence changes p = 12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 of Theorem 7.17 by

gluing at p0 (ℓ0 = 0),
                                  |π1 ( X )| = | H1 ( X )| = 4γ(v1 ) + 3γ(v2 )
independently of every orientation convention: for our data (v1 , v2 ) = (ε, −ε′ ) this is |4 − 3| = 1, and for
the comparison threefold X ′ with (v1 , v2 ) = (ε, +ε′ ) it is |4 + 3| = 7.

Proof. A sign could enter at four places: (S1) the rotation sense of g j at z j ; (S2) the translation part +v j /m j
    log
of g̃ j ; (S3) the regluing section ς j and its drift; (S4) the identification of the meridian lift with a power of
the deck generator. We show that at each place the same sign enters for j = 1 and for j = 2; only (S1) has
mathematical content, (S2)–(S4) being the observation that §5–§7.5 apply one formula with j as a parameter.
      (S1) The two elliptic generators rotate in the same sense, and this is forced. By Proposition 2.11(ii), ∆ is
the orientation-preserving subgroup of the reflection group of the hyperbolic triangle ABC with angles
π/3, π/4, 0 and vertices in counterclockwise order; x = σb σc and y = σc σa are the counterclockwise rotations
about A and B by 2π/3 and 2π/4, z = σa σb is parabolic, xyz = 1, and ∆ = ⟨ x ⟩ ∗ ⟨y⟩ ∼            = Z/3 ∗ Z/4. The
paper sets g1 := x −1 , g2 := y−1 — the two rotations are reversed simultaneously — so that g1′ (z1 ) =
e−2πi/3 = ζ 1 , g2′ (z2 ) = e−2πi/4 = ζ 2 and g0 := ( g1 g2 )−1 is parabolic (conjugate to z−1 ).
      That “simultaneously” is forced. Since the quotient has exactly one cusp (Proposition 2.11(iii); cf. [Bea83,
Ch. 10]), the parabolic elements of ∆ are exactly the conjugates of the non-zero powers of z = ( xy)−1 , i.e. of
the cyclically reduced words ( xy)n and (y−1 x −1 )n = (y3 x2 )n (n ≥ 1); and two cyclically reduced words in
a free product are conjugate if and only if they are cyclic permutations of one another. Suppose one tried
the mixed assignment ( g1 , g2 ) = ( x −1 , y). Then g0 = ( g1 g2 )−1 = y−1 x = y3 x, a cyclically reduced word of
syllable length 2, which is not a cyclic permutation of xy (permutations xy, yx) nor of y3 x2 (permutations
y3 x2 , x2 y3 ), hence not conjugate to any ( xy)±n : g0 is not parabolic. The symmetric mixed assignment
( g1 , g2 ) = ( x, y−1 ) is excluded in the same way: there g0 = ( xy−1 )−1 = yx −1 = yx2 , again cyclically reduced

                                                               55
of syllable length 2 and not a cyclic permutation of xy or of y3 x2 , hence not conjugate to any ( xy)±n .
(Conjugates hy±1 h−1 are not at issue here, since g1 and g2 are the rotations about the vertices A and B of the
fixed fundamental triangle, not arbitrary conjugates of them.) Geometrically, rot( A, −2π/3) ◦ rot( B, +2π/4)
is hyperbolic. Since §3 needs g0 parabolic — τ ◦ g0 = τ − 1, and g0 generates the cusp stabiliser used to
build the coordinate s at p0 (Proposition 2.11(iii)) — the rotation senses of g1 and g2 must agree.
     Which sense is then fixed at z1 . The law τ ◦ g1 = R1 ◦ τ with R1 (τ ) = 1 − 1/τ, τ a local biholomorphism
at z1 with τ (z1 ) = ρ = eiπ/3 (Proposition 3.6: τ − ρ has order 1 at z1 ), gives on differentiating g1′ (z1 ) =
R1′ (ρ) = ρ−2 = e−2πi/3 : clockwise. At z2 no independent check is available from τ alone: there τ − i
vanishes to order 2, so from τ ◦ g2 = S ◦ τ with S(τ ) = −1/τ one obtains, comparing the quadratic terms,
only
                                 g2′ (z2 )2 = S′ (i ) = −1,         i.e.    g2′ (z2 ) = ±i,
both signs being compatible with the order-2 vanishing of τ − i; this is exactly why the coupling through
g0 matters. Hence within the paper there is precisely one consistent choice, the printed one: ζ 1 = e−2πi/3 ,
ζ 2 = e−2πi/4 , both clockwise, which is Convention 3.17.

Lemma 7.21 (sweeping). Let S1 act smoothly and freely on a smooth manifold Z (possibly with boundary),
so that p : Z → Z/S1 is a smooth principal S1 -bundle, oriented along its fibres by the orbit direction, i.e.
by the standard generator [S1 ] ∈ H1 (S1 ), and let p! : H k+1 ( Z ) → H k ( Z/S1 ) be integration over the fibre for
that orientation. Normalise the slant product by
                      [S1 ]\α, y = α, [S1 ] × y           α ∈ H k+1 (S1 × Z ), y ∈ Hk ( Z ) .
                                                                                            

With a : S1 × Z → Z the action map, define
                                                                  sw(y) := a∗ [S1 ] × y .
                                                                                       
                               sw : Hk ( Z ) → Hk+1 ( Z ),
Then ⟨A, sw(y)⟩ = ⟨ιA, y⟩ for A ∈ H k+1 ( Z ), where ιA := p∗ p! A. Moreover the fibrewise translation by
δ̂ ∈ Λ⟨ A1 ,A2 ⟩ defines such a smooth free S1 -action on the compact manifold with boundary X ◦ , whose
restriction to F is translation, so that ι| H ∗ ( F) = ι δ̂ , contraction with δ̂. (The opposite choice of generator [S1 ]
reverses the fibre orientation and changes ι by a global sign, immaterial below.)

Proof. First, by naturality and the above normalisation of the slant product,
                                  ⟨A, a∗ ([S1 ] × y)⟩ = ⟨ a∗ A, [S1 ] × y⟩ = ⟨[S1 ]\ a∗ A, y⟩.
Next, [S1 ]\ a∗ A = p∗ p! A. Indeed, for a free action the map S1 × Z → Z × Z/S1 Z, (θ, z) 7→ (θz, z), is a
diffeomorphism, so (pr2 , a) identifies the trivial circle bundle pr2 : S1 × Z → Z with the pullback of
the oriented principal circle bundle p : Z → Z/S1 along p, the map a covering p; the fibre orientations
correspond (both are the orbit direction, oriented by [S1 ]). Integration over the fibre commutes with such
base change, whence (pr2 )! a∗ A = p∗ p! A; and for the product bundle pr2 , integration over the fibre is, with
the above normalisation of the slant product and of the fibre orientation, precisely [S1 ]\(·). This proves the
first assertion.
    Since δ̂ is ⟨ A1 , A2 ⟩-invariant and the transition maps R g are linear, translation by tδ̂ (t ∈ R/Z) is well
defined and smooth on J and on N1 , N2 . On J it is translation by tδ̂ in the fibre torus (Λ ⊗ R)/Λ, free
                                                                                ( j)
because δ̂ is primitive. On Nj it suffices to check freeness on S j = A0 /⟨α⟩, α( x ) = A j x + v j /m j : the image
of x is fixed by tδ̂ iff
                                                       v
                              ( Akj − I ) x + ∑i<k Aij mjj + tδ̂ ∈ Λ for some 0 ≤ k < m j .
Applying the A j -invariant form γ, which kills δ̂, gives k ℓ j /m j ∈ Z; as gcd(ℓ j , m j ) = 1 by admissibility this
forces k = 0, and then tδ̂ ∈ Λ, i.e. t = 0. The same computation applies on the nearby fibres of Nj , so the
action is smooth and free on X ◦ , which is a compact manifold with boundary M.
    Finally, the inclusion i : F ,→ X ◦ of a smooth fibre is S1 -equivariant, and both a∗ and the slant product
are natural, so i∗ (ιA) = ι F (i∗ A), where ι F = p∗F p F! is the corresponding operator for the translation action
on F. On the torus F = (Λ ⊗ R)/Λ this action is that of the subcircle Rδ̂/Zδ̂; p F : F → F/Sδ̂1 is a torus
quotient, H ∗ ( F ) = ∗ V is computed by translation-invariant forms, p∗F is the inclusion of ∗ (δ̂⊥ ), and
                       V                                                                               V

integration of an invariant form over the (oriented) orbit circles is contraction with δ̂. Hence ι F = p∗F p F! = ι δ̂
on H ∗ ( F ). For the fixed points of the finite subgroups µn ⊂ Sδ̂1 see Remark 9.25.

Remark 7.28. Both routes single out the same arithmetic: p ≡ −(4ℓ1 + 3ℓ2 ) (12) is local data at p1 , p2
(admissibility being exactly smoothness of X at the multiple fibres), while ℓ0 is the free gluing parameter at
p0 ; thus | p| = 1 iff (ℓ1 mod 3, ℓ2 mod 4) ∈ {(1, 3), (2, 1)}, realised by (0, 1, −1) and not by (0, 1, 1).

Corollary 7.29. For the threefold X of §6 (v1 = ε, v2 = −ε′ , tautological gluing at p0 ) one has (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) =

                       3

oriented 6-manifold X, H4 ( X ) ∼      = H 2 ( X ) = 0 and H5 ( X ) ∼= H 1 ( X ) = 0; the rank of H3 ( X ) is that of

8   Recognition: X is diffeomorphic to S6 .
Throughout this section X denotes the compact complex threefold constructed in §6, regarded as a closed
smooth 6-manifold and oriented by its complex structure.

Theorem 8.1. X is diffeomorphic to S6 .
                                                                             e ∗ ( M; Z) ∼
Lemma 8.2. Let M be a closed smooth simply connected n-manifold, n ≥ 2, with H           =He ∗ ( S n ; Z).
Then M is homotopy equivalent to Sn .

Proof. By the Hurewicz theorem [Hat02, Thm. 4.32], applied inductively in degrees 2, . . . , n, one gets
                                                       ∼
                                                       → Hn ( M; Z) ∼
πi ( M ) = 0 for i ≤ n − 1 and an isomorphism πn ( M ) −            = Z. Let φ : Sn → M represent a class
mapping to a generator. By naturality of the Hurewicz map φ∗ is an isomorphism on Hn , hence (the
remaining reduced groups vanishing) on all integral homology. Both spaces are simply connected and
of the homotopy type of CW complexes: this is clear for Sn , and for the closed topological manifold M it
is [Hat02, Cor. A.12]. Hence φ is a homotopy equivalence by the homology Whitehead theorem [Hat02,
Cor. 4.33].

                                                         63
Proof of Theorem 8.1. By Theorem 6.2, X is a compact complex manifold of dimension 3, so its underlying
smooth manifold is closed of real dimension 6; by Theorem 7.17 and Corollary 7.29 (equivalently The-
orem 7.22), π1 ( X ) = 1 and H∗ ( X; Z) ∼= H∗ (S6 ; Z). Lemma 8.2 with n = 6 shows that X is a homotopy
6-sphere. By Smale’s generalised Poincaré theorem in dimensions ≥ 5 [Sma61, Thm. A], X is homeomor-
phic to S6 and is a twisted sphere. By the h-cobordism theorem [Mil65], for n ≥ 5 the h-cobordism classes of
oriented homotopy n-spheres coincide with their oriented diffeomorphism classes, and by Kervaire–Milnor
[KM63, Lem. 4.5, Thm. 1.1] these classes form a finite abelian group Θn under connected sum, with Θ6 = 0
[KM63, §7]. Hence X is orientation-preservingly diffeomorphic to S6 with one of its two orientations; as S6
carries an orientation-reversing self-diffeomorphism (a reflection of R7 ), X is diffeomorphic to S6 , and the
diffeomorphism may be taken orientation-preserving for the complex orientation.

Remark 8.3. The proof uses only π1 ( X ) = 1 (Theorem 7.17) and H∗ ( X; Z) ∼  = H∗ (S6 ; Z) (Corollary 7.29
or Theorem 7.22); H∗ ( X; Z) is computed twice in §7: once by the Mayer–Vietoris route, which uses the
retraction and collapse of §7.2, and once by the Leray route of §7.7 with Appendix B, which together with
Theorem 7.17 and Corollary 4.8 does not use §7.2; the recognition of X as S6 therefore does not depend on
Proposition 7.2.
Remark 8.4. It is the diffeomorphism, not merely the homeomorphism of [Sma61], that is needed for the
statement of §1: transporting the complex structure of X through any diffeomorphism X → S6 yields an
integrable almost complex structure on the standard smooth S6 whose complex manifold is biholomorphic
to X.

Corollary 8.5. e( X ) = 2 and c3 ( X ) = ⟨c3 ( TX ), [ X ]⟩ = 2, where [ X ] is the fundamental class for the complex
orientation.

Proof. From Corollary 7.29 (or Theorem 7.22), e( X ) = ∑(−1)i bi ( X ) = 2; equivalently e( X ) = e(W ) + 0 + 0
as in §7, the multiple fibres 3S1 , 4S2 contributing e(S j ) = 0 (S j bielliptic, Theorem 5.4) and the remaining
fibres of f being 2-tori. For a compact complex n-fold, cn ( TX ) is the Euler class of the underlying oriented
real tangent bundle, so ⟨cn ( TX ), [ X ]⟩ = e( X ) [Hir, §4.10]; take n = 3.

Remark 8.6. In particular c3 ( X ) ̸= 0, and this non-vanishing is what rules out a( X ) = 2 in §9, while the
vanishing b2 ( X ) = 0 obtained in §7 rules out a( X ) = 3.


9     Complex-analytic invariants of X.
Throughout, f : X → B = P1 is the map of §6 and the notation is that of §2–§5: Σ = { p0 , p1 , p2 },
B◦ = B ∖ Σ, Fb = f −1 (b) = C2 /Π(z)Λ for b = π (z) ∈ B◦ , W = f −1 ( p0 ), f ∗ p j = m j S j with S j bielliptic.
Write σ1 := 6µγ + τu + w, σ2 := βγ + µu + δ for the rows of Π = [ Z | I ], viewed in V ⊗ C = Hom(Λ, C),
so σi (λ) = (Πλ)i and
                           F1 (z) := ⟨σ1 (z), σ2 (z)⟩ = H 0 (Ω1Fπ(z) ) ⊂ H 1 ( Fπ (z) , C).
Put e := dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 , the flat relative 2-form on T . As usual M(Y ) denotes the field of meromorphic functions
of a compact complex space Y, and a(Y ) = trdegC M(Y ).

Theorem 9.1. Let X and f be as in §6. Then:

    (i) a( X ) = 1, M( X ) = f ∗ M( B), f is the algebraic reduction of X, and the very general fibre of f has
        algebraic dimension 0;

    (ii) f ∗ O X = O B , R1 f ∗ O X ∼
                                    = O ⊕ O(−1), R2 f ∗ O X ∼
                                                            = O(−1); hence h0,0 = h0,1 = 1, h0,2 = h0,3 = 0;
 (iii) f ∗ ωX/B ∼
                = O B (1) and KX ∼
                                 = f ∗ O(−1) ⊗ O(2S2 ); in particular KX is not a torsion line bundle;
 (iv) c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = 0 in H ∗ ( X; Z); c1 c2 = 0, c3 = e( X ) = 2, χ(O X ) = 0, χ(Ω1X ) = −1, χ( TX ) = 1;

     (a smooth rational curve through both triple points) and normal weights (+1, −1);
                  p
 (vi) H 0 ( X, Ω X ) = 0 for p = 1, 2, 3, i.e.
                                                 h1,0 = h2,0 = h3,0 = 0
      (the case p = 3 being consistent with h0,3 = 0 in (ii) by Serre duality); together with (ii), (iv) and Serre
      duality this determines every Hodge number of X in terms of the single integer h1,2 = h2,1 , namely
      h1,1 = h2,2 = h1,2 + 1 and h p,0 = h0,q = 0 for p ≥ 1, q ≥ 2.
  Parts (i)–(vi) are proved in §§9.1–9.7. The Hodge number h1,2 = h2,1 is not computed here; see
Remark 9.21.

Lemma 9.3 (linear independence of the period functions). If a, b, c, d, e ∈ C satisfy
                                  a + b τ + c µ + d β + e (6µ2 − τβ) ≡ 0         on hz ,

Proof. By (9.1),
                      (6µ2 − τβ) ◦ g0 = 6µ2 − (τ − 1)( β + 1) = (6µ2 − τβ) + ( β − τ + 1).

Lemma 9.4 (η is an indefinite (1, 1)-class). For every z ∈ h◦z one has η ∈ NS( Fπ (z) ), η 2 = 12, and the

Proposition 9.5. The set
                                         C := {z ∈ h◦z : NS( Fπ (z) ) ̸= Zη }
is countable, and a( Fπ (z) ) = 0 for every z ∈ h◦z ∖ C .

Proof. By Lemmas 9.2 and 9.4, Zη ⊆ NS( Fπ (z) ) for every z, and
                                                                  {z ∈ h◦z : PE (z) = 0}.
                                                      [
                                           C=
                                                          V ∖Zη
                                                     V2
                                                E∈
For E ∈/ Zη the holomorphic function PE is not identically zero on the connected domain hz (Lemma 9.3),
so its zero set is discrete, hence countable; and 2 V ∼    = Z6 is countable. Thus C is countable.
                                                    V

    Let now z ∈ / C , put T := Fπ (z) , and suppose a( T ) ≥ 1. By the structure theory of the algebraic reduction
of a complex torus ([LB99, Ch. 2]; see also [BHPV, Ch. IV,VI]) there is a subtorus T1 ⊂ T such that
p : T → Ta := T/T1 is the algebraic reduction and Ta is an abelian variety of dimension a( T ).

      • If a( T ) = 2 then T = Ta is an abelian surface; let h ∈ NS( T ) be the class of an ample line bundle.
        Then Hh is positive definite, whereas h = nη for some n ̸= 0 and Hnη is indefinite by Lemma 9.4:
        contradiction.

      • If a( T ) = 1 then Ta is an elliptic curve and T1 is a 1-dimensional subtorus; the class φ := p∗ [pt] ∈
        NS( T ) of a fibre satisfies φ2 = 0. Moreover φ is the Poincaré dual of the fibre class [ T1 ] ∈ H2 ( T; Z),
        so for a flat Kähler form ω on T one has  Z           Z
                                                               φ∧ω =            ω > 0,
                                                           T               T1
        whence φ ̸= 0. But φ = nη with n ̸= 0 forces φ2 = 12n2 ̸= 0 (Lemma 9.4): contradiction.

Hence a( T ) = 0.

Lemma 9.6. Let g ∈ M( X ) and suppose that g| Fb is constant for uncountably many b ∈ B◦ . Then g = f ∗ φ
for a (unique) φ ∈ M( B) = C(t).

Proof. Let | P| ⊂ X be the polar divisor of g (a hypersurface containing the indeterminacy locus of g) and
put
                                             Ω : = f −1 ( B ◦ ) ∖ | P | ,


                                                                     67
a connected open subset of X on which g is holomorphic and f is a submersion. Only finitely many fibres
are contained in | P| (each is then a union of irreducible components of | P|); for every other b ∈ B◦ the
set Fb ∩ Ω is a connected 2-dimensional closed submanifold of Ω, dense in Fb , and g| Fb is a well-defined
meromorphic function on Fb .
     Let
                                     Z := { x ∈ Ω : (dg ∧ f ∗ dt)( x ) = 0} ⊂ Ω,
an analytic subset, where t denotes a local holomorphic coordinate on B (the condition is independent of
the choice). If g| Fb is constant then Fb ∩ Ω ⊂ Z.
    Assume Z ̸= Ω. Then Z is a proper analytic subset of the connected 3-fold Ω, so each of its irreducible
components has dimension ≤ 2, and there are at most countably many components (Ω is second countable).
Each Fb ∩ Ω contained in Z is an irreducible closed analytic subset of Z of dimension 2, hence is contained
in, and therefore equal to, one of the (finitely or countably many) 2-dimensional components; distinct b
give disjoint, hence distinct, components. So only countably many b would have g| Fb constant, contrary to
hypothesis.
     Hence Z = Ω, i.e. dg annihilates ker d f at every point of Ω; as the sets Fb ∩ Ω are connected, g is
constant on each of them, and g = φ ◦ f on Ω with φ a function on B◦ minus the finite set of the bad b’s.
The function φ is holomorphic there: locally φ = g ◦ s for a holomorphic local section s of the submersion
f |Ω .
     It remains to see that φ is meromorphic at each of the finitely many remaining points t0 ∈ B. Choose
x ∈ f −1 (t0 ) and local coordinates centred at x; since the germs of f −1 (t0 ) and of | P| at x are hypersurface
germs, there is a holomorphic disc κ : (∆, 0) → ( X, x ) with κ (∆) ̸⊂ | P| and f ◦ κ non-constant, say f ◦ κ (s) =
t0 + sk · (unit) in a local coordinate at t0 . Then g ◦ κ is meromorphic on ∆, and g ◦ κ = φ ◦ ( f ◦ κ ) off a
discrete subset of ∆. Consequently | g ◦ κ (s)| ≤ C |s|− M near 0 for some M, whence | φ(t)| ≤ C ′ |t − t0 |− M/k
on a punctured neighbourhood of t0 ; so (t − t0 ) N φ is bounded for N ≫ 0 and extends holomorphically by
Riemann’s theorem, i.e. φ is meromorphic at t0 . Thus φ ∈ M( B) = C(t) and g = f ∗ φ on the dense open
set Ω, hence everywhere.

Proposition 9.7. a( X ) = 1, M( X ) = f ∗ M( B), and f is the algebraic reduction of X.

Proof. Let g ∈ M( X ) with polar divisor | P|. By Proposition 9.5 the set π (C) ⊂ B◦ is countable, and only
finitely many fibres are contained in | P|; since B is uncountable, there are uncountably many b ∈ B◦ with
                                           a( Fb ) = 0   and    Fb ̸⊂ | P|.
For such b the restriction g| Fb is a meromorphic function on the compact connected torus Fb , hence constant.
By Lemma 9.6, g ∈ f ∗ M( B). Therefore
                                           M( X ) = f ∗ M( B) ∼
                                                              = C( t ),
the pullback f ∗ being injective because f is surjective; in particular a( X ) = trdegC C(t) = 1. Finally, f is a
surjective holomorphic map onto the projective curve B with connected fibres and f ∗ M( B) = M( X ), i.e.
f is the algebraic reduction of X.

Corollary 9.8. The set of b ∈ B for which Fb is not a smooth fibre with NS( Fb ) = Zη is contained in a
countable union of proper analytic subsets of B (i.e. is countable); for all other b,
                                        NS( Fb ) = Zη     and    a( Fb ) = 0.
In particular the very general fibre of the algebraic reduction f has algebraic dimension 0, and carries no
non-constant meromorphic function.

Proof. Immediate from Proposition 9.5: the exceptional set is Σ ∪ π (C), a countable subset of the curve B,
and proper analytic subsets of B are exactly the finite sets.
   The statement is independent of the marking used to identify H 2 ( Fb ; Z) with 2 V: the class η is
                                                                                      V

invariant under the monodromy group of the family over B◦ (Lemma A.1(6) of the Appendix; equivalently,
the monodromy-invariance lemma of §2), so the subgroup Zη ⊂ H 2 ( Fb ; Z), and hence the equality
NS( Fb ) = Zη, does not depend on the chosen marking.

                                                         68
Remark 9.9 (second proof that a( X ) = 1). We record the route through [CDP20]. It yields a( X ) = 1, and
hence also M( X ) = f ∗ M( B): indeed M( X ) then has transcendence degree 1 over C, so it is algebraic
over the subfield f ∗ M( B), which is algebraically closed in M( X ) because f is proper and surjective
with connected fibres onto the normal curve B [Ue75, §3]; whence M( X ) = f ∗ M( B). It does not yield
Corollary 9.8, and it uses the topological computations of §7 and §9.4.
    (a) a( X ) ̸= 3. A Moishezon threefold has b2 > 0 [CDP98, Lemma 1.4], while b2 ( X ) = 0 by §7.
    (b) If a(Y ) ≥ 2 for a compact connected complex manifold Y, then some non-constant h ∈ M(Y ) is not
holomorphic as a map Y 99K P1 . Choose algebraically independent g1 , g2 ∈ M(Y ). If one of them is already
non-holomorphic, we are done; otherwise Φ := ( g1 , g2 ) : Y → P1 × P1 is holomorphic, and Φ(Y ) is an
irreducible compact analytic subset (Remmert) of dimension 2 — a point or a curve (algebraic by Chow)
would give an algebraic relation between g1 and g2 — so Φ is surjective. By upper semicontinuity of
fibre dimension, S := {q : dim Φ−1 (q) ≥ 2} is a proper analytic subset, and it is finite: if it contained a
curve Γ then dim Φ−1 (Γ) ≥ 3 = dim Y, so Φ(Y ) ⊂ Γ. Pick ( a1 , a2 ) ∈ C2 ∖ S and x ∈ Φ−1 ( a1 , a2 ), and put
u := g1 − a1 , v := g2 − a2 ∈ OY,x : both vanish at x, and they are coprime in the factorial ring OY,x , since a
common prime factor p would give a hypersurface germ { p = 0} ⊂ (Φ−1 ( a1 , a2 ), x ), which has dimension
≤ 1. Then
                                                  1          1       u+v
                                          h :=          +          =
                                               g1 − a 1   g2 − a 2    uv
is a reduced fraction at x (a prime dividing uv divides u or v, and if it also divided u + v it would divide
both), with both u + v and uv vanishing at x; hence x is an indeterminacy point of h. (The choice ( a1 , a2 ) ∈
                                                                                                              /S
is essential: if the level sets shared a component with different multiplicities, such a sum could well be
holomorphic there.)
    (c) a( X ) ̸= 2. If a( X ) = 2, then by (b) there is a non-constant meromorphic, non-holomorphic map
X 99K P1 , so the hypotheses of [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] are met. We quote that theorem verbatim [CDP20,
Thm. 2.1, p. 679] (in it, B denotes a holomorphic vector bundle, not our base curve, and g is the meromorphic
map, not a generator of ∆):

      2.1. Theorem. Let X be a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold with b2 ( X ) = 0. Assume that there
      exists a non-holomorphic meromorphic non-constant map g : X 99K P1 . Let B be a holomorphic vector
      bundle on X. Then
      a) H i ( X, B ⊗ M ) = 0 for i ≥ 0 and M ∈ Pic◦ ( X ) generic.
      b) χ( X, B ⊗ M ) = 0 for all M ∈ Pic◦ ( X ).
      c) c3 ( X ) = 0; i.e., either b1 ( X ) = 0 and b3 ( X ) = 2, or b1 ( X ) = 1 and b3 ( X ) = 0.

followed there by “Another proof has been given in [LSS18].” (loc. cit.’s [LSS18] is our [LRS]). Our X has
b2 ( X ) = 0 (§7), so part c) would give c3 ( X ) = 0, contradicting c3 ( X ) = e( X ) = 2 (§9.4). (See also [LRS,
Thm. 3.1].) With (a) and f ∗ M( B) ⊂ M( X ) this gives a( X ) = 1.
     (d) A related holomorphic statement. Independently of the above,
           if h : X → P1 is holomorphic, then h = φ ◦ f for a (unique) holomorphic φ : B → P1 .              (9.3)
Indeed, L := h∗ OP1 (1) has c1 ( L ) = 0 because H ( X; Z) = 0 (§7); if h | Fb were non-constant for some b ∈ B ,
                                                  2                                                            ◦
                                                                                                          ∼
it would be surjective and D := (h| Fb ) (∞) would be a non-empty effective divisor with O Fb ( D ) = L| Fb .
                                          − 1

On a compact complex torus a line bundle with vanishing first Chern class is given by a unitary character
of the lattice (Appell–Humbert, [BL04, Ch. 2]), and its sections are the entire functions θ on C2 with
θ (ζ + ℓ) = χ(ℓ)θ (ζ ), which are bounded, hence constant, and non-zero only for χ ≡ 1; so O Fb ( D ) ∼= O Fb
                                                                                  ◦
with D ≥ 0 forces D = 0, a contradiction. Thus h is constant on the fibres over B and factors through the
proper map f with connected fibres onto the normal curve B [Ue75, §3].
     (e) Caveat. X contradicts [CDP20, Cor. 2.3], the failure being located in [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] (see §10);
using [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] is legitimate only insofar as its proof is independent of that step (it is, and [LRS]
reproves it). The proof of Proposition 9.7 uses neither §7 nor [CDP20].

Proposition 9.11. K := f ∗ ωX/B is invertible of degree 1. The multivalued frame e of K| B◦ has automorphy

or I1∗ (n = ord p0 ( jY )∞ = 1); and the fibres over p1 , p2 are the ones attached to the elliptic points ρ, i with

these are precisely the numbers 1 at the cusp and 12a j /m j = 8, 3 at the two elliptic points.

Proposition 9.13. f ∗ O X = O B , R1 f ∗ O X ∼ = O ⊕ O(−1), R2 f ∗ O X ∼
                                                                       = O(−1). Consequently h0,0 = h0,1 = 1,

Proof. f is proper with connected fibres onto the normal curve B, so f ∗ O X = O B .
   Step 0: flatness, and the topological input. The map f is flat. Indeed, X is a connected complex manifold, B
a smooth curve and f non-constant, hence open; equivalently, for each x ∈ X no non-zero germ in O B, f (x)
vanishes identically near x, so O X,x is torsion free — and therefore flat — over the discrete valuation
ring O B, f (x) . The same applies to the restrictions f 0 : N0 → D0 and f | Nj : Nj → D j . Moreover the scheme-
theoretic fibre of f 0 over p0 is the reduced divisor W: all rays of F lie at height 1, so t−1 (0) = ∑v Dv is
reduced (Lemma 9.10(ii)), hence so is its quotient W.
   The following topological fact will be used:

  (T) The inclusion W ,→ N0 induces an isomorphism on fundamental groups, both equal to Λ̄: N0 = Yε /Γ
      with π1 (Yε ) = 1 (Corollary 4.8), and W = t−1 (0)/Γ with t−1 (0) simply connected, being a deformation
      retract of a tube {|t| < ρ} ⊂ Y (Lemma A.2(6), a statement upstairs) which is simply connected by the
      argument of Corollary 4.8; Γ acts freely on both and the inclusion is Γ-equivariant. Hence restriction
      H 1 ( N0 ; C) → H 1 (W; C) is an isomorphism, both being Hom(Λ̄, C). Moreover, by Proposition 7.12,
      for b ∈ D0∗ the restriction
                                                           sp1
                                        H 1 (W; C) −−→ H 1 ( Fb ; C) = V ⊗ C                           (9.7)
      is injective with image V T0 ⊗ C = Cγ ⊕ Cu, carrying the basis γ, u of H 1 (W; C) of Lemma 9.12(iv) to
      γ, u ∈ V.

    Step 1: local freeness and base change. Over B◦ the fibres are complex 2-tori with hq (O Fb ) = (1, 2, 1). Near
p0 the fibre of the proper flat map f 0 over p0 is the reduced surface W, again with hq (OW ) = (1, 2, 1) by
Lemma 9.12(ii). Hence b 7→ hq ( f −1 (b), O) is constant on B◦ ∪ { p0 } for every q, and Grauert’s theorem
[GrRe], [BaSt, III.§3] applies there: Rq f ∗ O X is locally free of rank 1, 2, 1 on B◦ ∪ { p0 } and every base-change
map
                                Rq f ∗ O X ⊗ k (b) −→ H q ( f −1 (b), O),    q = 0, 1, 2,
is an isomorphism, compatibly with cup products. Near p j the scheme fibre is the non-reduced m j S j and we
                                            log
argue instead on the cover: Nj = J ′ /⟨ g̃ ⟩ with f˜ : J ′ → ∆ j smooth and proper, and taking Z/m j -invariants

1, 2, 1. Thus all Rq f ∗ O X are locally free on B, of ranks 1, 2, 1; write G := R1 f ∗ O X .
     Step 2: R2 f ∗ O X and deg G. Relative duality [RRV] gives R2 f ∗ O X ∼  = K−1 ∼ = O(−1) by Proposition 9.11.
The hypotheses are met: f is flat (Step 0) and proper, its fibres — including the non-reduced ones m j S j
— are Gorenstein, since X is smooth and f flat, so that ωX/B = KX ⊗ f ∗ K B−1 is invertible and is a relative
dualising sheaf, and the sheaves Rq f ∗ O X are locally free by Step 1; hence relative duality applies. We claim
that the relative cup product Λ2 G → R2 f ∗ O X is an isomorphism; as both sides are line bundles it suffices
to check non-vanishing at each point of B.
     Over B◦ this is, by the base change of Step 1, the isomorphism Λ2 H 1 (O Fb ) ∼         = H 2 (O Fb ) on a complex

   At p j we work on the cover. Linearise the action of the cyclic group Z/m j on the free O(∆ j )-module

ri ∈ {0, . . . , m j − 1} such that νi ζ jri = 1; likewise the invariants of Λ2 are generated by sr (v1 ∧ v2 ) with

9.4   Chern numbers and Riemann–Roch
By §7 we have H 2 ( X; Z) = 0 and H 4 ( X; Z) = 0, so c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = 0 integrally, and every Chern number
involving c1 or c2 vanishes; Gauss–Bonnet and §7 give
                                 c3 ( X ) = e( X ) = e(W ) + e(3S1 ) + e(4S2 ) = 2 + 0 + 0.
                                                             1
Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch [Hir], [AS] gives χ(O X ) = 24         c1 c2 = 0, matching 1 − 1 + 0 − 0 = 0 from
Proposition 9.13; for TX one has ch( TX ) = 3 + 2 c3 , td( X ) = 1, so χ( TX ) = 12 c3 = 1. By Serre duality
                                                 1
                                                                           p
χ(Ω3X ) = −χ(O X ) = 0 and χ(Ω2X ) = −χ(Ω1X ), so 2 = c3 = ∑ p (−1) p χ(Ω X ) = −2χ(Ω1X ), i.e. χ(Ω1X ) = −1.
This proves Theorem 9.1(iv).

9.5   Holomorphic forms on X
In this subsection we prove Theorem 9.1(vi):
                                                        p
                                             H 0 ( X, Ω X ) = 0 ( p = 1, 2, 3).
The method is elementary and explicit: a holomorphic form on X is pulled back to the three uniformisations
of §6 — the cover hz × C2 of J, the étale covers Jj′ → Nj of §5, and the toric cover Yε → N0 of §4 — where its
coefficients become functions of z alone, subject to an automorphy law under ∆ and to vanishing conditions
at the three points of Σ; the resulting objects are sections of negative line bundles on P1 .
     Convention. Throughout §9.5 a dot denotes d/dz on hz (so τ̇ = dτ/dz, Π̇ = dΠ/dz, ġ(z) the derivative
of the Möbius transformation g ∈ ∆); primes are reserved for derivatives in the base coordinates tc , t j . We
write ζ = (ζ 1 , ζ 2 ) for the fibre coordinates on hz × C2 , c = (c1 , c2 ) and b = (b1 , b2 ) for row vectors, so that
c · dζ = c1 dζ 1 + c2 dζ 2 , and
                      Tλ (z, ζ ) := (z, ζ + Π(z)λ) (λ ∈ Λ),      g̃(z, ζ ) := ( gz, R g (z)ζ ) ( g ∈ ∆),
so that T |U = (U × C )/Λ for U ⊆ hz open, and J = (T |h◦z )/∆.

Step 1: normal form of invariant forms on the covers
Lemma 9.15. Let U ⊆ hz be open and connected and let θ be a holomorphic p-form on T |U , p ∈ {1, 2, 3};
denote again by θ its pullback to U × C2 . We use the following two facts established earlier: L(z) = Π(z)Λ
is a lattice of full rank in C2 for every z ∈ hz (Theorem 3.4), and τ̇ ̸= 0 on h◦z (Lemma 9.14(iii)). Then there
are holomorphic functions on U such that
                                                                   
             (a) θ = a(z) dz + c(z) · dζ,    (b) θ = dz ∧ b(z) · dζ ,      (c) θ = c(z) dz ∧ dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2
for p = 1, 2, 3 respectively; moreover in case (a) one has c(z) Ż (z) = 0 for all z ∈ U.
    If in addition U is invariant under a subgroup ∆′ ⊆ ∆ and θ is invariant under g̃ for all g ∈ ∆′ , then for
g ∈ ∆′ :
                       (a)   c( gz) R g (z) = c(z),         a( gz) ġ(z) = a(z),   c( gz) Ṙ g (z) = 0;              (9.8)
                      (b)    b( gz) R g (z) ġ(z) = b(z);                                                            (9.9)
                       (c)   c( gz) ġ(z) det R g (z) = c(z).                                                       (9.10)

Proof. We have Tλ∗ dz = dz and Tλ∗ dζ = dζ + Π̇(z)λ dz.
    (a) Write θ = a(z, ζ )dz + c(z, ζ ) · dζ. Then Tλ∗ θ = a ◦ Tλ + (c ◦ Tλ )Π̇λ dz + (c ◦ Tλ ) · dζ, so Tλ -invariance
                                                                                

for all λ ∈ Λ gives first c(z, ζ + Π(z)λ) = c(z, ζ ); for fixed z the lattice L(z) = Π(z)Λ ⊂ C2 is of full rank
(Theorem 3.4), so the entire functions ci (z, ·) are constant: c = c(z). Secondly
                               a(z, ζ + Π(z)λ) − a(z, ζ ) = −c(z)Π̇(z)λ               ( λ ∈ Λ ),                     (9.11)
whose right-hand side does not depend on ζ; hence each ∂a/∂ζ i is L(z)-periodic, so constant in ζ, and
a(z, ζ ) = a(z) + K (z) · ζ with a, K holomorphic on U. Then (9.11) reads
                                        K (z) Π(z)λ = −c(z)Π̇(z)λ             ( λ ∈ Λ ),
an identity between R-linear functions of λ, hence valid for λ ∈ Λ ⊗ R. Restrict to Λtor ⊗ R: there Π(z)λ
runs through R2 ⊂ C2 and Π̇(z)λ = 0, because Π = [ Z | I ] and Λtor = ⟨ŵ, δ̂⟩. Thus the C-linear form K (z)
vanishes on R2 , so K ≡ 0; feeding this back, c(z)Π̇(z)λ = 0 for all λ ∈ Λ, i.e. c(z) Ż (z) = 0.
    (b) Write θ = a(z, ζ )dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 + dz ∧ (b(z, ζ ) · dζ ). With p := Π̇(z)λ one has Tλ∗ (dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 ) = dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 +
dz ∧ (− p2 dζ 1 + p1 dζ 2 ), so invariance gives a ◦ Tλ = a, hence a = a(z) as in (a), and
                                      b(z, ζ + Π(z)λ) − b(z, ζ ) = − a(z) (− p2 , p1 ).
Exactly as above b is affine in ζ with vanishing linear part (restrict to Λtor ⊗ R, where p = 0), and then
a(z) Ż (z) = 0. By Lemma 9.14(iii), τ̇ ̸= 0 off a discrete set, so a ≡ 0 and θ = dz ∧ (b(z) · dζ ).
    (c) Tλ∗ (dz ∧ dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 ) = dz ∧ dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 , so the coefficient is L(z)-periodic, hence a function of z.
    Finally g̃∗ dz = ġ(z)dz and g̃∗ dζ = R g (z)dζ + Ṙ g (z)ζ dz. In case (a),

                                                                       

and comparison of the dζ-part, and of the dζ-free part for all ζ, gives (9.8). In case (b), g̃∗ θ = ġ(z) dz ∧
 b( gz) R g (z) · dζ , giving (9.9); in case (c), g̃∗ θ = c( gz) ġ(z) det R g (z) dz ∧ dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 , giving (9.10).

Step 3: descent through the elliptic points, and two vanishing lemmas
Lemma 9.17. Let j ∈ {1, 2}, m = m j , ζ = ζ j , and let s = s j be the linearising coordinate on ∆ j (s ◦ g j = ζs,
t j ◦ π = sm ).
(D0) If C is holomorphic on ∆ j with C ◦ g j = C, then C is a holomorphic function of t j .
(D1) If α = A(s) ds is a holomorphic 1-form on ∆ j with g∗j α = α, then A = ∑n≡−1 (m) An sn and α =
               Alm−1 l −1
      ∑ l ≥1     m tj     dt j ; in particular α is the pullback of a holomorphic 1-form on D j .

(D2) If κ = C (s) ds⊗3 is a holomorphic cubic differential on ∆ j with g∗j κ = κ, then C = ∑n≡−3 (m) Cn sn and
     κ is the pullback of a meromorphic cubic differential on D j with a pole of order at most 2 at p j .
Proof. g∗j ds = ζ ds, so the three invariance conditions read C (ζs) = C (s), A(ζs)ζ = A(s), C (ζs)ζ 3 = C (s);
comparing Taylor coefficients gives n ≡ 0, n ≡ −1, n ≡ −3 (mod m) respectively. With t = sm , dt =
msm−1 ds: for (D1), slm−1 ds = tl −1 dt/m; for (D2) with m = 3 and n = 3l, s3l ds⊗3 = tl dt⊗3 /(27s6 ) =
tl −2 dt⊗3 /27; with m = 4 and n = 4l + 1, s4l +1 ds⊗3 = s4l +1 dt⊗3 /(64s9 ) = tl −2 dt⊗3 /64. In both cases l ≥ 0,
so the order at p j is ≥ −2.

Lemma 9.18 (weight 0). Let α be a ∆-invariant holomorphic 1-form on hz such that, on Ur , α = ã(s) ds with

Step 4: the vanishing theorem
                              p
Proposition 9.20. H 0 ( X, Ω X ) = 0 for p = 1, 2, 3; that is, h1,0 = h2,0 = h3,0 = 0.

Proof. In each case we show that the pullback of the form to h◦z × C2 vanishes; since J | B◦ is dense in the
connected manifold X, the form itself is then 0.
     p = 1. Let ω ∈ H 0 ( X, Ω1X ), with coefficients a, c as in Lemmas 9.15 and 9.16; thus c is holomorphic on
hz , c( gz) R g (z) = c(z), c(z) Ż (z) = 0, and c = O(tc ) at the cusp.
     By Lemma 9.14(i) the second component of c( gz) R g (z) = c(z) reads c2 ◦ g = c2 : the function c2 is
∆-invariant and holomorphic on hz . By (D0) it is a holomorphic function of t j near p j (j = 1, 2), and it
is a holomorphic function of t on B◦ ; hence it descends to a holomorphic function on B ∖ { p0 } ∼             = C. By
Lemma 9.16(ii) it extends holomorphically across p0 with value 0 there. A global holomorphic function on
P1 is constant, so c2 ≡ 0.
     The second column of c Ż = 0 now gives c1 τ̇ + c2 µ̇ = c1 τ̇ = 0, and τ̇ ̸= 0 on h◦z , so c1 ≡ 0 on h◦z and
hence on hz . Thus c ≡ 0 and ω = a(z) dz upstairs, with a holomorphic on all of hz by Lemma 9.16(i) and
a( gz) ġ(z) = a(z) on hz by the same lemma; i.e. α := a dz is a ∆-invariant holomorphic 1-form on hz , and
ã = O(tc ) at the cusp by Lemma 9.16(ii). Lemma 9.18 gives α = 0, i.e. ω = 0.
     p = 2. Let η ∈ H 0 ( X, Ω2X ); upstairs η = dz ∧ (b(z) · dζ ) with b holomorphic on hz (Lemmas 9.15, 9.16)
and b( gz) R g (z) ġ(z) = b(z).
     The second component of this relation is b2 ( gz) ġ(z) = b2 (z) (Lemma 9.14(i)), i.e. α := b2 dz is a
∆-invariant holomorphic 1-form on hz ; by Lemma 9.16(ii) its cusp coefficient b̃2 is O(tc ). Lemma 9.18 gives
b2 ≡ 0.
     The first component then reads b1 ( gz)r g (z) ġ(z) = b1 (z), i.e. ψ := b1 dz satisfies g∗ ψ = ȷg ψ for all g ∈ ∆;
and b̃1 = O(tc ) at the cusp. Lemma 9.19 gives ψ = 0, so b ≡ 0 and η = 0.
     p = 3. Let Ω ∈ H 0 ( X, Ω3X ); upstairs Ω = c(z) dz ∧ dζ 1 ∧ dζ 2 with c holomorphic on hz and, by (9.10)
and det R g = r g ,
                            c( gz) ġ(z) r g (z) = c(z),   i.e.    g∗ ψ = ȷg ψ for ψ := c dz.
By Lemma 9.16(ii), c̃ = O(tc ) at the cusp, so Lemma 9.19 gives ψ = 0 and Ω = 0.

   This proves Theorem 9.1(vi). For p = 3 the vanishing agrees with h3,0 = h0,3 = 0 from Proposition 9.13
and Serre duality (equivalently, with the non-effectivity of KX = f ∗ O(−1) ⊗ O(2S2 ), Proposition 9.11); the
cases p = 1, 2 are new. The same arguments apply verbatim to the variant X ′ of §6.

9.6   The remaining Hodge numbers
Remark 9.21 (What is proved, and the expected values). Combining Proposition 9.13, Proposition 9.20,
χ(Ω1X ) = −1, χ(Ω2X ) = 1 (§9.4) and Serre duality h p,q = h3− p,3−q , the Hodge numbers of X are
                     h0,0 = h3,3 = h0,1 = h3,2 = 1,      h p,0 = 0 ( p ≥ 1),   h0,q = 0 (q ≥ 2),
hence also h3,q = 0 for q ≤ 1 and h p,3 = 0 for p ≤ 2; and
                             χ(Ω1X ) = h1,0 − h1,1 + h1,2 − h1,3 = −h1,1 + h1,2 = −1,
                           χ(Ω2X ) = h2,0 − h2,1 + h2,2 − h2,3 = −h2,1 + h2,2 = 1,
the two relations being equivalent under Serre duality. Thus every Hodge number of X is determined by
the single integer
                            h1,2 = h2,1 ≥ 0,      via      h1,1 = h2,2 = h1,2 + 1.
The remaining Hodge numbers are determined by h1,2 , which we do not compute; the expected value is
                                 h1,2 = h2,1 = 1,     whence        h1,1 = h2,2 = 2.
(Equivalently: dimC E1 of the Frölicher spectral sequence equals 6 + 4h1,2 , expected 10.) A proof of
h1,2 = 1 would require the direct images Rq f ∗ Ω1X (log D ), D := W + S1 + S2 , equivalently those of
E := Ω1X (log D )/ f ∗ Ω1B (log Σ), at the three singular fibres, where Ω1X/B has torsion along W ∪ S1 ∪ S2 and
the invariant-theoretic description on the covers Jj′ and on YF must be matched with the residue sequences.

Corollary 9.22. The Frölicher spectral sequence of X does not degenerate at E1 . Moreover ∂ : H 0,1 → H 1,1
is injective, so h1,1 ≥ 1.

Proof. By Proposition 9.13, h0,1 = 1, and h1,0 = 0 by Proposition 9.20. If the spectral sequence degenerated
at E1 , then b1 ( X ) = h1,0 + h0,1 = 1. But b1 ( X ) = 0: indeed X is simply connected by the van Kampen
computation of §7 (a computation with the fundamental groups of the four pieces J, N1 , N2 , N0 and of their
overlaps). This contradiction proves the first assertion.
                                                                                               0,1
    For the second, the generator of H 0,1 must die in the spectral sequence, since E∞             is a subquotient
                                                   0,1              −r,r
of H ( X; C) = 0. No differential enters Er (the groups Er
       1                                                                 vanish). The differentials leaving it are
d1 = ∂ : H 0,1 → H 1,1 ; d2 : E20,1 → E22,0 , whose target is a subquotient of H 2,0 = 0 (Proposition 9.20); and dr
for r ≥ 3, with target Err,2−r = 0 since 2 − r < 0. Hence the class must be killed by d1 , i.e. ∂ is injective on
H 0,1 , and h1,1 ≥ h0,1 = 1.

    The fixed locus in W. On YF the action is the one-parameter subgroup of TN ′ with cocharacter e2 , and an

Remark 9.25. For every integer n ≥ 2 the finite subgroup µn ⊂ C∗ = Aut0 ( X ) has the same fixed-point set
as C∗ itself,
                                                 X µn = X C = D0 ∼
                                                           ∗
                                                                 = S2 .
Indeed, off W the circle S1 ⊂ C∗ acts freely: on X ◦ by Lemma 7.21, and on the smooth fibres of N0 it acts by
the translations ςe2 (ς ∈ R ∖ Z), which have no fixed points since δ̂ is primitive in Λ. On W, an element of
order n in the one-parameter subgroup of TN ′ with cocharacter e2 fixes the orbit of a cone σ̂ ∈ F pointwise
if and only if n1 (e2 , 0) ∈ spanR (σ̂ ) + N ′ . For the cone over a vertex v this would force n1 e2 ∈ Zv + Z2 ,
which is impossible; for the cone over an edge [v, v + ρ] it holds if and only if n1 e2 ∈ Rρ + Z2 , i.e. if and
only if n | det(ρ, e2 ); and the point orbits of the triangles lie on the closures of the edge curves. Since the
edge directions of T A2 are ±e1 , ±e2 , ±(e1 − e2 ), with det(e1 , e2 ) = det(e1 − e2 , e2 ) = 1 and det(e2 , e2 ) = 0,
for every n exactly the curves over the edges of direction ±e2 are fixed, and their image in W is the double
curve D0 ∋ P, Q of Proposition 9.24. In particular X µ p is a mod-p homology 2-sphere for every prime p, as
P. A. Smith’s theorem requires of a Z/p-action on a mod-p homology 6-sphere [Bre72, Ch. III]; compare
[HKP, Prop. 2.5].
Remark 9.26. Proposition 9.24 realises the first alternative of [HKP, Prop. 2.5]: for I ∼      = C∗ ⊂ Aut( X ) with
fixed set F, either F ∼   = P or F is two disjoint points; had δ̂ ↔ e2 not been an edge direction of the
                              1

triangulation, the computation above would have produced the second alternative, the two triple points
P, Q with opposite tangent weights. Also h0 ( TX ) = 1 is compatible with [HKP, Cor. 1.3] (h0 ( TX ) ≤ 2 for
any complex structure on S6 ). The exclusion of a compact Aut0 ( X ) in Proposition 9.23 is obtained directly,
from the non-constancy of the period matrix (Lemma 9.3).

9.8   Non-Kählerness
Remark 9.27. Since b2 ( X ) = 0 (§7), X carries no Kähler metric, and no Kähler current: by the characterisation
of the Fujiki class C through the existence of a Kähler current (see [Ang18]) such a current would make X
bimeromorphic to a compact Kähler manifold, whence the ∂∂-lemma ¯        and b2 ≥ h1,1 ≥ 1. Also a( X ) = 1 < 3
shows that X is not Moishezon.

Throughout this section X denotes the compact complex threefold constructed in §§3–6, f : X → B = P1the

uses [CDP20] or [CDP98], the one invocation of [CDP20] occurring in Remark 9.9, on which nothing below
depends. We use freely:

     through two triple points, and its normalisation η : We → W is dP6 , with the opposite sides of the
     hexagon of (−1)-curves identified in pairs (§4);

     fibre F, spanned by the primitive invariant class γ (§2); here and below we keep the notation of §2:
     Λ = H1 ( F; Z) is the period lattice, with frame (γ̂, û, ŵ, δ̂), and V = H 1 ( F; Z) = Hom(Λ, Z) with
     frame (γ, u, w, δ). The monodromy-invariant line in H 1 ( F; Q) = V ⊗ Q is Qγ (Lemma 2.7), and
     the co-invariants of H1 are Λ/ ker γ ∼  = Z, detected by γ. Moreover, writing ϖ : H◦ → B◦ for the

      the columns being the images of γ̂, û, ŵ, δ̂, and 1, τ, µ are linearly independent over C as holomorphic

    By (X1)–(X2), X satisfies the hypotheses of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680] while c3 ( X ) = 2 > 0, and X is
homeomorphic to S6 with a( X ) = 1, so that the conclusion of [CDP20, Corollary 2.3, p. 680] fails for the X
constructed here (the existence of such an X being the content of this paper). The purpose of this section is
to locate, as precisely as we can, the two places in the printed arguments of [CDP20, CDP98] which do not
cover X. They are:

  (i) the reduction “it suffices to show (11)” of [CDP20, §7, p. 692], by which the vanishing H 0 ( Xc , Ω1X | Xc ⊗
      L| Xc ) = 0 — i.e. hypothesis [CDP20, (1), p. 680] of [CDP20, Proposition 2.4, p. 680] — is reduced to the
      vanishing of H 0 (S, Ωe 1 ⊗ L|S) on the reduction S of the fibre. At the fibre W of our X the conclusion
                              S
      of that reduction is unconditionally false: R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0 for every L ∈ Pic( X ) (Theorem 10.5), so
      hypothesis [CDP20, (1)] holds for no L, [CDP20, Proposition 2.4] is vacuous for X, and the conclusions
      a) and c) of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680] fail for the X constructed here. Statement [CDP20, (11)]
      itself does hold at W for every twist coming from Pic( X ); what fails at W is the implication from it
      (§10.4);

  (ii) the monodromy line: [CDP20, Lemma 4.2, p. 684], which is [CDP98, Lemma 3.2, p. 84], together
       with the last step of the proof of [CDP98, Theorem 3.1, p. 85] (quoted in, and used for, [CDP20,
       Corollary 5.2, p. 688] in the torus case). The printed argument passes through the sentence “From
       (1) we deduce that H1 (U, Z) ≃ H1 (W, Z) ⊕ H1 ( F, Z)”, which presumes that the monodromy acts


                                                           84
       trivially on H1 ( F ); in general a split extension yields only the co-invariants (Lemma 10.7), and for
       X these have rank t′ = 1 instead of t = 4. Here more than a step fails: the conclusion of [CDP98,
       Theorem 3.1] = [CDP20, Corollary 5.2, p. 688] fails for the X constructed here, since H 1 ( X, O X ) ∼        =C
       while H 1 ( F, O F ) ∼
                            = C2 , so the restriction H 1 ( X, O X ) → H 1 ( F, O F ) is not surjective; consequently the
       conclusion of [CDP20, Proposition 5.5, p. 688] (R1 f ∗ O X ∼    = O(b1 ) ⊕ O(b2 ) with b j ≥ 0) fails for the X
                                           1       ∼
       constructed here, which has R f ∗ O X = O ⊕ O(−1), and the line bundle L with L|S non-torsion on
       every fibre which [CDP20, Proposition 5.6, p. 689] supplies as input to [CDP20, §7] is not produced
       for X by the printed argument (§10.5). This last gap, however, is repairable: such an L does exist on X
       (Proposition 10.8), so that the §5 input to [CDP20, §7] can be restored for X by other means, and the
       monodromy point can be repaired for X.

    The scope of the discussion below is the following. What is located at each place is a step of a printed
proof ; where the conclusion of the statement itself does not fail for X, the statement may well survive by
other arguments, and this applies in particular to [CDP20, Proposition 5.6] under hypotheses different
from those of X, whose conclusion is in any case true for X (Proposition 10.8). Where the conclusion of a
statement does fail for the X constructed here this is recorded explicitly: so for [CDP20, Theorem 2.2 a), c)]
and [CDP20, Corollary 2.3] (through (i)); and at place (ii) it is not only the printed proofs but also the
conclusions of [CDP20, Lemma 4.2]=[CDP98, Lemma 3.2] (the count r = s − 1 + b1 ( Xc ), which for X
would give r = 6, whereas the correct count is r = 3), of [CDP20,        Corollary 5.2]=[CDP98, Theorem 3.1]
and of [CDP20, Proposition 5.5] = [CDP98, Remark 3.8(1)] which fail for the X constructed here, these
statements presupposing trivial monodromy on H1 of the fibre; as a located step, what is at issue in [CDP98,
Lemma 3.2] is the deduction (1)⇒(2), and the corrected count r = s − 1 + t′ does hold at X (§10.5). This
failure at (ii) is nevertheless repairable for the purpose of the proof of [CDP20, Corollary 2.3] at X: the input
which [CDP20, §5] is to deliver to [CDP20, §7] — a line bundle non-torsion on every fibre — exists on
X by the countability argument of Proposition 10.8, so that the monodromy point can be repaired for X.
Place (i) alone already shows that the printed proof of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2] does not apply to X, and the
reduction at W cannot be repaired; place (ii) shows in addition that the printed route by which [CDP20, §5]
delivers its input to [CDP20, §7] breaks down at X, although the input itself is available here by another
argument. Nothing below bears on the remaining cases treated in [CDP20, CDP98].

10.1    The statements under discussion
We quote [CDP20] verbatim; numbering inside quotations is that of [CDP20], and page references are to
the journal version, Compositio Math. 156 (2020), 679–696.3 For a coherent sheaf F on a reduced complex
space S we write T (F ) ⊂ F for the subsheaf of sections supported on a nowhere dense closed analytic
subset and Fe := F /T (F ); in particular Ω
                                          e 1 = Ω1 /torsion.
                                            S    S

       2.2. Theorem (p. 680). “Let X be a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold with H 1 ( X, Z) =
       H 2 ( X, Z) = 0 and algebraic dimension a( X ) = 1. Assume that the algebraic reduction
       f : X → C is holomorphic. Then a) H i ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = 0 for i ̸= 1. b) χ( X, TX ⊗ M ) ≤ 0. c)
       c3 ( X ) ≤ 0.”
       2.3. Corollary (p. 680). “Let X be a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold homeomorphic to
       S6 . Then a( X ) = 0.”

   Here C ∼ = P1 and M is a general element of Pic( X ) = H 1 ( X, O X ), the identification being the one
furnished by the exponential sequence. In the proof of [CDP20, Cor. 2.3, p. 680] the case a( X ) = 3 is
excluded at once (“Obviously, a( X ) ̸= 3, otherwise X is Moishezon and therefore b2 ( X ) ̸= 0”); a( X ) = 2,
and a( X ) = 1 with non-holomorphic algebraic reduction, are disposed of by [CDP20, Theorem 2.1, p. 679],
the latter written out in detail in [LRS, Theorem 3.1, Corollary 3.2], following [CDP98]. The remaining
  3 The journal text agrees, up to copy-editing, with arXiv:1904.11179v2; we cite the journal as the primary reference and mention

the arXiv numbering only where a statement or wording is found there alone.

case — holomorphic algebraic reduction onto a curve, the case of our X — is treated only in [CDP20,

       2.4. Proposition (p. 680). “Let X be a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold with b2 ( X ) =
       0 and algebraic dimension a( X ) = 1. Assume that the algebraic reduction f : X → C is
       holomorphic. If
                                            (1)    R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) = 0
       for some (or general) L ∈ Pic( X ), then the assertions of Theorem 2.2 hold. . . . The key is to
       show that the restriction L| Xc of some or the general line bundle L to any fiber is never torsion.
       Then we compute directly on Xc ; here the case when Xc is singular, in particular non-normal,
       needs special care.”

   The production of a line bundle L whose restriction to every fibre is non-torsion — the “key” just
quoted — is the task of [CDP20, §5], which we examine in §10.5; the verification of [CDP20, (1)] for such
an L occupies [CDP20, §7], which opens ([CDP20, p. 692]) by reducing H 2 ( Xc , TX | Xc ⊗ L| Xc ) = 0 by Serre
duality to H 0 ( Xc , Ω1X | Xc ⊗ L| Xc ) = 0, and then, with S = red Xc , continues:

       “Using the (co)tangent sheaf sequence 0 → NS∗ → Ω1X |S → Ω1S → 0 it is immediate, provided

             e 1 = Ω1 /torsion. We first treat smooth fibers S = Xc .”
               S    S

    (The twist is by L∗ in [CDP20, Prop. 2.4, p. 680] and by L in [CDP20, §7, p. 692], the twist by ωX from
Serre duality having been absorbed into L, as we do explicitly in Step 2 below; since L ranges over Pic( X )
this is immaterial.) Statement [CDP20, (11), p. 692] is then established for smooth fibres [CDP20, Prop. 7.1,
p. 692], for multiple fibres with smooth reduction [CDP20, Rem. 7.2], for singular reduced fibres [CDP20,
Prop. 7.3, pp. 693–695], for multiple fibres with singular reduction [CDP20, Rem. 7.4] and for reducible
fibres [CDP20, Prop. 7.5]. We shall see that for our X statement [CDP20, (11)] does hold at the fibre W,
for every twist coming from Pic( X ), while hypothesis [CDP20, (1)] fails at W for every L ∈ Pic( X ): the
step which does not cover X is the passage, in the sentence just quoted, from the conormal sequence to
[CDP20, (11)]. The discussion concerns the fibre W only; the multiple fibres 3S1 , 4S2 , and the propositions
of [CDP20, §7], which are statements about Ω  e 1 ⊗ L|S , lie outside its scope.

10.2    A lemma on reduced normal crossings fibres
In this subsection NS∗ denotes a conormal sheaf; it is not the nilpotent matrix N = T0 − I of §2.

Definition 10.1 (Setting). Let X be a complex manifold of dimension 3, D ⊂ C the unit disc with coordinate
t, and g : X → D proper, surjective, holomorphic. Put S := g−1 (0) and assume: (a) S is reduced, i.e. g
vanishes to order one along every irreducible component of S; (b) S has normal crossings: every p ∈ S
admits local coordinates ( x, y, z) centred at p in which g = u · x, g = u · xy or g = u · xyz, with u nowhere
vanishing. We write D ⊂ S for the singular locus with its reduced structure (locally { x = y = 0},
respectively the union of the three coordinate axes), η : Se → S for the normalisation, and D  e := η −1 (D) with
its reduced structure. Thus S is compact, Se is a smooth compact complex surface (possibly disconnected),
η is finite and an isomorphism over S ∖ D, and D    e ⊂ Se is a divisor. Put dg := g∗ (dt) ∈ H 0 (X , Ω1 ).
                                                                                                       X

    Since S is reduced, its ideal sheaf is locally generated by g, so NS∗ = OX (−S)|S ∼      = OS is freely generated
by the class of g, whose image in ΩX |S is dg|S . A direct computation in the three local models, with Ω1X |S
                                            1

free over OS on dx, dy, dz, shows that h 7→ h dg|S is injective (for g = uxy a lift h̃ of a section killing dg|S

Proof. Two properties of dg|S , read off the local models: (i) dg|S is nowhere zero on S ∖ D (for g = ux,
dg|S = u dx); (ii) dg|D = 0, since dg ∈ ( x, y) · Ω1X for g = uxy and dg ∈ (yz, zx, xy) · Ω1X for g = uxyz.
(Equivalently: dg lies in c · Ω1X |S with c the conductor ideal, which for normal crossings is the reduced
ideal of D.)
     (a) Put σ̃ := η ∗ (dg|S ) ⊗ e, σD := 0; then σ̃ |De = 0 by (ii), so (10.3) yields a unique s ∈ H 0 (S, E), non-zero
by (i) and because e is nowhere zero.
     (b) On S ∖ D, s = dg|S ⊗ e is a section of NS∗ ⊗ A by (10.1); hence ρ(s) vanishes on a dense open set,
is supported on D and is torsion. As A is invertible, T (Ω1S ⊗ A) = T (Ω1S ) ⊗ A, so the image of s in
H 0 (S, Ωe 1 ⊗ A) is zero. If ρ(s) = 0 then by (10.4) and (10.1) there is σ ∈ H 0 (S, A) with s = dg|S ⊗ σ; pulling
           S
back and using that η ∗ (dg|S ) is nowhere zero on the dense open set Se ∖ D             e gives η ∗ σ = e there, hence
everywhere, so σ trivialises A. The converse is clear.
     (c) NS∗ ∼
             = OS , so H 0 (S, NS∗ ⊗ A) = H 0 (S, A). Let σ ∈ H 0 (S, A); then η ∗ σ = φ · e with φ constant on each
connected component of the compact surface e             S. Let Z ⊂ S be the union of the irreducible components
on which σ vanishes identically. If Z ̸= ∅ and Z ̸= S, then, S being connected and a finite union of
irreducible closed subsets, some component S′ ̸⊂ Z meets Z, say at p. Since η is finite and bimeromorphic,
the components of Se correspond bijectively to those of S, the one over S′ being Se′ := η −1 (S′ ∖ D); as σ ̸≡ 0

                                                              87
on S′ , the constant φ|Se′ is non-zero, so σ is nowhere zero on S′ , contradicting σ ( p) = 0. Hence σ ≡ 0 or σ
is nowhere zero; the latter gives A ∼ = OS , excluded. Thus H 0 (S, A) = 0, and by (a) the non-zero s is not in
the image.

Remark 10.4 (the normal case). Let S ⊂ X be a reduced compact fibre of a proper map to a curve, with
S normal. If s ∈ H 0 (S, Ω1X |S ⊗ A) has torsion image ρ(s), then Ω1S is locally free on Sreg , so s|Sreg is a
section of NS∗ ⊗ A, which extends across the finite set S ∖ Sreg by the second Riemann extension theorem;
so s ∈ H 0 (S, NS∗ ⊗ A). Hence if H 0 (S, NS∗ ⊗ A) = 0 = H 0 (S, Ω
                                                                 e 1 ⊗ A) then H 0 (S, Ω1 |S ⊗ A) = 0, and the
                                                                   S                    X
reduction of [CDP20, p. 692] to [CDP20, (11)] is valid. Lemma 10.3 shows that at a non-normal S this
extension argument fails.

Corollary 10.6. For the threefold X of the Main Theorem: (i) hypothesis [CDP20, (1), p. 680] of [CDP20,
Proposition 2.4, p. 680] holds for no L ∈ Pic( X ), so [CDP20, Proposition 2.4, p. 680] is vacuous for
X; (ii) conclusion a) of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680] fails for the X constructed here for i = 2 and
general M ∈ Pic( X ); conclusion b) fails as well, for every M ∈ Pic( X ), since c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = 0 (by
H 2 ( X; Z) = H 4 ( X; Z) = 0) and c1 ( M ) = 0, so that Riemann–Roch gives χ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = 21 c3 ( X ) = 1 > 0;
and conclusion c) fails since c3 ( X ) = 2 > 0; yet X satisfies the hypotheses of that theorem, namely
H 1 ( X, Z) = H 2 ( X, Z) = 0, a( X ) = 1 and holomorphic algebraic reduction f ; (iii) X is diffeomorphic, in
particular homeomorphic, to S6 and has a( X ) = 1, so that the conclusion of [CDP20, Corollary 2.3, p. 680]
fails for the X constructed here.

Proof. (i) and the i = 2 part of (ii) are Theorem 10.5(a),(c) with (X1), (X2); for the b) part of (ii), ch( TX ⊗ M ) =
3 + 12 c3 ( X ) and Td( X ) = 1 when c1 ( X ) = c2 ( X ) = c1 ( M ) = 0, whence χ( X, TX ⊗ M) = 12 c3 ( X ) = 1 by
(X2); the c3 part of (ii) is (X2); (iii) is (X1), (X2).
