to overcome is, however, unconditional, and it is the conclusion drawn from the step which fails for the
X constructed here: by Theorem 10.5(a), R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0 for every L ∈ Pic( X ), so hypothesis [CDP20,
(1)] holds for no L whatsoever, [CDP20, Proposition 2.4] is vacuous at X, and the conclusions a) and c)
of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680] fail for the X constructed here (Corollary 10.6). In particular this place
alone already shows that the printed proof of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680], and with it that of [CDP20,
Corollary 2.3, p. 680], does not cover X, independently of anything in §10.5. No repair of the quoted
sentence can restore [CDP20, Theorem 2.2] at X; what an argument covering X would have to control
instead is described in §10.6.
    A numerical symptom of the second place may be recorded here. For X the singular fibres are W, 3S1 ,
4S2 , so s = 3 and the number of irreducible components of singular fibres is r = 3, whereas b1 ( Xc ) = 4 for
a smooth fibre; thus [CDP20, Lemma 4.2, p. 684] would give r = s − 1 + b1 ( Xc ) = 6. The discrepancy is
explained in §10.5.

10.5 Second place: the monodromy line, [CDP20, Lemma 4.2] = [CDP98, Lemma 3.2], and the
     last step of [CDP98, Theorem 3.1]
      4.2. Lemma ([CDP20, p. 684]). “Let s be the number of singular fibers and r be the numbers of
      irreducible components of the singular fibers. Then r = s − 1 + b1 ( Xc ), where Xc is a smooth
      fiber.”

    The proof refers to [CDP98, Lemma 3.2, p. 84] (Lemma 5.2 of the arXiv text), of which it is the special
case g = 0, b1 = 0; the two statements are the same lemma. The set-up of [CDP98] is: Y a compact
complex threefold with b2 = 0, h : Y → V ′ holomorphic onto a smooth curve of genus g with connected
fibres, Σ′ ⊂ V ′ a finite non-empty set with A = h−1 (Σ′ ) containing all singular fibres, B′◦ := V ′ ∖ Σ′ ,
U = h−1 ( B′◦ ), s = card Σ′ , t = b1 ( F ) for F a general fibre, D1 , . . . , Dr the components of A (in [CDP98] Σ′
is called ∆ and B′◦ is called W; we keep Σ, W, and also the letters V, Λ of (X4), for the objects of this paper):

     “(1) The natural exact sequence of groups 1 = π2 (W ) → π1 ( F ) → π1 (U ) → π1 (W ) → 1 is exact
     and (non-canonically) split. (2) b1 (U ) = b1 (W ) + b1 ( F ) = 2g + s − 1 + t ≤ b1 ( X ) + s − 1 + t. (3)
     r = s − 1 + t + 2g − b1 ( X ). . . . (2) From (1) we deduce that H1 (U, Z) ≃ H1 (W, Z) ⊕ H1 ( F, Z).”

   The last sentence is the step we locate: a split extension of groups does not give a direct sum of
abelianisations, but a direct sum with the co-invariants of the kernel.

Lemma 10.7. Let 1 → K → G → Q → 1 be a split exact sequence of groups. Then Gab ∼

     In [CDP20, Lemma 4.2, p. 684], where g = 0 and b1 = 0, the count corrected in this way reads
r = s − 1 + t′ . For our X we have t′ = 1 by (X4): the invariants of H 1 ( F; Q) = V ⊗ Q form the line Qγ
(Lemma 2.7), equivalently the co-invariants of H1 ( F; Q) = Λ ⊗ Q form the line ΛQ / ker γ ∼       = Q, detected
by γ. Hence r = 3 − 1 + 1 = 3, in agreement with the three irreducible singular fibres W, S1 , S2 , whereas
the printed count r = 6 presumes trivial monodromy; so the conclusion of [CDP20, Lemma 4.2]=[CDP98,
Lemma 3.2], and not only its printed proof, fails for the X constructed here. What is located here is
again a step of the printed proof: under hypotheses forcing t′ = t the printed conclusion of [CDP20,
Lemma 4.2]=[CDP98, Lemma 3.2] stands. That the deduction (1)⇒(2) of [CDP98, Lemma 3.2, p. 84] is not
valid in general is seen already in the projective world: let S0 = ( E × F0 )/G be a bielliptic surface [BHPV,
V.5] (called hyperelliptic in [CDP20]), with E, F0 elliptic curves and G finite acting on E by translations and
on F0 with F0 /G ∼ = P1 ; the first projection gives a holomorphic fibre bundle S0 → E/G with fibre F0 whose
monodromy factors through the cyclic group of order 2, 3, 4 or 6 by which G acts on F0 modulo translations,
an action without non-zero invariants on H1 ( F0 ; Q). With an elliptic curve E′ put Z := S0 × E′ over E/G;
its fibres have b1 = 4 and t′ = 2, g = 1, and removing s ≥ 1 fibres one finds by Künneth and Lemma 10.7
b1 (U ) = (s + 1) + 2 = s + 3, whereas 2g + s − 1 + b1 ( F ) = s + 5. (Z is projective, so it does not satisfy the
standing hypotheses of [CDP98, CDP20]; the example bears only on the group-theoretic deduction.)
The same line in [CDP98, Theorem 3.1], and its role at X. The substitution t ⇝ t′ is needed again in the last step
of the proof of [CDP98, Theorem 3.1, p. 85] (Theorem 5.1 of the arXiv text), where one reads “By 5.2(2) we
conclude that rk H 0 (W, R1 f ∗ (Z)) = t” — an appeal to the very sentence “H1 (U, Z) ≃ H1 (W, Z) ⊕ H1 ( F, Z)”
discussed above, and valid as printed only when the monodromy is trivial; in general it yields t′ . That
theorem asserts the surjectivity of H 1 (Y , O) → H 1 ( F, O F ), and it is the source of [CDP20, Corollary 5.2,
p. 688], which reads:

      5.2. Corollary (p. 688). “The restriction map H 1 ( X, O X ) → H 1 ( Xc , O Xc ) is surjective for all
      c ∈ C.”

     The hypotheses under which this is asserted are the standing hypotheses of [CDP20, §5], which
we paraphrase: X is a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold with b2 ( X ) = 0 (in the application,
H 1 ( X, Z) = H 2 ( X, Z) = 0) and a( X ) = 1 whose algebraic reduction f : X → C is holomorphic, the general
fibre Xc being a complex 2-torus or a Kodaira surface, and R1 f ∗ O X is locally free ([CDP20, Proposition 5.1]).
The proof of Corollary 5.2 reads “Suppose first that F is a Kodaira surface or a torus. Then the assertion
is Theorem 3.1 in [CDP98]; the proof works since we now know that R1 f ∗ (O X ) is locally free”. Our X
satisfies all of these hypotheses, by (X1), (X2) and (X4). The local freeness of R1 f ∗ O X demanded here does

                                                            91
hold for X — it is local freeness of R1 f ∗ O X with base change (Proposition 9.13), whose statement gives
local freeness together with a splitting; base change then follows by descending induction on q in the
cohomology-and-base-change theorem for the flat proper morphism f , all Rq f ∗ O X being locally free [BaSt,
Ch. III, §3] — although the printed proof of [CDP20, Proposition 5.1] passes through a step which does not
apply to X.5
     At X, however, more than a step fails: the conclusion itself fails for the X constructed here. By (X2),
H 1 ( X, O X ) ∼= C (Theorem 9.1), while H 1 ( F, O F ) ∼   = C2 for a smooth fibre F, a complex 2-torus by (X4);
hence the restriction
                                              H 1 ( X, O X ) −→ H 1 ( F, O F )
is not surjective, so the conclusion of [CDP98, Theorem 3.1]=[CDP20, Corollary 5.2, p. 688] fails for
the X constructed here. At X the statement therefore cannot be restored by repairing the monodromy
                                                                                                                   p,q
line, nor by any other argument. Consistently with this, for X the Leray spectral sequence E2 =
H p (P1 , Rq f ∗ Q) ⇒ H p+q ( X; Q) forces d2 : E20,1 → E22,0 = H 2 (P1 ; Q) ∼ = Q to be injective (H 1 ( X; Q) = 0) and
surjective (H 2 ( X; Q) = 0), so H 0 (P1 , R1 f ∗ Q) ∼
                                                     = Q, in agreement with t′ = 1 and not with t = 4.
     The consequences for [CDP20, §5] are immediate. The statement at issue is:

       5.5. Proposition (p. 688). “R1 f ∗ (O X ) ≃ O(b1 ) ⊕ O(b2 ) with b j ≥ 0.”

     (The splitting into line bundles is Grothendieck’s on C ∼   = P1 , R1 f ∗ O X being locally free of rank 2
under the standing hypotheses; what Proposition 5.5 adds is the positivity b j ≥ 0.) Its proof reads “We
observe that R1 f ∗ (O X ) is generically spanned by Corollary 5.2, since H 1 ( X, O X ) = H 0 (C, R1 f ∗ (O X )).
Hence b j ≥ 0”. The conclusion of [CDP20, Proposition 5.5, p. 688] fails for the X constructed here:
R1 f ∗ O X ∼
           = OP1 ⊕ OP1 (−1) by (X2) (§9), a summand of negative degree; so that conclusion (and likewise
that of [CDP98, Remark 3.8(1), p. 88], Remark 5.8(1) of the arXiv text) fails for the X constructed here, and
is not merely unproved there. Finally, [CDP20, Proposition 5.6, p. 689] uses the positivity of the summands
b j to produce a line bundle L ∈ Pic( X ) whose restriction L|S to every fibre is non-torsion — the “key”
quoted in [CDP20, Proposition 2.4, p. 680], and the input which [CDP20, §5] is to deliver to [CDP20, §7].
That argument is unconditionally unavailable at X: the hypothesis b j ≥ 0 on which it rests fails at X.
     Its conclusion, however, is true for X, by a countability argument resting on Proposition 9.13. Theo-
rem 10.5(b) shows that for each individual fibre the set of z ∈ C ∼= Pic( X ) with Lz torsion on that fibre is
countable — this is what was used in §10.4 at the single fibre W — but it does not by itself produce a
single L non-torsion on all fibres simultaneously, the fibres forming an uncountable family and a union of
uncountably many countable sets needing not be proper. The periods of f supply the missing uniformity.

properties for L = Lz .

                  2 Re ξ = c,      2 Re ζ = d,       2 Re(ξτ + ζµ) = b,         2 Re(6ξµ + ζ β) = a.               (10.6)
For λ = γ (i.e. a = 1, b = c = d = 0) the first two equations give ξ = is, ζ = it with s, t ∈ R, and the third
gives s Im τ + t Im µ = 0; since (Im τ, Im µ) ̸= (0, 0) — otherwise πû = τπŵ + µπδ̂ with τ, µ real and the
lattice would be degenerate — we get (ξ, ζ ) = iϱ (Im µ, − Im τ ) with ϱ ∈ R, and ϱ ̸= 0 because γ ̸= 0 (the
fourth equation of (10.6), the only one in which β occurs, then merely fixes the scale ϱ). As conjugation
preserves complex lines and (Im µ, − Im τ ) is a real vector, the image of a class λ with data (ξ, ζ ) lies on Pq
if and only if (ξ, ζ ) is proportional to (Im µ, − Im τ ), i.e. if and only if
                                                 ξ Im τ + ζ Im µ = 0.
Writing ξ = 2c + is, ζ = 2d + it as allowed by the first two equations of (10.6), this single complex equation
splits into
                            c Im τ + d Im µ = 0         and    s Im τ + t Im µ = 0,
while the third equation of (10.6) reads c Re τ + d Re µ − 2(s Im τ + t Im µ) = b. The two conditions are
therefore together equivalent to c Im τ + d Im µ = 0 and c Re τ + d Re µ − b = 0, that is, to the single
holomorphic equation

(Neither the coefficient a nor the period β occurs, as they must not: a and β enter only through the fourth

For λ = γ, (10.7) is satisfied identically, as it must be.)

                                            S     

that image contains {q ∈ B◦ : rk Λq = 2}. For each such q the bad set Q · Λq is countable, so these fibres
contribute only countably many bad z in all; the fibres with rk Λq = 1 contribute only z ∈ R.
     The remaining fibres, and the reductions S j . At f ∗ ( p j ) = m j S j and at W the restrictions C → H 1 ( f ∗ ( p j ), O)
and C → H 1 (W, O) are injective and C-linear by base change (Theorem 10.5(b)), so {z : Lz | f ∗ ( p j ) torsion}
and {z : Lz |W torsion} are countable. The same holds on the reductions S j , which are the fibres occurring in
§10.4 and §6. Let φ j : C = H 1 ( X, O X ) → H 1 (S j , OSj ) be the restriction, a C-linear map. Since c1 ( Lz ) = 0
in H 2 ( X; Z) = 0, also c1 ( Lz |Sj ) = 0, so by the exponential sequence on the compact complex space
S j the bundle Lz |Sj is trivial as soon as φ j (z) = 0; thus φ j = 0 would force Lz |Sj ∼                   = OSj for every z.
                                                                                             0
This is excluded by the multiplicity of the fibre: O X (S j ) ∈ Pic( X ) = Pic ( X ), say O X (S j ) = Lz j , and
O X (m j S j ) = f ∗ OP1 (1) = L12 gives z j = 12/m j , while O X (S j )|Sj has order exactly m j ≥ 2 in Pic(S j ) (§6); in
particular L12/m j |Sj = O X (S j )|Sj ̸∼
                                        = OSj . Hence φ j is injective, and since H 1 (S j ; Z) is finitely generated (S j
having the homotopy type of a finite complex) the set
                          {z ∈ C : Lz |Sj torsion} = Q · φ−      1
                                                                   im [ H 1 (S j ; Z) → H 1 (S j , OSj ) ]
                                                                                                           
                                                              j
is countable. Hence every z outside R together with a countable set gives L = Lz with L| f ∗ (c) non-torsion
for all c ∈ P1 and L|Sj non-torsion for j = 1, 2.

Remark 10.9. The proof uses only the rank-one invariance recorded in (X4), which fixes the line Pq , and the
linear independence of 1, τ, µ (Lemma 9.3).
     Two conclusions follow. First, at place (ii) it is not only the printed proofs but the conclusions of
[CDP20, Lemma 4.2]=[CDP98, Lemma 3.2] (r = s − 1 + b1 ( Xc ) would give 6, our count gives 3), of [CDP20,
                                                                                                       
Corollary 5.2]=[CDP98, Theorem 3.1] and of [CDP20, Proposition 5.5] = [CDP98, Remark 3.8(1)] which
fail for the X constructed here, all three presupposing trivial monodromy on H1 of the fibre; yet this failure
is repairable for the purpose of the proof of [CDP20, Corollary 2.3] at X, since the line bundle which [CDP20,
§5] is to deliver to [CDP20, §7] does exist on X by the countability argument of Proposition 10.8, so that
the §5 input can be restored for X even though the printed route to it breaks down. Secondly, at place (i)
the situation is different: taking the very L of Proposition 10.8 — and, if one wishes, z also outside the
countable sets of §10.4 and Theorem 10.5(c) — the proviso “provided L|S is non-torsion” of [CDP20, §7,
p. 692] is met at every fibre, and nevertheless
                                       H 0 W, Ω1X |W ⊗ ( L∗ ⊗ ωX )|W ̸= 0
                                                                     

by Theorem 10.5, so that R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ L) ̸= 0 and hypothesis [CDP20, (1)] fails for this L as for every other.
Thus the monodromy point can be repaired for X; the reduction at the non-normal fibre cannot. The
reduction at [CDP20, p. 692], not the monodromy line, is what [CDP20, Theorem 2.2] would have to do
without at X.

10.6    What an argument excluding such an X would have had to control
With hypothesis [CDP20, (1), p. 680] unavailable, the mechanism by which the assertions about χ are
obtained — [CDP98, Corollary 1.3] with Serre duality and the Leray spectral sequence — still applies.
Recall:

       “1.3 Corollary. Let X be a smooth compact threefold with b2 ( X ) = 0 carrying an effective divisor
       D. Then H 0 ( X, B ⊗ L) = 0 for generic L in Pic0 ( X ) and every vector bundle B on X.”


                                                              94
   (The statement is used, and proved, in the order “for every B, for generic L depending on B”; one
generic L serving for every B would be untenable, as B = L∗ shows.) The fibres of f are effective divisors
and Pic( X ) = Pic0 ( X ) by Step 1; hence H 0 ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = 0 for general M and, applied to B = Ω1X ⊗ ωX
with Serre duality, H 3 ( X, TX ⊗ M ) ∼
                                      = H 0 ( X, Ω1X ⊗ ωX ⊗ M∗ )∗ = 0 for general M. Thus for general M
                         χ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = −h1 ( X, TX ⊗ M ) + h2 ( X, TX ⊗ M ),
and by Theorem 10.5 and its proof h2 ( X, TX ⊗ M) ≥ dimC R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M) p > 0; so χ( X, TX ⊗ M) ≤ 0 of
                                                                          
                                                                                         0
[CDP20, Theorem 2.2 b), p. 680] would require a compensating lower bound for h1 ( X, TX ⊗ M ). For M
as in Theorem 10.5(c) all cohomology of ( TX ⊗ M )| Xc vanishes for c outside a finite set Σ1 ⊃ Σ — finite
because {c ∈  / Σ : M | Xc ∼  = O Xc } ⊂ Ξ and Ξ is finite, as shown in Step 5 of the proof of Theorem 10.5
— so by Grauert’s theorem [BaSt, Chap. III] the sheaves R j f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ), j = 0, 1, vanish over P1 ∖ Σ1 .
Hence f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) is torsion free (a local section over a connected U annihilated by a non-zero function
would be a section of the locally free sheaf TX ⊗ M on the connected open set f −1 (U ) of the connected
manifold X vanishing on a non-empty open subset, hence zero) of generic rank 0, so f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) = 0, and
R1 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) is a torsion sheaf; the Leray spectral sequence gives h1 ( X, TX ⊗ M ) = h0 P1 , R1 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) ,
                                                                                                                    

the total length of that torsion sheaf. So an argument excluding a threefold such as X — or establishing b)
and c) of [CDP20, Theorem 2.2, p. 680] for it — would have had to produce a lower bound for the torsion of
R1 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) at the degenerate fibres, compensating the torsion of R2 f ∗ ( TX ⊗ M ) at W computed above:
information of a different kind — local deformation and obstruction data at the degenerate fibres — from
the fibrewise vanishing statements of [CDP20, §7, pp. 692–696], and not obtainable from the §5 input,
which even when restored at X (Proposition 10.8) supplies only a line bundle non-torsion on every fibre
and no control whatever of torsion at the degenerate ones. For X itself the compensation does not occur:
c3 ( X ) = 2 > 0 by (X2).

A.2. The fan F and the surfaces Dv
Let N ′ = Z3 with height y3 and let T A2 be the triangulation of R2 with vertices Z2 and triangles {v, v +
e1 , v + e2 }, {v + e1 , v + e2 , v + e1 + e2 }, with edge directions ±e1 , ±e2 , ±(e1 − e2 ); F consists of 0 and the
cones over the faces of T A2 × {1}.

Lemma A.2.         1. Every cone of F is unimodular and any two meet in a common face; hence Y = YF
    is a smooth separated toric complex manifold with dense torus TN ′ , and t = χ(0,0,1) is a global
    holomorphic function with t−1 (C∗ ) = TN ′ and t−1 (0) = v∈Z2 Dv , Dv the prime divisor of the ray
                                                                  S

    through (v, 1). In the chart Uσ ∼  = C3 of a triangle σ, with coordinates z1 , z2 , z3 dual to its vertex rays,
    t = z1 z2 z3 .

   2. The star of a vertex v consists of the six triangles {v, v + a, v + b} with ( a, b) a consecutive pair of the
      cyclic list e1 , e2 , e2 − e1 , −e1 , −e2 , e1 − e2 . Under N ′ → Nv′ := N ′ /Z(v, 1), (y, y3 ) 7→ y − y3 v, these six
      neighbours map to that list, so the star fan of R≥0 (v, 1) is the hexagonal fan of dP6 and Dv ∼             = dP6 for
      every v.

   3. Dv ∩ Dv′ ̸= ∅ iff {v, v′ } is an edge of T A2 , and then Dv ∩ Dv′ ∼
                                                                        = P1 is the invariant curve of the ray
      v′ − v in the star fan of v, one of the six (−1)-curves of the hexagon of Dv . Triple intersections are
      single reduced points, one per triangle.

   4. For λ̄ ∈ Λ, φλ̄ (y, y3 ) = (y + y3 B0 λ̄, y3 ) lies in GL( N ′ ), preserves y3 and acts on height one by
      v 7→ v + B0 λ̄; as T A2 is Z2 -invariant, φλ̄ (F) = F and Φλ̄ ( Dv ) = Dv+ B0 λ̄ , and since B0 ∈ GL2 (Z) the
      action of Λ on { Dv } is simply transitive.

   5. The group actually acting on Yε is {Ψλ̄ }, with Ψλ̄ = τλ̄ ◦ Φλ̄ , where the translation part τλ̄ is
      translation, at a point p, by the t-dependent torus element (cλ̄ (t( p)), 1) ∈ TN ′ ; in particular τλ̄
      preserves every TN ′ -orbit of Y (in particular every Dv , every Dv ∩ Dv′ and every triple point); hence
      the orbit structure of {Ψλ̄ } on the components and strata of t−1 (0) coincides with that of {Φλ̄ }
      described in (4), and the conclusions of (4) may be applied verbatim to the threefold quotient
      N0 = Yε /{Ψλ̄ } and to its central fibre W = t−1 (0)/{Ψλ̄ } (see Proposition 4.6). Consequently W is
      irreducible, ν : dP6 ∼
                           = D0 ,→ t−1 (0) → W is the normalisation, the three pairs of opposite sides of the

                                                                                                                  = P1 ,
      and the six torus-fixed points of D0 fall into two translation classes, the triple points P, Q. Hence
      e(W ) = 2.

Mum72]. The character m = (0, 0, 1) ∈ M′ = ( N ′ )∗ satisfies ⟨m, (v, 1)⟩ = 1 for every primitive ray generator

order one along each Dv . In the chart Uσ of a triangle the three primitive generators form a basis of N ′
and m takes the value 1 on each of them, so t = z1 z2 z3 there. (2) Inspection plus the standard fan of dP6
[Ful93, Oda88]. (3), (4) Orbit–cone correspondence and direct computation. (5) Since τλ̄ is translation by
the torus element (cλ̄ (t( p)), 1) ∈ TN ′ , it preserves each torus orbit, so the Ψ-orbits of strata are the Φ-orbits
of (4); two star-edges {0, a}, {0, a′ } are Z2 -equivalent iff a′ = − a, and the six star-triangles form two orbits;

    Step 1 (open tube → closed tube). Let n0 = (0, 0, 1) ∈ N ′ and let λn0 : C∗ → TN ′ be the corresponding

    Step 2 (closed tube → divisor). Since ρ/2 ≤ η0 , Proposition 7.2, applied to the reduced normal-crossing
divisor t−1 (0) and to the radius ρ/2, gives a deformation retraction of {|t| ≤ ρ/2} onto t−1 (0); this is the
retraction constructed in §7.2 (polar decomposition, honeycomb subdivision, and the lifted deformation
retraction). Composing the two retractions proves (6); the statements on π1 and H∗ follow. (Alternatively,
for those two statements one may avoid Step 1 altogether: {|t| < ρ} = n {|t| ≤ ρn } for any ρn ↑ ρ with
                                                                              S

ρn ≤ η0 , each inclusion t−1 (0) ,→ {|t| ≤ ρn } is a homotopy equivalence by Proposition 7.2, and loops and
singular cycles have compact image, so the colimit gives the isomorphisms for π1 and H∗ .) Finally, the
nerve of the covering { Dv } is T A2 , but its members meet in P1 ’s rather than in contractible sets, so the
nerve theorem is not available for this covering.

Lemma A.5 (The mapping torus at p0 ). Let F = (Λ ⊗ R)/Λ, let Φ0 be induced by M0 , and let M be its
mapping torus, i.e. f −1 (∂) for a small circle ∂ around p0 . Then H∗ (M; Z) = (Z, Z3 , Z6 , Z6 , Z3 , Z), all free.

Proof. The Wang sequence of M → S1 [Hat02] gives
                         0 → coker(Λk M0 − I ) → Hk (M; Z) → ker(Λk−1 M0 − I ) → 0,
and by Lemma A.1(7) kernels and cokernels are free of ranks (1, 2, 4, 2, 1), so the sequences split and the
ranks add to (1, 3, 6, 6, 3, 1).

Remark A.6. Input to §7. Fix a radius ρ with 0 < ρ ≤ η0 (recall that Lemma 7.5 provides η0 < min(ϵ, 1)).
The closed tube N0′ := f 0−1 {|tc | ≤ ρ/2}, of radius ρ/2 ≤ η0 , retracts onto W by the retraction of §7.2
(Proposition 7.2), which is constructed Γ-equivariantly upstairs on Y≤η and descended to the quotient; and
∂N0′ ∼
     = M. We do not assert a deformation retraction of the open tube N0 onto W. For the open tube N0 the
argument used is of a different nature: the Step-1 flow of Lemma A.2(6) is available upstairs only, on Y,
since it does not commute with the Ψλ̄ and hence does not descend to the quotient. Downstairs we use
instead the π1 comparison: by Corollary 4.8 together with the simple connectivity of t−1 (0),
                             π1 ( N0 ) = Λ = π1 (W ),   H 1 ( N0 ; Z) ∼
                                                                      = H 1 (W; Z).
Further: Nj′ = f j−1 {|t j | ≤ r ′ } retracts onto S j , with ∂Nj′ the mapping torus of x 7→ A j x + v j /m j on F, whose
m j -fold fibrewise cover is F × S1 ; and f −1 ( B◦ ) retracts onto the F-bundle over a wedge of two circles given
by A1 , A2 .


B    A second proof of the invariants statement of Proposition 7.12.
Throughout this appendix we keep the notation of the local model at p0 : the proper holomorphic map
f 0 : N0 → ∆ε from the complex 3-manifold N0 , its central fibre W = f 0−1 (0) (a reduced divisor), the double
locus D = C̄1 ∪ C̄2 ∪ C̄3 ⊂ W with its two triple points P, Q, the smooth fibres F = Ft = f 0−1 (t) for 0 < |t|
small, the lattice Λ = H1 ( F; Z) and V = H 1 ( F; Z), the monodromy M0 on Λ (resp. T0 on V), and the
compact two-torus T f = Λtor ⊗ S1 ⊂ Tc acting on Yε through the torus TN ′ ; this action preserves t and
commutes with every Ψλ̄ = (cλ̄ (t), 1) · Φλ̄ (Lemma 4.3), because TN ′ is commutative and φλ̄ restricts to
the identity on T f (Lemma 4.3(i): φλ̄ is induced by the lattice map (y, y3 ) 7→ (y + y3 B0 λ̄, y3 ), which is the
identity on the sublattice y3 = 0 of cocharacters of T f ); hence T f acts on N0 = Yε /Γ over ∆ε , preserving f 0 .
We write W ◦ := W ∖ D and D◦ := D ∖ { P, Q}, and ν : F → W for the normalisation of W, where F is the


                                                           100
toric del Pezzo surface of degree 6 carrying the anticanonical hexagon C1 , . . . , C6 of (−1)-curves (in cyclic
order), ν identifying Ck with Ck+3 to give C̄k , k = 1, 2, 3 (Theorem 4.5, Proposition 4.6).
    The purpose of this appendix is to re-prove, by sheaf theory on W alone — without the retraction of N0′
onto W, without the collapse map and without the cell decomposition of §7.2 — the part of Proposition 7.12
which asserts that spq is injective with image the monodromy invariants.

B.1   Statement and strategy

base change; ( j∗ L)0 = limr H 0 ( Dr∗ , L) is the group of invariants of the local system L = j∗ Rq f 0∗ Z with fibre

    Strategy. (i) Nearby cycles: the complex ψ := ψ f0 Z N0 ∈ Dcb (W ) computes H • ( F; Z), sp is the edge map
of the hypercohomology spectral sequence
                                        E2a,b = H a (W; Hb ψ) =⇒ H a+b ( F; Z),
and the cohomology sheaves Hb ψ are read off the three toric local models t = z0 , t = z0 z1 , t = z0 z1 z2 of

all free. (iii) Rank bookkeeping against H • ( F ) = ∧• Z4 forces all differentials touching row 0 in degrees
̸= 3 to vanish (§B.5). (iv) Degree 3 is settled by a compact-support (Gysin) argument along a single T f -orbit
in the smooth locus of W (§B.6). (v) Saturation and the identification with the invariants (§B.7).
    The only inputs are: the standard facts on ψ f for a proper holomorphic f [SGA7, Exp. XIII], [Dim04,
§4.2], [KS90, §8.6]; the local models of Theorem 4.5; the Mayer–Vietoris resolution of ZW attached to

ker(∧q T0 − I ), which are those of ker(Λq M0 − I ) by transposition (Lemma A.1(7); pure linear algebra from
 T0 = I + N).

B.5   Differentials: everything touching row 0 vanishes, except possibly in total degree 3
All E2a,b are free (§B.4). We first track ranks; saturation of the images is dealt with in §B.7.

B.6   Degree 3: sp3 has rank 2
Lemma B.3 (specialisation with supports). Let K ⊂ W ◦ be compact. There are an open neighbourhood K ′
of K in W ◦ , a radius r > 0, and an open embedding Θ : K ′ × Dr ,→ N0 over Dr with Θ(·, 0) = idK′ ; if K
is T f -invariant, K ′ and Θ can be taken T f -equivariant. For t ∈ Dr∗ put Kt := Θ(K × {t}) ⊂ Ft . Then the
composition
                                                                        spq
                                    H q (W, W ∖ K) −→ H q (W ) −−→ H q ( Ft )
equals
                           H q (W, W ∖ K) ∼
                                          = H q ( Ft , Ft ∖ Kt ) −→ H q ( Ft ),
the isomorphism being Θ-transport.

Proof. Since f 0 is a submersion at every point of the compact set K ⊂ W ◦ (on W ◦ the local model is f 0 = z0 ),
there is a relatively compact open neighbourhood K ′′ of K in N0 on which f 0 is a submersion; replacing K ′′
by T f · K ′′ — still open, relatively compact, and contained in the locus where f 0 is a submersion, which is
T f -invariant because T f preserves f 0 — we may assume K ′′ is T f -invariant; choose a Riemannian metric
on K ′′ , averaged over the compact group T f (which preserves f 0 ), and let ξ 1h , ξ 2h be the horizontal lifts
(orthogonal to ker d f 0 ) of ∂Re t , ∂Im t . For t ∈ C put
                                             ξ t := Re(t) ξ 1h + Im(t) ξ 2h ,
a single vector field depending linearly on the parameter t, with d f 0 (ξ t ) ≡ t. For r > 0 small the time-one
flow Φ1t is defined on a neighbourhood K ′ ⊂ W ◦ of K for all |t| < r, and we set Θ(w, t) := Φ1t (w).
          ξ                                                                                                         ξ

Then f 0 (Θ(w, t)) = t (as f 0 (w) = 0 and ds     d

its differential at K × {0} is bijective (the identity on Tw W ◦ , and the ξ-directions onto T0 D), and Θ is
                       ′

injective (Θ(w, t) = Θ(w′ , t′ ) forces t = t′ by applying f 0 , and then w = w′ by injectivity of a time-one
flow); shrinking K ′ and r, the map Θ : K ′ × Dr ,→ N0 is therefore an open embedding over Dr . If K is
T f -invariant, take K ′ to be T f -invariant; since the metric, hence ξ t , is T f -invariant, Θ is T f -equivariant.
      Let now Ar := f 0−1 ( Dr ) and Z := Θ(K × Dr ), which is closed in Ar . The statement at the level of classes

B.7   Saturation and invariants
                             4,0                                3,1    2,2
   • q = 0: trivial. q = 4: E∞   = H 4 ( F ) (the other pieces E∞   , E∞   vanish), so sp4 is an isomorphism.
                                 0,1
   • q = 1: H 1 ( F )/ im sp1 = E∞   = H 0 (W; V ) ∼
                                                   = Z2 is free (α = 0), so im sp1 is saturated of rank 2.

   • q = 2: H 2 ( F )/F2 has graded pieces E∞  1,1
                                                   = H 1 (W; V ) ∼
                                                                 = Z (as β = 0) and E∞   0,2
                                                                                             = ker d2 | E0,2 ⊂ Z2 ,
                                                                                                            
                                                                                                             2
      both free; hence im sp2 = F2 H 2 ( F ) is saturated of rank 4.

   • q = 3: §B.6.

In each degree im spq ⊆ H q ( F ) T0 (classes extending over f 0−1 ( Dr ) are monodromy invariant), im spq is
saturated of rank bq (W ), and the ranks b• (W ) = (1, 2, 4, 2, 1) agree with
                                      rk H q ( F ) T0 = rk(∧q V ) T0 = (1, 2, 4, 2, 1)
(Lemma A.1(7)). A saturated subgroup of the (saturated) invariant subgroup, of the same rank, is the
whole of it. This proves Theorem B.1.

B.8   Scope
Remark B.4. Theorem B.1 supplies the injectivity of spq and the identification of its image with the T0 -
invariants, which is what §§7.4–7.7 use of Proposition 7.12; the identification of spq with c∗ for the collapse
map c, the generators of H2 (W ), and the normalisations sp2 (T∨B ) = γ ∧ u, sp2 (C̄k∨ ) of Proposition 7.12
are not re-proved here and continue to rest on §7.2 and Proposition 7.12. The ancillary facts used above
— the toric local models of Theorem 4.5, the hexagon combinatorics of Proposition 4.6, the flat model of
Ft = Yt /Γ with its T f -action (Proposition 4.7), and Lemma A.1 — do not involve §7.2; the ranks (1, 2, 4, 2, 1)
of H • (W; Z) are computed in §B.4 from the resolution (B.2) and agree with, but are not taken from,
Proposition 7.11.
